The Institute of Ismaili Studies
The Institute of Ismaili Studies was established in 1977 to promote scholarship and learning on Islam — both in its historical contexts and in the contemporary world — and to foster a better understanding of its relationship with other societies and faiths.

The Institute's programmes encourage a perspective that reaches beyond the theological and religious heritage of Islam to explore the relationship between religious ideas and the broader dimensions of society and culture. They therefore promote an interdisciplinary approach to the study of Islamic history and thought. Particular attention is given to questions of modernity as Muslims seek to connect their heritage to the challenges of the present day.

Within the Islamic tradition, the Institute's programmes support research in areas that have so far received relatively little scholarly attention, among them the intellectual and literary expressions of Shi'ism in general and Ismailism in particular.

In studying Islamic societies, the Institute's programmes draw on the full range and diversity of cultures in which Islam is practised today — from the Middle East, South and Central Asia, and Africa to the industrialized societies of the West — taking into account the variety of contexts that shape the ideals, beliefs, and practices of the faith.

These objectives are pursued through concrete programmes and activities organized and carried out by the Institute's various departments. The Institute also collaborates periodically with other institutions of learning in the United Kingdom and abroad on a programme-specific basis.

<!-- page 9 -->
viii
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

The Institute's academic publications fall into a number of interrelated categories:

1. Occasional papers or essays addressing broad themes of the relationship between religion and society, with special reference to Islam.
2. Monographs exploring specific aspects of Islamic faith and culture, or the contributions of individual Muslim thinkers or writers.
3. Editions or translations of significant primary or secondary texts.
4. Translations of poetic or literary texts that illustrate the rich heritage of spiritual, devotional, and symbolic expression in Muslim history.
5. Works on Ismaili history and thought, and the relationship of the Ismailis to other traditions, communities, and schools of thought in Islam.
6. Proceedings of conferences and seminars sponsored by the Institute.
7. Bibliographical works and catalogues documenting manuscripts, printed texts, and other source materials.

This book falls into category three listed above.

In facilitating these and other publications, the Institute's sole aim is to encourage original research and analysis of relevant issues. While every effort is made to ensure that publications meet a high academic standard, there will naturally be a diversity of views, ideas, and interpretations. The opinions expressed in these publications therefore belong to their authors alone.

Preface and Acknowledgements

The question of the imamate — of leadership in Islam — has historically provoked heated debate and a wide range of responses among Muslim intellectuals. One scholar who grappled seriously with this demanding subject was Ahmad b. Ibrahim al-Naysābūrī, a distinguished figure of the Fatimid period. Al-Naysābūrī came from Nīshāpūr, an eclectic and intellectually vibrant city in Khurāsān renowned above all for its erudition and its scholars' embrace of rationalistic philosophy. He produced several works, but it is the Kitab ithbāt al-imāma that holds decisive importance for the philosophical curriculum of medieval Muslim thought. In it he brings rational tools to bear on his theology, allowing us to grasp not only the significance of his own thinking but also the intellectual commitments of his age.

Starting from the premise that supreme leadership — the imamate — is the pole and foundation of religion, al-Naysābūrī develops his thesis through several approaches, foremost among them the theory of "degrees of excellence." According to this theory, God created each genus and species with a unique capacity and a distinct advantage not found in any other. Drawing on examples from the ten Aristotelian categories and on natural metaphors from the mineral, plant, and animal kingdoms, he acknowledges the disparities within each genus and species, identifies paradigmatic exemplars in each domain, and argues by parallel that the imam stands at the apex of humanity.

The Kitab ithbāt al-imāma offers an unparalleled window into the complexities of the Imam-Caliph al-Hakim's reign (386–411/996–1021), adding considerably to our understanding of that period.

<!-- page 15 -->
xiv
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

Editing and translating a manuscript presents a host of distinctive textual and interpretive challenges. I was fortunate to be mentored throughout by Professor Wilferd Madelung, a leading expert in the field, who guided me ably through the various stages of this work. The project itself was initiated by Dr Farhad Daftary, Head of the Department of Academic Research and Publications at the Institute of Ismaili Studies, who invited me as a Visiting Research Fellow and put before me a selection of manuscript titles from which to choose my research project. To both of them I convey my deepest gratitude and appreciation for setting me on such a steep and rewarding learning curve, one that has culminated in this publication.

I bear primary responsibility for this edition and translation of the Kitab ithbāt al-imāma of Ahmad b. Ibrahim al-Naysābūrī, though I should note that Professor Madelung read several drafts and offered numerous suggestions that I subsequently incorporated. Sadruddin Fattoum was invaluable for his native command of Arabic and helped unravel many complex passages as I worked through the text. Professors Hermann Landolt, Heinz Halm, and Paul E. Walker read the text and translation and offered many useful suggestions.

This work could not have been completed without the help of several other institutions and individuals. A number of these institutions invited me to deliver lectures and participate in conferences where many of my ideas took clearer shape. They include the Department of Theology and Religious Studies, University of Bristol (April 2002), the BRISMES (Edinburgh) and MESA (California) conferences in 2001, the Institute's Department of Community Relations, our Forum at the Department of Academic Research and Publications (DARP), and, more recently, the Seventeenth Colloquium on the History of Egypt and Syria in the Fatimid, Ayyubid, and Mamluk Eras (10th–15th centuries), held at the Department of the Cultures and Languages of Near East and North Africa, University of Ghent, Belgium. In this connection, I am grateful for the written comments of Professor Michael Brett of the School of Oriental and African Studies, University of London.

Many individuals and colleagues have provided personal support, references, offprints, comments, criticisms, and suggestions. Soraiya Khan Mohamed, recommended by Zinat Merali, very generously typed my handwritten Arabic manuscript, and Haitham Sharba helped

DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

the former Director Professor Azim Nanji, the library's former Heads Professor Eric Ormsby and the late Dr Duncan Haldane, the Acting Head Alnoor Merchant, and all those responsible for maintaining the remarkable work of collecting and preserving manuscripts. The editing process itself entailed locating and examining all existing variants of the manuscript and deciphering their content. Not long after beginning the project, we discovered — thanks to Delia Cortese — that an edition of the Ithbāt already existed (Mustafa Ghalib, Beirut, 1404/1984), which meant we had to determine whether a new edition was justified and warranted. Working through all ten manuscripts alongside the existing edition with care, it soon became clear that the edited text contained not only significant omissions but also certain additions that should never have been included.

I feel blessed and grateful for my three wonderful children, Ali, Baqir, and Abida, who have followed the progress of this work with patience and love. The support of my own family — especially my parents, my brother and sister, and their respective families — has been exceptional. I should not, however, overlook the invisible inspiration flowing from the Guiding Intellect that both transcends and informs the human intellect.

Arzina R. Lalani

<!-- page 18 -->
Introduction

The founding of the Fatimid caliphate in Ifrīqiya (present-day Tunisia and eastern Algeria) in 297/909 represented a moment of extraordinary significance — not only for the Ismaili Shi'is but for the broader Shi'a community as a whole. It was the first major Shi'i leadership of an Islamic state since the reign of 'Ali b. Abī Țalib. The Ismaili imam-caliphs went on to transform the nascent Fatimid dawla (state) into a flourishing empire that carved out its own place among the diverse civilizations of Islam. Their reign proved to be one of the most outstanding periods in Islamic history, remarkable not only for its political achievements but also for its literary, artistic, scientific, and economic vitality.

The persistence of Shi'i efforts began to bear fruit from around the middle of the 3rd/9th century. The Hamdanids, the Būwayhids, the Zaydī states in Yemen and the Caspian region, and the Fatimids in the Maghrib and Egypt were all Shi'i. The achievements of the Fatimid Ismailis, however, stood in a class of their own. First emerging openly on the stage of world history in the 260s/870s, the Ismailis built a network of communities stretching from the Maghrib in the west to Sind in the east, and from the mountains of Daylam on the Caspian shores to the highlands of Yemen in the south. They rose to particular prominence in the 4th/10th century, when they began to develop a remarkable and comprehensive intellectual programme in support of their cause. Their strength lay in their ability to assimilate the new sciences successfully within the Islamic tradition and to offer a coherent, all-encompassing theory of the universe and humanity's place within it.

The 4th/10th century has been called by Louis Massignon the 'Ismaili century' in the history of Islam. See his 'Mutanabbi devant le siècle ismaëlien de l'Islam', in Al Mutannabi: Recueil publié à l'occasion de son millénaire (Beirut, 1936), p. .


<!-- page 19 -->
This intellectual vision found expression in the writings of many remarkable scholars, some of whom also elaborated the central Shi'i principle of the imamate.2 The imamate was unquestionably the most central doctrine of the Fatimids; it supplied the very raison d'être for their caliphate.

Historically, the question of the imamate has prompted a wide range of responses from scholars within the Muslim community. Among the genre of works devoted to this subject is an important treatise, the Kitāb ithbāt al-imāma of Ahmad b. Ibrāhīm al-Naysābūrī, an Ismaili Shi'i scholar of the Fatimid period. Although many manuscript sources on Ismaili thought are now readily accessible, critical editions remain scarce, and translations into English scarcer still. The primary aim of this work is to introduce the ideas of this highly significant early-5th/11th-century scholar to specialists in the field as well as to the general reader.

As in earlier periods, Muslim society of the fourth and fifth centuries was pluralistic, reflecting elements of diverse traditions, cultures, and communities of interpretation, with every group searching for its own bearings and defending its own positions.3 By the time al-Naysābūrī composed his treatise on the imamate during the early decades of the 5th/11th century, the Sunni perspective of ahl al-sunna wa'l-jamā'a — literally, 'people of the practice and community', or 'people of community practice' — was firmly established. The Kharijīs, following several uprisings, had been pushed to the margins both geographically and ideologically. The Mu'tazilīs, having been quite active, still counted important representatives among Muslim thinkers and writers. They argued that the foundations of true knowledge resided in both scriptural revelation and human reason. As Madelung has observed,4 Mu'tazilī thought spread among the

<!-- page 20 -->
Imāmiyya, who were initially associated with the Banū Nawbakht family. This prompted a sharp reaction from the school of Qumm under the leadership of Ibn Babawayh (d. 381/991), who subsequently relocated to Rayy. Shaykh al-Mufīd (d. 413/1022), who headed the school of Baghdad, later wrote a critique of Ibn Babawayh's creed, taking issue with him on several points and insisting in particular that the imams had encouraged reasoned argumentation in defence of religion. He held that reason and intellect, working in conjunction with God's revelation, are capable of establishing the basic truths of religion.5

The Ismailis had accepted neither Neoplatonic philosophy nor Mu'tazilī theology, as Paul Walker notes in his study of al-Kirmānī,6 and their scholars took considerable care to distinguish their own teachings from those of the Mu'tazila and the philosophers (falāsifa). Although the Ismailis acknowledged the role of reason, their theology was more revelational than rational at its core.7 Ismaili theology categorically rejected the philosophical view of God as first cause, describing God instead as beyond reason and being. These were among the questions being broadly discussed and debated by Muslim scholars of the time.

The 5th/11th century marked a high point of literary activity among Ismaili scholars of the Fatimid period. By the time al-Naysābūrī appeared on the scene, Ismaili theology had already undergone significant evolution. Heinz Halm compares this process to the development of Christian theology three hundred years later at the time of Thomas Aquinas.8 Like other Muslim scholars, the Ismaili thinkers of the period had encountered and absorbed the Platonic, Aristotelian, and Neoplatonic traditions through Arabic translations of Greek works, well in advance of medieval Europe. The Ismaili scholars had succeeded in

<!-- page 21 -->
articulating their tradition through the philosophical vocabulary of the age without in any way compromising the essence of their Shi'i Islamic message.

**The Author and his Works**

Ahmad b. Ibrāhīm al-Naysābūrī, an important Ismaili Shi'i scholar and writer of the early 5th/11th century, flourished during the reigns of the Fatimid Imam-Caliphs al-'Azīz (365–386/975–996) and al-Hākim bi-Amr Allah (386–411/996–1021). Biographical information on al-Naysābūrī is sparse, but his talent, character, and cultural background can be gleaned from several of his works that have survived and been copied across the centuries — a fact that itself attests to his significance and popularity. His nisba 'Nīshāpūrī' (al-Naysābūrī in Arabic) suggests he came from Nīshāpūr, the centre of the Ismaili da'wa in Khurāsān, a region particularly noted for its rationalistic philosophy.9 It was here that Muhammad al-Nasafi and his colleagues elaborated the metaphysics of Shi'i theology within a framework sometimes described as Neoplatonic rationalism. Muhammad b. Ahmad al-Nasafī (d. 332/943), who had succeeded the Amīr al-Marwāzī as head of the da'wa in Khurāsān, was a major philosopher among the early Ismailis.10 He wrote the Kitāb al-mahsūl (The Book of Outcome), perhaps the first substantial work to employ philosophy in the service of Ismaili theology. This work, composed around 300/913, appears unfortunately to be lost, but its core doctrine can be recovered

<!-- page 22 -->
from later citations, most notably the Kitāb asrār al-Bāținiyya11 and al-Kirmānī's Kitāb al-riyād. It subsequently attracted a series of refutations and critical responses not only from Sunni and Zaydī authors, but also from al-Nasafi's fellow Ismaili scholars — in particular Abū Hātim al-Rāzī (d. 322/934–935), who wrote his Kitāb al-işlāh (The Book of Correction) to amend certain of his colleague's views.12 By contrast, Abū Ya'qūb al-Sijistānī (d. after 361/971) wrote his Kitāb al-nusra (The Book of Support) to defend al-Nasafi's position, and Hamīd al-Dīn al-Kirmānī (d. after 411/1020) brought them all into harmony in his Kitāb al-riyād (The Book of Meadows).13

Al-Naysābūrī's work Kitāb istitār al-imām wa tafarruq al-du'āt fi'l-jazā'ir li-țalabi-hi (Book on the Imam's Concealment and the Dispersal of Dā'īs in Search of him to Different 'Islands')14 is a significant historical treatise that discloses the names of the three hidden Ismaili imams who followed Muhammad b. Ismā'īl. The Russian scholar Wladimir Ivanow (1886–1970) rightly observed that 'in the Istitār we have precious crumbs of information about a period which is a lost world in history, information which cannot be found anywhere

<!-- page 23 -->
else ...'15 The work recounts important details concerning the 'hidden imams' 'Abd Allah al-Akbar and his successors down to 'Abd Allah al-Mahdī, the founder of the Fatimid dynasty. The narrative describes the hardships suffered by 'Abd Allah al-Akbar and the efforts of certain dā'īs who had lost contact with him to seek him out. The Istitār also provides valuable information about the early Ismaili da'wa and the setbacks encountered by 'Abd Allah al-Mahdī on his journey from Salamiyya to the Maghrib. Al-Naysābūrī is especially well versed in the da'wa during the dawr al-satr, the 'period of concealment' in early Ismaili history.

Al-Naysābūrī's other work, the Risāla al-mūjaza al-kāfiya fī adab al-du'āt (Brief and Concise Epistle on the Etiquette of the Summoners), belongs to a genre of literature known as adab (appropriate conduct). In it he offers an excellent account of the qualities and duties of an ideal dā'ī. In al-Naysābūrī's view, the dā'ī possesses high educational qualifications, exceptional moral and intellectual attributes, and strong organisational abilities. Grounded in sufficient knowledge of both the zāhir and bātin — the sharī'a and its esoteric interpretation (ta'wīl) — the dā'ī was typically trained in jurisprudence and well versed in the Qur'an and hadīth, while also being knowledgeable in philosophy, history, and religions other than Islam.16 Ivanow's pioneering article on the organisation of the Fatimid da'wa drew directly on this treatise.17

<!-- page 24 -->
It is clear that al-Naysābūrī's works and ideas exerted considerable influence on many of his contemporaries, but most notably on the chief dā'ī, al-Mu'ayyad fi'l-Dīn al-Shīrāzī (d. 470/1077), who, besides being a scholar and poet, became a prominent statesman. Verena Klemm argues that al-Mu'ayyad actually lived by the ideals of the dā'ī that al-Naysābūrī had articulated in his Risāla al-mūjaza.18 Al-Mu'ayyad not only looked to al-Naysābūrī as his benchmark in ideals and morals, but drew on him with confidence as a point of reference in his own works.19 Al-Mu'ayyad in turn influenced the celebrated philosopher-poet Nāșir-i Khusraw, a central figure in the Central Asian Ismaili tradition.20

A work frequently ascribed to al-Naysābūrī is the Risāla al-zāhira fī ma'rifat al-dār al-ākhīra (The Resplendent Treatise on the Recognition of the Abode of the Hereafter), an eschatological treatise; however, Ivanow's attribution has since been questioned by Poonawala.21 Yet another work by al-Naysābūrī is the Kitāb al-tawhīd (The Book of Unity),22 which he himself mentions in the Kitāb ithbāt al-imāma, the subject of the present study.

The Kitāb ithbāt al-imāma of al-Naysābūrī, considered alongside comparable works, is an extremely important expression of the Fatimid vision

<!-- page 25 -->
of the imamate. Those comparable works include the Risāla fi'l-imāma of Abu'l-Fawāris,23 a contemporary of al-Naysābūrī discussed below, and al-Masābih fī ithbāt al-imāma of al-Kirmānī, yet another contemporary of al-Naysābūrī.24 There is also an earlier work in this category, the Tathbīt al-imāma, written by the Fatimid Imam-Caliph al-Mansūr (334–341/946–953).25 Cast in the form of questions and answers, it sets out the rational necessity of the imam. For al-Mansūr, the imamate was fard akbar — the greatest religious obligation incumbent on humanity, without which it can neither function nor act righteously.26 It is this tradition of rational argument that al-Naysābūrī follows, and his work is therefore especially noteworthy within this particular genre. What distinguishes al-Naysābūrī is his deployment of Aristotelian logic in relation to 'degrees of excellence' as a virtue. He thus emerges as a formidable scholar of Ismaili theology whose significance and contribution have, until now, been largely overlooked.

Traditionally, the imamate was discussed in works dealing with the principles of the faith (usūl al-dīn) rather than in legal treatises (furū'), but later Sunni scholars, following al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī, generally treated the imamate within expositions of the law.27 This was largely a response to the Shi'is, who had long placed the imamate at the very heart of religion. Al-Naysābūrī's treatise and those noted above are therefore essential reading, as they present a Shi'i and specifically Ismaili understanding of the imamate. A particularly significant aspect of al-Naysābūrī's work is his intellectual demonstration of the imamate as a rational necessity — a contribution that was both timely and crucial in a milieu where


<!-- page 26 -->
INTRODUCTION
9
was an intense debate over whether the imamate was obligatory solely on the grounds of revelation, or whether reason and intellect also made it necessary.

The Contents of Kitab ithbāt al-imāma

The Kitab ithbāt al-imāma is divided into seven sections (fuşūl) of varying lengths. The first section is an opening paragraph of prayer, salutations, and introduction in which the author explains how his work differs from those that came before:

Therefore, we wish to write a treatise on affirming the imamate using evidence (istishhād) and deduction (istidlāl) from the [outer] horizons (al-āfāq) and [our] inner selves (anfūs), from politics or governance (siyāsa) and norms (rusum) by way of intellect ('aql), self-evidence (darūra), innate constitution (al-fitra), natural disposition (al-jibla), and agreement (al-ittifaq) from all religious communities (ahl kull milla), as well as from philosophers (al-falāsifa) and mathematicians (ahl al-ķisāb). We shall further deduce evidence from the structure of religious duties (fara'id) and practices in a way that has not been done by the elders of the da'wa.28

Al-Naysābūrī's aim is unmistakable. He wants to establish the authoritative leadership of the true imams not only through citations from the Qur'an and the Prophet's traditions, as his predecessors had done, but also through rational and philosophical argumentation — induction, deduction, evidence from mathematicians, and consensus across all communities. Where necessary, he supports his arguments with Qur'anic verses and traditions from the Prophet and the imams. In his introduction, he defines the imamate as the pole (qutb) and foundation of religion (asās al-din). He is careful to avoid giving the impression that the proof of messengership takes precedence over that of the imamate, since, as he argues, the latter is already contained within the former.

Al-Naysābūrī further maintains that acknowledging the imam entails acknowledging the messenger, even though not everyone who accepts the

28. Paragraph [5] of the text and translation.

<!-- page 27 -->
10
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
messengers also believes in the imam. In his view, proofs for recognizing the messenger are addressed to those outside the sphere of Islam, while arguments for the imamate are directed at Muslims. It is therefore clear that his work was intended for a broad audience — the Muslim community (umma) as a whole — rather than his own community alone, or even the Shi'a in general.

The imam, al-Naysābūrī states categorically, is always present in the world; the world is never without him, whereas the messenger appears at certain times and not others. The imamate thus remains perpetually present in the world, across every age and era. It is the imam who preserves the religious law and its esoteric truths — the revealed law (shari'a) and its inner dimension (haqīqa). The evolution of religious cycles therefore depends on him, and it falls to him alone to interpret and act upon the prophet's religious law; only the imam has the authority to make changes or substitutions.29 It is thus only through the imam that one can reach the inner truth of religious law, its esoteric interpretation, and its deeper meaning.

Following the introductory section, al-Naysābūrī presents six further sections. The second argues the necessity of the imamate on innate, intellectual, natural, self-evident, and normative grounds. He emphasizes that people argue and disagree about the imamate in the same way as they dispute God's existence even after having accepted Him.

The third section describes the disparity and degrees of excellence found across all species, which al-Naysābūrī considers the strongest indication and proof for the imamate and the imams. Just as a physical body requires the various elements from which it is formed, so too the soul, spirit, and religious noetic forms depend on the imam for their nourishment. In this, he sees a testimony of the symbol (mathal) for what it symbolizes (mamthul). His argument is that if people were to strive for the needs of the soul as earnestly as they do for the needs of the body, they would not remain in doubt, and would neither drift away nor attribute human qualities to God.

In the fourth section, al-Naysābūrī offers various examples of degrees of excellence — most notably the sun's superiority among the

29. Paragraph [4] of the text and translation.

<!-- page 28 -->
INTRODUCTION
11
planets30 — relating it to the imam's position within religion. The fifth section draws further analogies of degrees of excellence from minerals, precious stones, and grains, pointing to a paradigm of perfection within each category to demonstrate that the imam stands at the apex of humanity. Likewise, in the sixth section, al-Naysābūrī presents examples from the plant and animal kingdoms of beings that have reached the heights of refinement. He argues that just as humans hold dominion over all minerals, plants, and animals, so too the imam — as the quintessence of mankind — has supremacy over all humanity. Al-Naysābūrī then shows why humanity needs a teacher in order to learn, and proceeds to deduce evidence for the necessity of the imam from arithmetic and geometry, as well as from the structure of religious duties (fara'id) and practices. These include the significance of the Ka'ba, the imam's role as protector of the Muslim treasury (bayt al-māl), and the meaning of jihād, pilgrimage, and the imam's responsibility for purifying believers, executing legal punishments, and upholding the duty of 'ordering good and forbidding evil'.

The final, seventh section of the Ithbāt seeks to establish the imamate from the time of Adam, and shows how Adam's designation of his son to the imamate proves that the world could never exist without an imam. Drawing on the prophets Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, al-Naysābūrī asks pointedly: 'If people accepted the imamate in previous cycles, how can they [now] deny designation (nașs) and call for choice (ikhtiyār) in the family of Muhammad?' He then argues at length for 'Ali's succession, and in establishing 'Alī's superiority he exposes the weaknesses of the caliphs who preceded him. At the same time, he enumerates the virtues of the guardian of the time (walī al-'asr) and the Commander of the Faithful — that is, the Imam-Caliph al-Hakim — without naming him directly. He establishes that just as every genus and species has its summit, the imams too have their ultimate limit, one that is unattainable by others, and that this position belongs to 'Alī as the Commander of the Faithful.

30. In Aristotelian natural science, the sun occupies the fourth and middle position among the celestial bodies: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon.

DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

Themes in the Text

A notable feature of the Ithbāt al-imāma is that while al-Naysābūrī refers to the dignitaries of the Ismaili da'wa, he mentions none of them by name. The Ismaili dā'īs Hamīd al-Din al-Kirmānī and Abu'l-Fawāris were both contemporaries of al-Naysābūrī, yet none of them cite one another in their works. All three, however, wrote on the imamate at one time or another, even if from different angles. Abu'l-Fawāris was a dā'ī in Syria, while al-Kirmānī and al-Naysābūrī had both come from Iran before moving to Cairo. It is quite plausible that al-Naysābūrī spent time in Syria before heading to Cairo, since in his Istitār al-imām he shows detailed knowledge of the inner workings of the da'wa in Salamiyya during the dawr al-satr, and is the only writer with access to the names of the hidden imams.31 He was certainly in Cairo by the time of al-'Aziz and lived well into the reign of al-Hakim. It is therefore quite likely that he met and knew several dignitaries in the Fatimid hierarchy.

The concept of the qā'im and that of the qiyāma is a recurring theme in Fatimid literature. In early Ismaili cosmogony and cosmology, a series of seven cycles or heptads of imams convey knowledge of the Unity of God (tawhid) to mankind in seven stages: those of Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus, Muhammad, and finally the qā'im, or 'One who arises'. Each 'proclaimer' (nāțiq) advances this knowledge a stage further and is aided in that task by a legatee, also known as the 'silent one' (şāmit). The pairs in the first six eras were Adam–Seth, Noah–Shem, Abraham–Ismā'īl, Moses–Aaron, Jesus–Simon Peter, and Muhammad–'Alī b. Abī Țālib. The final cycle, that of the qā'im, is when spiritual knowledge becomes fully realized as humanity reaches its perfection through God's divine word. Early Ismailis accorded the position of the qā'im as the seventh imam of the era of Islam to Muhammad b. Ismā'īl.32

31. The Fatimids gave their authorization to this presentation of their own early history. See Heinz Halm, Das Reich des Mahdi Der Aufstieg der Fatimiden (Munich, 1991), p. 22; English trans. M. Donner, The Empire of the Mahdi: The Rise of the Fatimids (Leiden, 1996), p. 13.
32. Wilferd Madelung, 'The Fatimids and the Qarmatis of Bahrayn', in Farhad

INTRODUCTION
13
In early Ismaili literature, the terms mahdī and qā'im are both used for the expected messianic imam. Some predictions came to be fulfilled by the Fatimid Imam-Caliph al-Mahdi, the founder of the dynasty, while others were to be realized by his successors. This theory was elaborated by al-Qādī al-Nu'man (d. 363/974) in his Sharh al-akhbār, where numerous traditions on the mahdī are quoted from various sources and applied to the Fatimid al-Mahdi and his successors.33 Accordingly, the title 'al-Mahdi' was reserved for the first Fatimid caliph, while the eschatological imam still awaited in the future was called the qā'im.34

For al-Naysābūrī, his ruling Commander of the Faithful — al-Hakim — is 'the qā'im in his time and age and the qā'im of all those who preceded him, and the perfector of them all'.35 He notes that the imams must always remain present in the world, while the messengers arise at certain times and not others. All messengers are imams first — that is, they initially attain the rank of the imamate — and only then rise as the seventh imam of their cycle when necessary to establish a religious law. Al-Kirmānī, writing his Rāhat al-'aql36 after al-Naysābūrī's Ithbāt, likewise regards the imam as the qā'im of his age.

Al-Naysābūrī was evidently a high-ranking official in the da'wa, as is clear both from the range of works he produced and from his description in the Risāla al-mūjaza of the professional etiquette expected of those serving the imam. In that work, as well as in the Ithbāt, he enumerates the hierarchical ranks of the da'wa as 'proofs' (hujaj), 'adjuncts' (lawāhiq), 'summoner' (dā'ī), 'wing' (janāh), and 'licensee'

34. Ivanow, Ismaili Tradition, pp. 97–122, Arabic texts, pp. 1–31. See also the discussion in Farhad Daftary, The Ismācīlīs: Their History and Doctrines (2nd ed., Cambridge, 2007), pp. 96–97 and pp. 116–119, as well as his analysis of the Fatimid doctrine of the qa'im, pp. 164–165, 207–208.

(ma'dhun) and 'motivator' (mukallib), with the imam as the supreme embodiment of the da'wa. He is the apex of humanity, a status and rank that no one else can attain in his age. In al-Naysābūrī's hierarchy there are accordingly six grades, with the imam forming the seventh. During al-Naysābūrī's time, the supreme embodiment of the da'wa was the Imam-Caliph al-Hakim bi-Amr Allāh.

Al-Naysābūrī also mentions the philosophers (al-falāsifa) without identifying any of them by name. He draws on Aristotelian logic — including the ten categories — throughout many of his arguments concerning degrees of excellence across all genera and species.37 When listing these categories, he praises the wisest among the philosophers, yet insists that the true sages are in reality the imams, from whom people have derived the foundational principles of all disciplines of knowledge and wisdom.38 It is less clear, however, whom he has in mind when he invokes the 'philosophers' critically. He was most likely referring to al-Fārābī (d. 339/950) or al-Kindī (d. 256/870). Ibn Sīnā (d. 428/1037) was certainly a contemporary. Could al-Naysābūrī have known him? Both came from Khurāsān, though from different cities. Ibn Sīnā famously acknowledged that he did not follow the Ismaili views held by his father and brother; he turned away from them and aligned himself with the Peripatetic tradition represented by al-Fārābī. He did subscribe to Aristotelian views, but not in those respects that Ismailis found most congenial to their thought. The rational metaphysics of Ibn Sīnā posed a serious challenge to Ismaili prophetic theology.39 It is therefore quite possible that al-Naysābūrī was responding to both Ibn Sīnā and al-Fārābī, even while deploying Aristotelian logic to substantiate his own theology. Like his predecessors, al-Naysābūrī did not reject philosophy wholesale, but only those elements of it that conflicted with his own conception

37. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274) applied these Aristotelian arguments in Christian theology, one of them being that things exhibit degrees of value or perfection. Long before him, however, al-Naysābūrī had used these same philosophical arguments in Islamic theology to prove the necessity of the imamate.

of accurate knowledge.40 Al-Kirmānī too, as is well known, argued against the philosophers across many of his works, just as he did against the Zaydīs, many of whom had adopted the Mu'tazilī doctrine. His critique targeted those who held that human intellect could determine the fundamental truths of knowledge independently, without being taught, and that knowing the oneness of God required no prophet.41 By extension, for Shi'is, this also implied the need for an imam — which is precisely what both al-Kirmānī and al-Naysābūrī appear to be arguing for in their respective treatises.

In his extended section on governance, al-Naysābūrī sets out the qualifications that the philosophers specify for a political leader. He divides authority (siyāsa) into three categories: the authority of the individual (siyāsat al-khāșsa), the authority of the family (siyāsat al-āmma), and the authority of the general public (siyāsat al-'amma).42 In governing himself, the individual must restrain himself from all vices, bad traits, bestial habits, and blameworthy desires, exercising firm command over his higher self against his lower self. He disciplines his soul when it errs through reproach and repentance, and rewards it when it does well through praise, contentment, and steadiness in right action. The governance of one's family means managing one's household and dependents by protecting their welfare, encouraging them to cultivate virtues and praiseworthy character, correcting those who err through reprimand and similar means, and rewarding those who do good through commendation and like methods.43 The governance of the general public is that of governing

cities, protecting the welfare of people's livelihoods and steering them away from reprehensible morals and bad deeds.

Al-Naysābūrī then agrees with the philosophers that anyone incapable of governing himself should not be entrusted with managing his family, and anyone who cannot manage his family has no business managing communities or peoples in any capacity. At this point he singles out a particular scholar without naming him, noting that "the author of this classification aimed at encouraging obedience to the imam, for he describes the imam by an attribute of a ruler so that people would seek a ruler possessing that qualification and description."44 Al-Naysābūrī is in all probability referring to al-Fārābī, for whom "the meaning of the philosopher, first chief, king, legislator and imam are all one." In his Tahșīl al-sa'āda, al-Fārābī concludes that the intellectual virtues acquired by the philosopher must be actualized into the conditions and circumstances that give rise to a state.45

This connects to al-Naysābūrī's further critique of the philosophers regarding the maxim anna al-falsafa hiya al-tashabbuh bi'l-bāri' — "that philosophy is seeking to resemble the Creator" — to the extent that human capacity allows. In his view, seeking to resemble the Creator means treating humanity as God treats them, and engaging with both others and oneself in accordance with the way God acts toward human beings and in keeping with their nature.46 This maxim appears in varying forms and is cited by al-Kindī, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, al-Fārābī, Isaac Israeli, the Ikhwān al-Șafā', and Maimonides. Commentators on Aristotle have also included

this definition of philosophy, ascribing it to Plato.47 In al-Naysābūrī's view, the philosophers have stolen this formulation from the "people of the truth" (ahl al-haqīqa),48 since in his estimation only the Commander of the Faithful (al-Hakim) has been able to realize the phrase's true meaning. The imam-caliph's treatment of humanity mirrors God's treatment of them with respect to the nature of the world — in providing sustenance and in all related matters. Al-Naysābūrī follows this by describing and illustrating the circumstances relevant to the era of al-Hakim.

Degrees of Excellence

In his Ithbāt, al-Naysābūrī affirms the imamate through several approaches, drawing on religious duties, accounts of the prophets, and arguments from philosophers and mathematicians. The theory of "degrees of excellence," however, is the most prominent feature of his text and, in his view, the most compelling proof of the imamate. He argues that imamate — supreme leadership — pervades the entire universe and is also present within our inner selves, at the very core of our being. Defining the imamate as the pole and foundation of religion, the author employs a range of methods to advance his thesis, from the philosophers' ten categories to natural phenomena drawn from the mineral, plant, and animal kingdoms as analogies.

Accepting that differences and disparities exist within every genus and species, al-Naysābūrī identifies paradigms of perfection in each domain, and thereby demonstrates by analogy that it is the imam who stands at the apex of humanity.

Abu'l-Fawāris, a contemporary of al-Naysābūrī, invokes this same theme of degrees of excellence — framed in terms of disparity within humanity — in his Risāla fi'l-imāma, touching on it briefly in one of his chapters:

The judgement of reason assures and testifies that the presence of disparity among mankind is devised wisdom. Had we been equal

47. Plato, Theaetetus, 176 B, cited in Berman, 'The Political Interpretation', pp. 53–61. Berman also gives references to many Muslim philosophers including al-Kindī, al-Fārābī and the Ikhwān al-Șafā'.
48. The author is referring to his own community.

<!-- page 35 -->
18
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
in qualities, conditions and knowledge, we would all have been on a par, and no order could have been achieved. Wisdom would have been corrupted; we would not have obeyed one another, and each of us would have claimed to be perfect in every science and craft, capable of handling all matters whether great or small, noble or base. Disparity among us is therefore to our advantage. In accordance with this divine wisdom, God (glory be to Him) made the superior among us a mediator between Him and us, so that He might guide us.49

In addressing the question of the imamate, Abu'l-Fawāris typically opens with Qur'anic verses, follows them with Prophetic traditions to support his thesis, and then reinforces these with rational arguments. The passage above responds to a question posed in his fifth chapter: "If God accords the imamate to someone in preference to another, then where is God's justice?" Al-Naysābūrī, by contrast, generally leads with intellectual arguments and then draws on Qur'anic verses intertextually to substantiate points he has established through reason.

This same premise of tafāwut, or disparity, also appears in al-Sijistānī's Ithbāt al-nubuwwa, particularly in his opening section, where he stresses that differential disparity is essential both to creation and to the maintenance of the world. All things, he maintains, are structured on the basis that some are inherently superior to others, and it is this hierarchy that determines each thing's place in the world. He also notes briefly that the motions of the celestial spheres cause the elements to combine in various ways, thereby producing both species and individuals marked by differences.50 His work makes frequent use of the terms fadl and tafādul, and his notion of differential disparity clearly depicts a hierarchy whose summit is the messenger-prophet.

INTRODUCTION
19
Al-Naysābūrī is clearly inspired by al-Sijistānī's approach, which he adapts for his own treatise on the imamate. The question of who has the right to lead the Muslim community does not, however, appear to interest al-Sijistānī, and his discussion of prophecy is celestial and theoretical rather than pragmatic and political — which is precisely what al-Naysābūrī seeks to achieve in his own work. The imamate as an institution has no place in al-Sijistānī,51 whereas for al-Naysābūrī it lies at the very heart of creation and constitutes an inner dimension of prophecy.

Although al-Naysābūrī employs the premise of tafāwut, he primarily pursues and builds on the theme of "degrees of excellence" under the term tafādul, around which he systematically constructs his entire argument, making it the foundation of his work. He extends this concept to all genera of minerals, plants, and animals, building his case on several premises, beginning with the ten metaphysical categories identified by the sages among the philosophers.52 He draws a parallel between the way substance (jawhar) stands at the summit of all nine other accidents — quantity (kammiyya), quality (kayfiyya), relation (idāfa), place (makān), time (zamān), possession (jida), posture (nușba), action (fā'il), and affection (maf'ūl)53 — through which those accidents are established, and the way the imam stands as the jawhar, or substance, in relation to all other ranks in the hierarchy of the da'wa.

In elaborating his hypothesis, al-Naysābūrī turns to the four basic constituents: heat, cold, dryness, and humidity. Among these, he observes that heat takes precedence, since it is through heat that all the others are sustained. In the same way, the imam takes precedence over every rank in the hierarchy, for it is he who illumines souls, bestowing upon them intellectual light, motion, and sublimity. He states:

52. Aristotle maintained that these categories were applicable to all phenomena. Everything has a natural function and therefore strives to fulfil that function, which constitutes its best natural state.

In recasting the Aristotelian categories, al-Naysābūrī uses the form fā'il/maf'ūl rather than the more common fi'l/infi'āl. I would like to thank Professor Hermann Landolt for pointing this out to me.

We also say that among the four basic constituents (al-'anāșir) from which the first composites (tarākib) were composed, two are active and two are passive. The two active ones are heat and cold, while the two passive ones are dryness and humidity. The most exalted and noblest among them is heat, from which arise luminosity, light, motion, movement, and elevation, while the other three are sustained through it. Heat thus holds precedence in origin and in existence. No foundation or realm of nature would be complete without it. Likewise, the imam illumines souls,54 and from him come intellectual light (al-nūr al-'aqlī), motion (al-haraka), and elevation (al-'ulūw). No principle or religious realm is complete without him.55

In discussing the celestial spheres, al-Naysābūrī cites the outermost sphere (falak al-aflāk), within which all other spheres orbit, as the supreme one, and draws a correspondence between it and the imam in relation to the other dignitaries of the da'wa:

All heavenly bodies and religious signs of the zodiac function under his command. If someone were to claim that this is rather a symbol for the qā'im (resurrector), given that the highest principle in the world points to the intellect with respect to the four principles, we would answer that the imam is the qā'im in his time, the proclaimer (nāțiq) of his age, and the one who assumes the position of the qā'im. The imam's position within his realm is that of the Universal Intellect ('aql al-kullī).56

This concept is developed further when al-Naysābūrī discusses the disparities (tafāwut) and degrees of excellence (tafādul) in the human body. He argues that the foremost organ is the head, since it is in the head that the face resides — the feature by which a person is recognized. He then turns to the internal organs, asserting that the brain is the most excellent among them, as it is the seat of the intellect. By the same reasoning, he argues for the position of the imam in the world: just as all the body's functions are governed by the brain, so all the affairs of creation revolve around the imam. And just as the intellect is the most pre-eminent faculty

54. Literally, "the imam is the light of the souls."
55. Refer to paragraph [16] in the text and translation.

<!-- page 38 -->
INTRODUCTION
21
in the human being, so too the imam is the universal intellect ('aql al-kullī), the source of all human intellect in whom everyone is united.57

On the differences and degrees of excellence among the planets, al-Naysābūrī singles out the sun as the summit: without its light human eyes cannot function, and without it no growth occurs in plants, animals, or newborns. By the same analogy, the imam is the sun of religion (shams al-dīn), through whom human insight is enlightened, souls are illumined, and hearts shine with blessedness.58 Using gold as a metaphor, al-Naysābūrī presents the imam as the most excellent substance in both religion and intellect, just as gold holds supremacy among minerals. He then turns to precious stones, arguing that sapphires stand apart from the rest, and that among a range of multi-coloured sapphires it is the red ruby that is most exceptional, since it retains its essential nature even when cast into fire or water. The ruby is therefore the supreme jewel among all precious stones, just as the imam is supreme among all creation.

Al-Naysābūrī draws further examples from plants and trees. He begins with wheat, which he considers the most nutritionally balanced grain. Among all fruit-bearing trees, he regards the date palm as the most beneficial to humankind, whether its fruit is eaten raw or cooked. He also notes that certain plants reach such a degree of refinement that they serve as antidotes for those dying from chronic illnesses. These examples are marshalled to demonstrate the degree of perfection present in each natural domain, with parallel instances of excellence drawn among human beings. Al-Naysābūrī thus concludes:

Man is the pinnacle of animals, plants, minerals and all other realms of nature. Through him, the welfare and affairs of animals and everything that came before were perfected and set in order. Likewise, the imam is the pinnacle and summit of human beings — their quintessence and their perfection.59

DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
A number of considerations may have moved al-Naysābūrī to write on the imamate. He does not mention al-Hakim by name in his treatise but refers to him as the amīr al-mu'minīn, the Commander of the Faithful, and feels compelled to comment on "the time in which we live."60 He was certainly not the only one troubled by the circumstances of the day: two other Ismaili authors, al-Kirmānī and Abu'l-Fawāris, were also writing on the subject of the imamate. It should be noted, however, that Abu'l-Fawāris makes no mention of the difficulties in the capital in his Risāla fi'l-imāma; his concern is simply to argue why the imamate is a necessity, presenting reasons why the Qur'an, the traditions, and the sharī'a cannot substitute for an imam who must be installed by naşș. He was an important dā'ī in Syria and mentions al-Hakim by name in a work that has sixteen chapters, corresponding to his imam being the sixteenth in the line. He mentions no doctrines associated with the Druzes, so it can safely be assumed that his work was composed before the beginnings of their movement in 408/1017. Syria represented a success for al-Hakim, who extended his authority as far as Aleppo.61 While in Syria, Abu'l-Fawāris was also entrusted by Caliph al-Hakim with a mission to Jerusalem, according to Ibn al-Qalānisī.62

The internal challenge posed to al-Hakim by those among his followers who wished to ascribe divinity to him may have been another reason for undertaking this work. Al-Naysābūrī calls them the ghulāt (exaggerators), though they referred to themselves as the muwahhidūn (monotheists). They later became known in history as the Druzes, taking their name from one of their leaders, Anūshtikīn al-Darzī (lit. "the tailor").63 Their preaching and propaganda had given rise to serious unrest

in Cairo so that even the majālis al-hikma had to be shut down for
a while. However, al-Naysābūrī, like his contemporary al-Kirmānī,
rejects the ascription of divinity to al-Hakim.64

In the early part of the 5th/11th century, the Abbasid caliph al-Qādir (381-422/991-1031) grew sufficiently alarmed at the Fatimids' rising popularity to commission the 'Baghdad Manifesto', which denied their 'Alid genealogy. A fierce polemic against the Bāținiyya also appears in al-Farq bayn al-firaq by Abū Manşūr 'Abd al-Qāhir b. Țāhir al-Baghdādī (d. 429/1037), who spent time in Nīshapūr, the homeland of our author.65 The polemics of Abu'l-Qāsim al-Bustī (d. 420/1030),66 a Zaydī Mu'tazilī authority, against the Ismailis appear likewise to be among the external pressures that drove scholars such as al-Naysābūrī, al-Kirmānī and Abu'l-Fawāris to mount a defence of the imamate. Al-Naysābūrī himself puts it in these terms:

'Proof of the messengership is needed for those who are outside the domain of Islam, while affirming the imamate is necessary for Muslims who are closer to the true believers. The struggle with them through debate and argument has priority over those denying the messenger and the messengership.'67

DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

Al-Naysābūrī was thus engaging with a contemporary debate among scholars of various persuasions over whether the imamate could be defended on rational grounds, apart from its certainty as established by revelation. All Muslim scholars regard the imamate as a necessity, though many — Sunnis in particular — discount the role of reason, holding that Islamic leadership is valid only on the basis of religious law. Al-Baghdādī, for example, argues that Muslims must have an imam in order to implement ordinances, enforce legal penalties, command armies, distribute revenues and protect the domain of Islam.68 Al-Naysābūrī's entire final section is devoted to establishing the imamate of the amīr al-mu'minīn. There, beyond proving the imamate of 'Alī, he also defends the actions of the reigning imam, al-Hakim. To do this, he must first establish 'Alī's hierarchical supremacy — a task that requires him to expose the limitations of the earlier caliphs, whose excellence in Sunni sources is measured simply by the chronological order in which they assumed the caliphate.69 Al-Naysābūrī, by contrast, applies the criteria of knowledge, jihad and piety to demonstrate 'Alī's excellence in a systematic way, and this demonstration is the hallmark of his thesis of tafādul, or degrees of excellence.70 His argument is inevitably bound up with the question of succession to the Prophet: unless he can establish 'Alī's superiority, he cannot contest

68. Abū Manşūr 'Abd al-Qāhir al-Baghdādī, Kitāb uşūl al-dīn (Istanbul, 1928), p. 272, cited in Makarem, Political Doctrine, p. 56, n. 6, where Makarem has given further sources on the Sunni concept of imāma.

69. The Kitab al-'Uthmāniyya of al-Jāhiz (d. 255/869) argues largely for the superiority of Abu Bakr over 'Alī and opposes any excessive exaltation of 'Alī. For a time there were those who held that 'Alī should rank above 'Uthmān, but ultimately the 'Uthmaniyya — among the forerunners of the Ahl al-sunna wa'l-jama'a — came to accept that chronological order was the order of merit. See the discussion in W. Montgomery Watt, The Formative Period of Islamic Thought (Edinburgh, 1973), pp. 166-169. Also compare the views of the early Zaydis on the imamate of al-afdal (the best) over that of al-mafdul (the preferred), and how the Batriyya differed in this respect from the Jārūdiyya, who were largely influenced by al-Baqir's school. See A.R. Lalani, Early Shi'i Thought: The Teachings of Imam Muhammad al-Bāqir (London, 2000), pp. 46-53.

70. See details in section seven of the text and translation. The concept of fadl or excellence also occurs in the Qur'an. See Qur'an, 16: 71 and 17: 70.

<!-- page 42 -->
INTRODUCTION
25
the distinction of his descendants, among them his present imam, al-Hakim. That question, in turn, was inseparable from the deeper issue of whether the Qur'an and the traditions, in their application to the community, were to be interpreted by scholars or by the rulings of the imam.

Conditions were clearly extremely difficult in the Fatimid capital, Cairo, where al-Naysābūrī was working, especially during the latter part of al-Hakim's reign.71 Anti-Fatimid tradition has left a thoroughly distorted image of this caliph. Heinz Halm's findings are pertinent here:

The Christian chronicler, John of Antioch (Yahya al-Anțākī), offered, from a distance, a diagnosis of mental disorder. Later Sunni authors maintained that he stopped washing himself for seven years, that he lived in an underground room for three years without ever leaving it and that he worshipped the planets Mars and Saturn. All this is nonsense. Even the reproach that al-Hakim was unpredictable and erratic and that he used to countermand instructions he had just given cannot be confirmed from the sources. If we refer to the contemporary annals of Cairo, and above all to al-Hakim's edicts (sijillāt), as handed down in the sources, we acquire a very different picture.72

Al-Naysābūrī is clearly aware of the challenges facing the imam, and in the latter's defence he employs several different strategies to show that whatever measures were being taken across the empire served the common good. He draws on the analogy of a teacher who endures difficulty from students, pressing them to acquire knowledge and cultivate proper conduct even when they resist, resent the teacher and prefer idleness — knowing that once these students mature they will recognise the benefit they received and regret their earlier stubbornness.73 A second analogy the author employs is that of a physician treating a

71. Sadik A. Assaad, The Reign of al-Hakim bi Amr Allah (386/996-411/1021): A Political Study (Beirut, 1974).

72. Halm, The Fatimids, p. 35. See especially his 'Der Treuhänder Gottes: Die Edikte des Kalifen al-Hakim,' Der Islam, 63 (1986), pp. 11-72, where he unravels numerous historical reports of al-Hākim's edicts.

73. See paragraph [36] of the text and translation.

<!-- page 43 -->
26
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

patient who moves between medication, lancing wounds, cauterization and, when necessary, amputation. Al-Naysābūrī speaks of the imam as a compassionate physician of souls who acts in a comparable way when religious ills — bred by disputes, arbitrary desires and divergent doctrines — spread among the people. He characterises al-Hākim's purpose as 'enjoining good and forbidding evil' (al-amr bi'l-ma rūf wa'l-nahy 'an al-munkar), one of the imam's primary duties.

Description of the Arabic Manuscripts

As noted earlier in this introduction, ten manuscripts were available for the present edition of the Arabic text of the Kitāb ithbāt al-imāma, all obtained from the Library of The Institute of Ismaili Studies, four of which were selected for this new edition. A fifth manuscript was consulted during the proof-reading stage.

The following manuscripts were used:

1. A manuscript of 64 folios, 15 lines to a page (12 on the first page), belonging to the Fyzee collection with shelf mark MS (R) 56 C.1, copied in 1134/1722 by Malik Khanbhai Tuhafjī Bhanpūrī. This manuscript has been designated as alif and was examined only in photocopied form. In his catalogue of the Fyzee collection, Mu'izz Goriawala notes that it has been partially damaged by water and is also worm-eaten.74

2. A manuscript of 61 folios, between 11 and 17 lines to a page (7 on the first page), formerly belonging to the Ismaili Society of Bombay, with shelf mark MS 91 (Ar), copied in 1244/1828. It was completed in Zayn al-Din's class and copied by Malik 'Abd al-'Ali Mullā Shaykh-bhāi Bhānpūrī. This manuscript has been designated as bā. Adam Gacek has described it in his catalogue of Arabic manuscripts in the Library of The Institute of Ismaili Studies.75

3. A manuscript of 153 pages, 13 lines to a page with 10 lines on the first page, belonging to the Institute's Zahid 'Alī collection with a

74. Mu'izz Goriawala, A Descriptive Catalogue of the Fyzee Collection of Ismaili Manuscripts (Bombay, 1965), pp. 36-37.

75. Adam Gacek, Catalogue of Arabic Manuscripts in the Library of The Institute of Ismaili Studies (London, 1984-1985), vol. 1 p. 32.

<!-- page 44 -->
INTRODUCTION
27
shelf mark MS. 1212 (Ar I, ZA). It was completed on the night of the 16th of Shaban, 1259/1843 and copied by Mawlanā Najm al-Dīn's servant ('abd), Ismācīl b. Mullā Khan-bhāi Bihārī, at the request of Mullā Ghulām Husaynjī b. Miyān-Khān 'āmil of Andūr and under the supervision of his master, Miyan Sahib 'Abd al-Muhsīn. This manuscript has been designated with the Arabic letter tā.76

4. Totalling 134 pages (129 pages in other variants), this manuscript has 14 lines per page with 11 lines on the first page and shelf mark MS 94 (Ar). No date or copyist's name is given. It appears to be a late 13th/19th century copy and is designated here with the Arabic letter tha.77

5. A fifth manuscript, MS 1047 (ArI), which the Institute acquired from Syria after the draft of the present edition was complete, was also compared and consulted. It has an illuminated headpiece on the front page and comprises 78 folios with 12 lines per page. Neither the date of completion nor the copyist's name is given. It is worm-eaten and written in black naskhi script with some corrections and additions in the margins. This manuscript is designated as jīm.

All these manuscripts are quite recent in date yet retain archaic spellings, which have been modernised in this edition. The pious benedictions following the names of the prophets and imams — such as şalla Allāh 'alayhi wa ālihi and 'alayhi al-salām — which are abbreviated in the manuscripts have been written out in full in the Arabic edition.

Notes on the Translation

The translation follows the original text as closely as possible, and is often literal, except that benedictions have at times been omitted to maintain the flow of the prose. Certain passages of a polemical nature have also been excluded from the English translation. To render the text more clearly, punctuation, paragraph breaks, hamzas, shaddas and certain

76. Delia Cortese, Arabic Ismaili Manuscripts: The Zāhid 'Alī Collection in the Library of The Institute of Ismaili Studies (London, 2003), p. 53.

77. Gacek, Catalogue of Arabic Manuscripts, vol. 1, p. 32.

<!-- page 45 -->
28
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE

vowel marks — especially in the Qur'anic verses — have been inserted. Following the fusul of the manuscript, the work has been divided into sections accordingly. Paragraph divisions and their numbering are editorial additions to facilitate cross-referencing between the Arabic and English texts. The translations of the Qur'anic verses are my own, adapted from several translations including those of 'Abdullah Yusuf 'Alī, Arthur J. Arberry, Marmaduke Pickthall and Mir Ahmad Ali.

Certain terms in the text posed particular challenges in translation. This is especially true of the title word ithbāt, which I have rendered as 'affirming' and 'affirmation' throughout, though it could equally be translated as 'proof', 'evidence', 'certitude', 'confirming', 'establishing', 'verifying', and other related words. The other challenging term was tafādul, which admits several meanings but is here translated as 'degrees of excellence' and used in the title of this study. It could equally be rendered as 'variation in perfection, merit, superiority, precedence and priority'.

In the Name of God, the All-Compassionate, the All-Merciful,

Section One

[ ] Praise be to God, who sent messengers and prophets and appointed imams and caliphs as a blessing for His friends (awliyā'), a punishment to enemies, a benefit for religion and the world, and as an edifice ('imāra) for this present world and the world to come. I praise Him with the eulogy of one who is aware of the immense value of the bounty conveyed through them, who is grateful for it and who journeys for God's sake, hastening towards it. I bear witness that there is no deity except God, with the testimony of a worshipper who understands the requirements of this profession and strives to fulfil its obligations. I bear witness also that Muhammad is His worshipper and Messenger, preferred above all His messengers and immune from all mistakes and errors — may God bless him with innumerable and infinite blessings. May God also bless his loyal and truthful legatee ['Alī], the adept in the clarification and spiritual realities of the Qur'an, and the imams descended from the martyr,1 to whom belong praise, glory, infallibility and uprightness. May He bless likewise the master of the palace and age [al-Hakim], supported by conquest and victory. May the most excellent salutations of God, and the most bounteous blessings and purest greetings be upon him and on the heir-apparent of the Muslims (wali al-ahd al-muslimīn)2 and the vicegerent (khalifa) of the Commander

1. The reference is to al-Husayn b. 'Alī, the martyr of Karbala, from whose lineage all imams of the Shi'i Ismailis and Ithna'asharīs descend.

2. The reference is to al-Hakim's cousin, 'Abd al-Rahim b. Ilyas, who was appointed heir-apparent of the Muslims in 404/1013. A țiraz from the final years of al-Hakim's reign explicitly names him as such. See examples of these inscribed cloths made at Tinnīs in the year 410 in RCEA (Répertoire chronologique d'épigraphe arabe), 6, no. 2212, cited in Paul E. Walker, Exploring an Islamic Empire: Fatimid History and its Sources (London, 2002), pp. 104-106. There is also evidence from al-Maqrizī, vol. 2, p. 103, of coins minted in his name in 404/1013, as noted by Walker in his article, 'The Ismaili Da'wa in the Reign of the Fatimid Caliph

of the Faithful, as long as tongues speak and darkness and light continue to alternate.

And now:

[2] The imamate is the pole (qutb) and foundation of religion (aşāș al-dīn) around which all religious and worldly affairs revolve, and a benefit for this present life and the life hereafter. Through it the affairs of God's servants are governed, the land prospers and reward is accepted in the abode of return. It is through the imamate that knowledge of God's absolute unity (tawhid) and of the revealed Message is attained on the basis of proof, demonstration and evidence, as is knowledge of the religious law, its foundations, its interpretation (ta'wīl) and its clarification.

[3] We have said that the imamate is the pole and foundation of religion, and have not given precedence to the messengership over the imamate, because in affirming (ithbāt) the imamate there is also an affirmation of the messengership.3 Whoever acknowledges the imam acknowledges the messenger, but not all who acknowledge the messenger acknowledge the reality of the imam. Proof of the messengership is needed for those who stand outside the domain of Islam, while affirming the imamate is necessary for Muslims who are closer to the true believers. The struggle with them through debate and argument takes priority over the struggle against those who deny the messenger and the messengership. This is because the summons to Islam and to the messengership has already reached the polytheists and proof has been established for them, so that confronting them with the sword is more fitting. Moreover, the world can never be without an imam at any given moment, whereas the messenger is present at some times but not others. Consequently, in affirming the imamate there is also an affirmation of the messengership.

al-Hakim', p. 176. Besides appointing a walī 'ahd al-muslimīn to oversee temporal matters, al-Hākim also appointed Abū Hishām 'Abbas ibn Shu'ayb as walī 'ahd al-mu'minīn to handle religious affairs. See S. Makarem, 'Al-Hakim bi-Amrillāh's Appointment of his Successors', Al-Abhath, 23 (1970), pp. 319-324. Makarem cites the compendium of Druze Epistles 26 and 74.

3. Al-Naysābūrī is keen to emphasise that in proving the imamate he is also proving the messengership, since the latter is contained within the former.

God, the Exalted, called the imam a messenger when He said: "And remember in the Scripture, Ismāʿīl was indeed faithful in fulfilling his promise while he was a messenger, a prophet."4 Likewise, God called the messenger an imam when addressing Abraham: "Indeed, I am appointing you an imam for all humanity." This was after Abraham had completed his messengership, and God, the Exalted, said: "And (remember) when His Lord tried Abraham with His commands and he fulfilled them, He said: 'I am appointing you an imam for all humanity.'"5

[4] It is related from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, peace be upon him, that he said: "God chose Abraham [initially] as a prophet (nabī), then as a friend (khalīl), then as a messenger (rasūl), and finally said: 'I am appointing you an imam for all humanity.'"6 God called Adam a vicegerent (khalīfa),7 and the title "vicegerent" is one of the names of the imams. God called David, peace be upon him, a "vicegerent,"8 while he was one of the imams of the past cycles, and He called the proofs (ḥujaj) "prophets."9 He called Muhammad and other messengers "prophets," addressing them as prophets just as He addressed them as messengers.10 This is because the messenger first attains the rank of the imamate, and then, if the times require the establishment of a new religious law, he becomes the seventh imam of that cycle and undertakes the promulgation of that law.11 [In this way] the

See Muḥammad b. Yaʿqūb al-Kulaynī, al-Uṣūl min al-kāfī (Tehran, 1388/1968), vol. 1, p. 175. See also Lalani, Early Shiʿi Thought, p. 76 n. 75. Note the absence of ʿabd in the text above. 11. Each cycle is headed by one who conveys to the people what they are able to comprehend. They are called "proclaimers" (nuṭaqāʾ, pl. of nāṭiq) of the divine message, and each also has a "silent one" (ṣāmit) responsible for interpreting the inner meaning of the message. Thus, the nāṭiq's function is to proclaim the revelation plainly (tanzīl), while the imam elucidates its esoteric interpretation (taʾwīl), guiding the believers according to the needs of the time. See the Introduction above for details and for the significance of the seventh imam.

<!-- page 51 -->
imam occupies the place of the messenger in his own age and time. Before arising to promulgate the religious law, the messenger is himself one of the imams, having assumed command from a previous imam. After completing the religious law, he hands command over to the next imam. The imam is therefore always present in the world; the world is never deprived of him. The imam upholds and maintains the religious law together with its esoteric reality, as we mentioned earlier. It is now clear that the progression of the religious cycle depends on the imam, and that it is the imam's prerogative how he acts on the prophet's law during his time. No one can reach the prophet, attain his rank, or arrive at the proper understanding of the religious law — which is changed or replaced only by the imam — without going through the imam. It is through the imam alone that one can attain the inner truth of the religious law, its interpretation, and its meaning.

[5] Many works written by the elders of the daʿwa to affirm the imamate draw on deduction and evidence from well-known Qurʾanic verses revealed concerning the imamate and the imams, peace be upon them, as well as traditions reported from the Prophet. Those who came before have left nothing in this matter for those who came later, and there is little point in simply restating the same ideas in different words. We therefore wish to write a treatise affirming the imamate using evidence (istishhād) and deduction (istidlāl) drawn from the outer horizons (al-āfāq) and our inner selves (anfus),12 from politics and governance (siyāsa) and from established norms (rusūm), by way of intellect (ʿaql), self-evidence (ḍarūra), innate constitution (al-fiṭra), natural disposition (al-jibla), and the agreement (al-ittifāq) of all religious communities (ahl kull milla), as well as from philosophers (al-falāsifa) and mathematicians (ahl al-ḥisāb). We shall further draw evidence from the structure of religious duties (farāʾiḍ) and practices in a manner that

12. Qurʾan 41:53, later cited in full by the author: "We shall show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes manifest to them that it is the truth."

<!-- page 52 -->
has not been attempted by the elders of the daʿwa. If what follows is found to be sound, it is owing to the excellence of support from the master of the age and the time (walī al-ʿaṣr wa'l-zamān). If there is any error, it is due to my own weakness and shortcoming — and that is most fitting for one such as me.

Section Two

[6] We say, then: the necessity (wujūb) of the imamate and of the imams, peace be upon them, is grounded in natural disposition (jibla), innate constitution (fiṭra), nature (ṭabīʿa), intellect (ʿaql), governance (siyāsa), prescription (waḍʿa), and established norms (rusūm).13 It is obligatory in all revealed law (sharīʿa), religion, and religious communities, just as the existence of the Creator is necessary by innate constitution, natural disposition, and intellect in every religious community and religion. Yet people fell into disagreement about the Creator after His necessity had been established and after they had accepted His existence. They began drawing conclusions according to their personal whims and opinions, and through blind imitation of their forebears and teachers, claiming that the Creator — whose existence innate constitution and natural disposition require — is in reality nothing but substance, or the elements, or the stars, or the sun, or something else of that kind. At times they described the Creator with attributes that amount to likening Him to His creation — that is, anthropomorphism — or to something bordering on polytheism, basing all of this on their own judgement without any authentic knowledge. In the same way, with regard to the necessity of the imamate, people fell into disagreement after committing themselves to the imam. Some insisted that the imam established by intellect and self-evidence is simply the wise man of every age — a view akin to that of the philosophers, who call their teachers "imams" (aʾimma), "sages" (ḥukamāʾ), "head of the scholars" (raʾs al-ʿulamāʾ), "master of the world" (saʾis), and "the teacher" (al-muʿallim).

13. Compare with Abū'l-Fawāris' al-Risāla fī'l-imāma, ed. and tr. Makarem as Political Doctrine, pp. 22–25 of the English translation and pp. 2–7 of the Arabic text.

<!-- page 53 -->
Section Three

[7] Followers of various religions have called their past imams "prophets" and "sages" in every cycle, while certain followers of Islam have applied the title "imam" to usurping, oppressive false pretenders, and have likewise called their scholars and jurists "imams."14 Yet, if you examine all these cases carefully, every one of them confirms the existence of the imam as a necessity (ḍarūra) and is unable to deny it.15 Whoever seeks salvation and guidance must set aside his inclinations and desires and come to understand the deficiency in every false claimant to this designation — his want of knowledge, gnosis, and cognition, his lack of piety, and his failure to meet all the other conditions of the imamate. Once such a person recognizes the pretender's ignorance and the shallowness of his piety, modesty, and his inability to fulfil any of the conditions of the imamate, he will reject that pretender's claim until such time as he attains recognition of the true imam (maʿrifat imām al-ḥaqq).

[8] We shall now examine some of the claims made by each pretender, along with his weaknesses and limitations. We shall turn his own principles against him and strip the imamate from those who are unfit for it. We shall then establish the imamate of the true imams, peace be upon them, with proofs and demonstrations, and establish the obligation of obeying them, entrusting our affairs to their command, and receiving revelation, interpretation, and the confession of Divine Unity from them — just as we set out in Kitāb al-tawḥīd (The Book on Divine Unity).16 All those who truly know created beings come to recognize their inability and deficiency and reject any claim of divinity for them, until they ultimately deny divinity to all created beings.

14. In the author's view, only the prophets and imams are entitled to the designation "imam."
15. Ḥamīd al-Dīn al-Kirmānī, in his work al-Maṣābīḥ fī ithbāt al-imāma (Beirut, 1969), pp. 80–95, enumerates fourteen points to prove the necessity of the imamate. See Makarem, Political Doctrine, pp. 37–39, where these points are summarized. For details, see Paul Walker's new Arabic edition and English translation of the Ithbāt as Master of the Age (London, 2007), pp. 71–79 (English) and pp. 36–45 (Arabic).
16. As observed by Ismail Poonawala in his Biobibliography, this work of al-Naysābūrī is held in the collection of the late Muṣṭafā Ghālib. See also Cortese, Ismaili and Other Arabic Manuscripts, pp. 94 and 96.

<!-- page 54 -->
In this way Divine Unity remains pure — free from the ascription of human qualities to God (tashbīh) and free from the denial of all attributes to God (taʿṭīl).

[9] We also say that God, the Exalted, through His subtle wisdom and boundless power, created for the human body certain principles, causes, and means. He made all the derivatives and realms of nature manifest in them and through their effects, so that they would emerge in a predetermined manner and within a set term, until He brought forth from them a body with senses that correspond to and are in harmony with those principles. He made those principles and means the body's sustenance and nourishment, through which it survives and through which its corporeal and perceptual reward and punishment are determined. In the same way, He established principles, causes, and means for souls, spirits, and religious noetic forms, from whose effects they become manifest. Through these, their sustenance, nourishment, survival, and reward are determined, while their punishment is determined by the disruption of their support and their distancing from their origin.

[10] God, may His name be exalted, made the principles and causes of the body a testimony and proof for the principles of the soul, as well as for its derivatives and its nourishment, so that the religious creation would mirror the physical creation, corresponding and parallel to it. Just as the body manifests and perfects itself only through the celestial spheres together with the stars (kawākib) — for it cannot be perfected through the spheres alone without the stars, even though the stars themselves revolve around the spheres, and yet the course of events is always shaped by the stars — so too the manifestation and perfection of the religious noetic forms and the everlasting souls come about only through the influence of the imams, peace be upon them. Their nourishment comes from the imams' knowledge, for the imams are the religious stars, as the Prophet, may God bless him and his family, said: "My companions are like the stars; whomsoever you emulate, you will be guided."17 This holds even as the imams act in the place of the

17. al-Nuʿmān, Sharḥ al-akhbār, vol. 2, pp. 248–249. Note that this hadith is not listed in A. J. Wensinck, A Handbook of Early Muhammadan Tradition (Leiden,

<!-- page 55 -->
prophets, who are like the celestial spheres. The course of affairs for all created beings, and their ultimate recourse, is therefore toward the imams. Were we to elaborate fully on the parallel between the religious celestial spheres and the natural celestial spheres, this epistle would become overlong and exceed its intended scope. We shall therefore confine ourselves to certain indications in the introduction of this treatise, for those who wish to pursue further research and grasp aspects of what we have left unmentioned, if God, the Exalted, wills.

[11] We say, accordingly, that the existence of differences and degrees of excellence in all things — encompassing the elements, the realms of nature, and their derivatives — constitutes the firmest evidence and proof for affirming the imamate and the imams.18 This is because God, the Exalted, created all things different and disparate in their degree of excellence across all genera and species, and then assigned to each genus and species an upper limit (ghāya), a summit (dhurwa), an end (nihāya), and a distinguishing virtue (faḍīla) that nothing else in that category can attain. He either made those genera and species foundational to creation — without which humanity cannot manage, nor their bodily existence and function be complete — or He made them sources of nourishment, remedy, elegance, adornment, and indispensable tools. In all things He placed an advantage (manfaʿa) that exists nowhere else. He powerfully endowed in them a capacity that creation would necessarily need — one it can neither avoid nor sustain its physical affairs without. He also subjected them to a test and impelled them to seek whatever would remedy their deficiency.
