Thus, when one cannot find the cure for that deficiency in its immediate place, one seeks it elsewhere or hastens to wherever it may be found.

18. See Abū'l-Fawāris, al-Risāla fī'l-imāma (ed. and tr. Makarem as Political Doctrine, p. 34 of the English translation and p. 18 of the Arabic text), where he says that the judgement of reason assumes and testifies that the disparity of mankind is a divinely conceived wisdom, conceived primordially by God.

<!-- page 56 -->
As long as one remains in the world of the body, one cannot for a single moment do without breathing (tanāsum) and drawing benefit from the elements of one's world — its derivatives, realms of nature, vegetation, and nourishment. All of this is a wisdom from God, the Exalted, and a mark of His justice, so that these aspects might serve as witnesses and as ephemeral, obligatory symbols (al-mithālāt al-jabriyyāt) for the symbolized realities that are religious, eternal, and chosen. The benefit of all symbolized realities — encompassing principles, realms of nature, nourishment, and religious remedies — is more deserving, more worthy, and more powerful than the obligatory symbols. The need for them is greater than the need for what nature compels, because the benefit of the natural symbols returns to the body, while the benefit of the symbolized realities returns to the spirit and the soul. The degree to which the intellectual symbolized realities excel the natural symbols corresponds to the degree to which the spirit excels the body. Likewise, the superiority of the symbolized over the symbol corresponds to the superiority of the everlasting over the perishable. The excellence in each one of them reflects the deficiency of the other. Whoever is deprived of the physical things he needs will not necessarily be destroyed by that deprivation — and even if such destruction were to occur, it would only be the destruction of his body, which will undoubtedly perish and die in any case. In the absence of the symbolized religious reality, however, the soul faces destruction and plunges into perpetual punishment. If people's striving to obtain what the soul needs were equal to their striving and desire to obtain what the body needs, then no two people would doubt, go astray, or fall into anthropomorphism (tashbīh).

[12] We also say that everything God, the Exalted, has created in this order — and humanity's need of it, as we described earlier — is an admonition pointing them toward the symbolized religious reality. It is a witness for all who seek such testimony, a proof for all who ask for evidence, and an insight for all who observe and reflect. It is equally a proof against those who deny the symbolized religious reality of the imams and their hierarchies — denying their knowledge and the benefit they bring — while at the same time accepting the benefit derived from natural elements, planets, and celestial spheres.


<!-- page 57 -->
God, the Exalted, established these elements, means, and natural causes so firmly that no one can deny them, argue against them, alter them, or change them in any way. This stands in stark contrast to what certain people did with the religious law and with the testimony the Prophet had called them to bear — namely, affirmation of the imamate and the imams, the obligation to follow them, devotion to them, obedience to them, condemnation of those who disobey them, and the promise of reward for those who obey. They distorted all of this, just as they changed and distorted the Quran. God, the Exalted, has informed us of this in His words: *…changing words from their context while they forgot a portion of what We have reminded them of* (5:13). And God, Whose mention is exalted, has also said: *Indeed, forgetfulness is only an excess of disbelief…* (9:37). Yet what they committed was not forgetfulness — they abandoned the testimony deliberately, as though they had never seen or heard it.

[13] Just as the testimony of the outer horizons and the inner selves is compulsory rather than chosen — and therefore impossible for anyone to alter, replace, or suppress — so too did the Messenger, may God bless him, make testimony compulsory, allowing witnesses no choice to change, alter, or conceal it. He threatened those who would change or conceal it with a painful punishment. God, the Exalted, commanded that judgement be rendered on the basis of the testimony of two witnesses, saying: *If one of them falls into error, the other will remind. And the witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned* (2:282). By this He meant that true judgement in religion — and in affirming the imamate and the hierarchies — rests with two witnesses: one established by law and the other compelled by nature. When people deny the legally established testimony, the one bound by nature — namely, the horizons and our own inner selves — steps in to remind, enforce, and verify the conventional witnesses. As God, the Exalted, says: *We shall show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes manifest to them that it is the*

<!-- page 58 -->
*Truth* (41:53). Similarly, when the Prophet was asked, "Who is the witness of your prophethood?" he answered: "My witness is every stone and every piece of clay."23 He was thereby pointing to the testimony of the horizons and the souls. When legal and natural witnesses both testify, and the intellect serves as the criterion for weighing that testimony without preference or partiality, truth swiftly becomes manifest.

[14] We shall now discuss the differences and degrees of excellence within each principle and realm of nature, and show that the ultimate limit and highest summit in every genus and species points to the imam in every age and time. If these genera and species demonstrate the imamate and the imams through a different framework — such as the seven planets serving as testimony for the seven imams and other heptads — that does not preclude proof being derived, through the peak and perfection of every genus and species, for the imam as we have just described.24

[15] The wisest among the philosophers speak of ten categories and hold that nothing can be mentioned or discussed except in relation to these ten: substance (jawhar), quantity (kammiyya), quality (kayfiyya), relation (idāfa), place (makān), time (zamān), possession (jidda), posture (nusba), action (fā'il), and affection (maf'ūl). The highest of the ten is substance, upon which all others rest and over which all others it excels, since the remaining nine are established through it. The substance that philosophers describe is therefore a proof for the permanent, intellectual, luminous, and authentic substance by which all souls are illumined and upon which all nine religious accidents (al-a'rād) depend.25 Yet this substance has no

<!-- page 59 -->
need of any created thing. It draws benefit from nothing and no one in the world; rather, everything benefits from it and stands in need of it. That is the imam, peace be upon him. All other ranks stand below him just as the nine accidents stand in relation to substance.

[16] We also say that among the four basic constituents (al-'anāṣir) from which the first composites (tarākib) were formed, two are active and two are passive. The active ones are heat and cold, while the passive ones are dryness and humidity. The highest and noblest among them is heat, which gives rise to luminosity, light, motion, movement, and elevation, while the other three are established through it. Heat thus takes precedence both in origin and in existence, and no foundation or realm of nature is complete without it. In the same way, the imam illumines souls, and from him come intellectual light (al-nūr al-'aqlī), motion (al-ḥaraka), and elevation (al-'ulūw). No principle or religious realm is complete without him.

Among the celestial spheres, the most exalted and supreme is the outermost sphere (falak al-aflāk), within which all the others orbit. In the same way, the imam in his religion is the supreme religious sphere encompassing all others, and all heavenly bodies and religious signs of the zodiac move under his command. If someone were to argue that this is rather a symbol for the qā'im (the Resurrector), on the grounds that the highest principle in the world corresponds to the Intellect with respect to the four principles,27 our reply is that the imam is the qā'im in his own time; he is the proclaimer (nāṭiq) in his age, and he is the one who occupies the position of the qā'im. The imam's position in his own realm is that of the Universal Intellect ('aql al-kullī).

**Section Four**

[17] We also say that among the four elements (al-arkān), the highest and noblest is fire, which stands at the summit and peak —

<!-- page 60 -->
a level no other element or nature can reach.28 In the same way, the imam in his age and time is the highest religious pillar through whom souls are illumined. It is on this basis that Abraham commanded glorification of the fire — if what has been reported from him is accurate — and forbade extinguishing or putting it out.29 The real intent, however, pointed toward glorifying the imamate, which no one must extinguish through the injustice of those who falsely claim its position. The fire worshippers fell into confusion, failing to grasp the symbolized reality behind the symbol. God, the Exalted, described the adversaries and usurpers of the imamate in His words: *They would like to extinguish God's light with their mouths, but God will not allow that to happen until He perfects His light, even if the unbelievers may detest it* (9:32).

[18] We also maintain that among the many planets (kawākib) there are differences and gradations in degrees of excellence. Their peak and summit in nobility and merit is the sun, through which physical eyesight draws its light and through which all plants, animals, and newborns grow and reach their perfection. No plant or newborn grows without the influence of the sun.31 Like-

<!-- page 61 -->
wise, the imam is the sun of the religion, through which insight is enlightened and souls are illumined by the light of guidance and wisdom, radiating through the hearts of God's friends. It is because of this that some people have been moved to glorify the sun. Yet sun-worshippers too fell into confusion, grasping only the symbol without recognizing the reality it signified. For this reason, the Prophet, may God bless him, spoke of the rising of the sun from the west as a sign of the imam's own "rising," peace be upon him, in the Maghrib.32

Were the physical sun to deviate from its established orbit — from its ascent and descent from its centre — even for the blink of an eye, the entire world would be destroyed. That, as we know, is impossible both rationally and naturally. The Prophet's statement therefore holds true only on the basis of esoteric interpretation. Indeed, that interpretation has proven correct, as we have witnessed with our own eyes by the grace and favour of God.33

[19] We also say that among the twelve signs of the zodiac, four are the most glorious and excellent, and among those four one stands at the peak and summit in nobility. Likewise, among the twelve proofs (ḥujaj),34 the one who is connected to the imamate is the most excellent, the noblest, and the most exalted of them all.

<!-- page 62 -->
**Section Five**

[20] We maintain that the fourth clime (iqlīm), which corresponds to the fourth heaven and aligns with the sun, is the most moderate as well as the most excellent and noble of all. In the same way, the imam is the most excellent and most glorious within the hierarchy, standing as their peak and apex.35

[21] We also say that among the melting mineral substances the most excellent is gold. It is the medium through which people conduct their transactions; it establishes the value of all valuables, and it is unaffected by fire or water and cannot be destroyed. Likewise, the imam, peace of God be upon him, is the most excellent religious and intellectual substance in his age and time. Through him the transactions of creation and the well-being of people — in both worldly and religious terms — are brought to completion. Each melting substance has its own peak of nobility.

Among precious stones, the finest is the sapphire, and among all sapphires — white, green, yellow, and red — it is the red ruby that excels above them all. No other jewel endures like the ruby: it preserves its essence unchanged by fire or water, retaining at all times its purity, radiance, and gravity. It draws properties from its source that no other substance can. Not even other sapphires, emeralds, the Badakhshi stone, carnelian, agate, diamonds, turquoise, or jade can retain those qualities. The crown among jewels is therefore the red ruby, just as the imam is the pinnacle of all creation in his age and time — unique among all the ranks of the hierarchy: the proofs (ḥujaj), the adjuncts (lawāḥiq), the summoner (dā'ī), the wing (janāḥ), the licensee (ma'dhūn), and the motivator (mukallīb).36

<!-- page 63 -->
The imam is thus the apex whose status, rank, and position no one in his age or time is able to attain. From another angle, the seven jewels serve as proofs for the seven proclaimers and the seven imams. But the argument on which we are relying here — degrees of excellence — carries greater force. That is because the natural symbol is present in every age and time, and the symbolized reality must therefore also be present in every age and time. Since the stars, the natural elements, the realms of nature, and the substances are ever-present, it is equally necessary that the ranks of the hierarchy exist in every age and time, so that they may always exercise their influence upon souls.

[22] We maintain that there are seven grains that provide human beings with food and nourishment, and their peak and summit is wheat — the most balanced in nature and nobility, and the grain that builds the body. In the same way, the imam is the peak and summit through whom souls benefit and are nourished. From him comes their livelihood and their life, and from him they receive knowledge.

**Section Six**

[23] As for fruit-bearing trees, the peak and summit among them is the date palm. It resembles human beings in having male and female, and it requires fertilisation just as animals require mating. Furthermore, every part of the date palm is beneficial and nothing goes to waste; its fruit is eaten at all times, whether raw or cooked. When someone dreams while asleep that he is plucking dates from a date palm, the interpretation is that he is going to receive benefit from a certain believer. That believer, in reality, is the imam — who is the head of all believers.

[24] We also maintain that among plants, certain mild medicines have attained such a degree of refinement and excellence that they have become antidotes (tiryāqan) for those on the verge of death. Among these, some cure chronic diseases while others are lower in degree. The peak and summit among them points to the imam; the


<!-- page 64 -->
The imam and his word become antidotes for souls — a means of healing and relief from death, illness, doubt, and discord. The opponents who forcibly usurp the position of the imams, peace be upon them, resemble the fatal poisons and harmful substances found among medicines. They are deadly poisons that destroy those who listen to them, accompany them, or incline toward them — unless the imam reaches those people with the great antidote that rescues their souls from destruction. These opponents are likewise comparable to useless and worthless stones. It is for this reason that God, the Exalted, said: *'... pebble-covered hell where you will return'*³⁷ and His words: *'... and guard yourself against the Fire that has been ignited for the people and the stones that have been prepared for the non-believers.'*³⁸

[25] We also say that among animals the horse is the finest, among birds the eagle, and among beasts of prey the lion holds first place. The differences among these various species — in strength, usefulness, capacity for training, and the summit each attains within its own kind — point to the fact that there is a peak within the genus of animals, just as within the human species that summit is the imam, peace of God be upon him.

[26] We further say that some plants are sweet, delicious, and appetizing, and people benefit from them. Others are bitter, offering neither nourishment nor pleasure. Still others bear no fruit and are thorned, injuring those who come near them. The sweet and pleasant ones are like the imams — a symbol of their speech and knowledge, which captivate the soul and delight the eye. The bitter, putrid, and thorny ones symbolize the leaders of the literalists (ahl al-zahir). For this reason God, the Exalted, said: *'The example of a good word is like a good tree whose roots are firmly established and whose branches reach the heavens, giving its fruit perpetually in every season by the permission of its Lord. So God sets forth similitudes for mankind in order that they may reflect. And the similitude of a bad word is like that of a bad tree uprooted from the top of the earth, having no stability.'*³⁹

37. Qur'an, 21: 98.
38. Qur'an, 2: 24.
39. Qur'an, 14: 24–26.

<!-- page 65 -->
[27] We also say that some plants are sweet-smelling, their fragrance refreshing and healing people of all diseases and ailments. The summit of their goodness and fragrance is a symbol of the imam and his words, which refresh and bring ease to people, while the putrid, repellent, and offensive plants are an indication of the imams of error, whose speech harms souls and may even destroy them.

[28] We maintain that the musk-giving gazelle and the honey-producing bee — indeed their chief (ya'sub) — are an indication of the imam. It is for this reason that the Prophet, may God bless him, said to the Commander of the Faithful, 'Ali: *'You are the chief (ya'sub) of the believers.'*⁴⁰ And God, the Exalted, said: *'And your Lord inspired the bee to choose its homes in the mountains and on the trees and on that which they thatch; then eat from all the fruits and follow the path of your Lord skilfully, that He has made smooth for you. From within its bellies a drink of varying colours comes forth in which there is healing for mankind ...'*⁴¹

[29] In the same way, we maintain that humankind is the ultimate among animals and within the realms of nature. The advantage found in everything that preceded the creation of mankind — principles, derivatives, and realms of nature alike — returns ultimately to humankind. The merits of the world, of animals, plants, and all other things, are contained within the human being; their benefit, glory, value, and goodness all become manifest through mankind. Were it not for mankind, the merit of all things would never become apparent, nor would the order of the world hold. Nothing would be complete; no benefit would accrue, nor would anything be preserved — whether from plants or animals. It is the human being who harnesses water and irrigates the land, bringing it under rational control and drawing it from valleys, rivers, and wells. It is man who tills the earth, cultivates it, builds dwellings upon it, and waters plants and fields, bringing the land under his rational governance.

Beyond this, it is the human being who has made use of wind and air; it is man who discovered fire by means of flint and achieved the crossbreeding of animals where none existed before. Man grafted

40. al-Majlisi, Bihar al-anwar, vol. 38, p. 144.
41. Qur'an, 16: 68–69.

<!-- page 66 -->
different trees to produce fruits previously unknown in the world. He extracted jewels from minerals and brought beneficial trees, seeds, and fruits under his control and governance. He protected the welfare of animals and plants and their produce, and brought animals under his command. Man is the pinnacle of animals, plants, minerals, and all other realms of nature. Through him, the welfare of animals and everything that came before was perfected and ordered. Likewise, the imam is the pinnacle and summit of all human beings — their quintessence and their perfection. It is through the imam that the affairs of mankind are set in order. Just as the benefit of everything that preceded human beings returns to mankind, so too the benefit of all humanity and their quintessence returns to the imams, peace be upon them. Human beings learn virtues and knowledge from them, and acquire minds and understanding through them. It is through the imams that human welfare is safeguarded in worldly and religious matters and in the life hereafter. The imams guide people toward recognition of the Creator (ma'rifa al-Sani'), teaching the necessity of gratitude to the benefactor and the proper manner of expressing it. It is through the imams that mankind recognizes the messengers and learns how they ought to be obeyed.

[30] Just as the benefit of all animals and everything that preceded them returns to mankind — and man thereby became their leader — so too the benefit of all human beings returns to the imams, who became their leaders, their commanders, and their restrainers. Were it not for the imams, and the direction and guidance they give humanity, and their protection of human welfare in this life and the next, and their encouragement to acquire virtues and avoid vices and animal habits, there would be no difference between beasts and human beings — indeed, animals would be the superior. If man did not accept the training needed to acquire virtues for his soul, and did not learn to stimulate his intellect through the imams, he would be worse off than animals, since animals are not as prone to evil as humans are, and animals possess numerous benefits. Without intellect and religion, man would be wholly given over to evil and corruption. It is on this account that God, the Exalted and Glorious, said: *'For the worst of beasts in the sight of God are the deaf and dumb — those who*

<!-- page 67 -->
*understand not.'*⁴² The Exalted also said: *'They are like cattle — nay, more misguided, for they are heedless of warning.'*⁴³

Were there no humanity, there would be no wisdom in the creation of animals and plants, for there would be no one to benefit from them. Likewise, were it not for the imams, peace be upon them, there would be no wisdom in creating humanity, and their superiority would never become manifest. Human beings have been created for religion and for the acquisition of virtues and knowledge. So learn knowledge and religion from the imams, peace be upon them, for through them religion is established and perfected. If the excellence of man were not made manifest through religion, then the value of plants and animals would not become known either, and there would be no wisdom in the creation of humanity, plants, and animals. And if there were no wisdom in their creation, there would be no wisdom in the creation of all the worlds.

[31] It is therefore true to say that all the worlds — the entirety of natural creation — have been created for humanity, and that humanity has been created for the imams, peace be upon them: to receive religion from them, to be perfected through them, and to manifest their virtues, their organization, and their foundation. Since all the worlds and all creatures were created for the imams, peace be upon them, and all the benefits of the worlds belong to them, and the rest of creation depends on them and stands to them as children, whatever they bestow upon creation is given by their favour, generosity, and mercy. If they withhold anything, they are under no obligation to give, nor do they owe gratitude for it. Whoever takes anything from this world and what it contains without the permission and approval of the imam has taken what is haram — forbidden — and has committed an act of usurpation. Thus God said: *'And that which God bestows on the Messenger from the people of the villages, it is for God and His Messenger.'*⁴⁴ The word 'bestowal' (fay') means 'return,' and it is for this reason that a shadow is called fay' — because it returns to the place from which it first came. The whole world comes from

42. Qur'an, 8: 22.
43. Qur'an, 7: 179.
44. Qur'an, 59: 7.

<!-- page 68 -->
the fortune and property of the imams, and whatever returns to their property is, in truth, a fay' — a return.

[32] We further say that plants surpass the earth by virtue of a single additional spirit — the spirit of growth (ruh al-nama') — and the earth thereby becomes subservient to the plants growing upon it, which draw their strength from it. Animals surpass plants by yet another spirit added to the spirit of growth — the spirit of sensation (ruh al-hiss) — and so they subdue the earth, moving across it, and subdue the plants by eating them. Since humans surpass animals by means of the spirit of articulated speech or reason (ruh al-nutq), they take charge of the earth: they build, sow, and plant upon it, bringing it under their wisdom and control. They eat plants and fruits, using what they wish and burning what they will. Man also subdues animals — riding those he needs, slaughtering some, milking others, shearing their wool, and employing them as he sees fit, even against the animals' own inclination.

When the imam assumes his charge, he is endowed with additional spirits, powers, and a purity that no ordinary human being possesses. Thus the pinnacle of purity in the human being is combined in the imam together with the subtlety and refinement of nature. The peak of the sensuous animals, their choicest elements, the furthest felicity of the celestial spheres, the balance of nature, and the subtlest influence of all the celestial spheres and stars are so combined in him that he becomes the quintessence of the whole world. Then the subtleties of the spirit of reason, pure thought, and the entire intellectual spirit unite in the imam, along with the total sanctified spirit (ruh al-quds). Man possesses only a portion of this spirit — no more than what he acquires from the imam, and no more than the imam chooses to bestow by his grace. Connected to this is the creative spirit (ruh al-ibda'), which is the perfect seventh spirit that God has prepared for humanity in the world of reward. It is the spirit of Oneness (ruh al-wahda).

[33] These are some of the merits that God, the Exalted, has bestowed upon him, in addition to what he attains purely through purity of character, generosity, giving, forbearance, courage, and other qualities that the likes of us cannot enumerate — along with his freedom from all impurity, shame, and defect. So when God

<!-- page 69 -->
gathered in the imams all these faculties, merits, and spirits — in addition to what mankind already possesses — He subjected all humanity to them, giving them control over everything that preceded mankind: animals, plants, earth, and minerals, making all of it subservient to the imams. This mirrors the way humans dominate animals and rule over them by virtue of one additional spirit, and the way animals control plants by virtue of one additional spirit, and the way plants dominate the earth by virtue of yet another additional spirit. Moreover, the great number of spirits residing in the imams, peace be upon them, determines the extent of their control and dominion over humanity, entitling them to rule over people convincingly in accordance with the requirements of those faculties and spirits — in light of what we described earlier concerning human beings. It is therefore not for human beings to remove themselves from the imam's domain and reign, just as it is not for animals to remove themselves from the control of man. Those animals that refuse to submit to human control — such as beasts of prey that offer no benefit or merit — are judged by reason and by law to be lawfully killed, destroyed, and eliminated, because they refuse to obey human beings and remain under the authority of their supremacy.

In the same manner, those who distance themselves from obedience to the imam prove themselves to be like wild beasts, whose killing, destruction, and elimination becomes lawful — on account of their rejection of the imams' rule as required by religious law, intellect, nature, and sound governance.⁴⁵

But whoever obeys the imams, peace be upon them, and submits to their rulings in this world will escape the sword and punishment here, and will live in peace, well-being, and justice. It is obligatory upon the imam, peace be upon him — by the norm of kingship, the demands of nature, sound policy, and law — to protect the welfare of mankind, just as man, in governing animals, protects their well-being, nourishment, and flourishing. Whoever submits to the imams' rule over their own spirit and body will be saturated with knowledge and wisdom, invigorated by it and enabled to

45. See Daftary, The Ismailis, 2nd ed., pp. 178–191, where he describes other serious revolts including that of Abu Rakwa Walid b. Hisham, who claimed descent from the Umayyads of Spain.

<!-- page 70 -->
stand out among other people, living in the tranquillity of knowledge and enjoying its fragrance. They will depart this abode of deeds for the abode of recompense in an upright form, fully prepared to receive their recompense and eternal life.

Whoever submits to the imams' rule outwardly but not inwardly will fall into the torment of ignorance, perplexity, doubt, discord, and regret, and will arrive at the abode of recompense in a crooked form, unprepared to receive recompense, and will remain in torment and punishment.

[34] We further say that human beings, at all times and in every moment, look after the welfare of animals — protecting them from whatever harms them and leading them toward what benefits them. Man guards their watering places and grazing grounds, their seasons of reproduction and the bearing of offspring, shielding them from destruction and from environments that would harm their eating and drinking. Often cattle covet things that would harm them, and man prevents this, safeguarding their well-being even against the animals' own will.

Likewise the imam, peace be upon him, protects the outcome of human affairs — their welfare, all their conditions, means, transactions, and hidden interests — out of compassion for them. Were it not for the imam, man would have no order in his worldly affairs, no safety or escape from those stronger than him, no security from disputes, and no protection from punishment in the hereafter. Furthermore, man endures pain from animals and bears their bad behaviour with patience, in expectation of their improvement, for the judgement of wisdom requires that one who possesses knowledge must not destroy, harm, or eliminate any living creature without good reason — rather, he is obliged to save them from destruction. Similarly, wisdom dictates that property must be protected from destruction and must not be rendered unavailable for human use. Likewise the imam, peace be upon him, tolerates the misconduct of people and bears pain and hardship from them, since the principles of sovereignty and the wisdom of sound judgement require that the ignorant be guided and their welfare protected, shielded from perdition and ruin, in the hope that they will eventually return to good conduct and attain salvation. The imam does not look at their


<!-- page 71 -->
The imam does not look upon people with contempt for their ignorance or their lack of obedience; rather, he looks upon them with an eye of mercy.

[35] We also say that a man tolerates pain and hardship from his young children and prays for them, hoping they will realize their human potential and be rightly guided. In the same way, the imam, peace be upon him, tolerates pain and hardship from people out of mercy and compassion — a compassion like that of parents for their children. He spends on them in both worldly and religious matters, hoping they will be rightly guided and saved.

[36] We say further that a teacher endures hardship on account of young boys, compelling them to learn the sciences and proper conduct, while the child resists this, resenting the teacher and preferring play and idleness. The teacher cares for the child and bears with him, knowing that if the child could see what benefit he would gain, he would not resent the teacher. The teacher also knows that when the boy reaches maturity, he will recognize the value of that discipline and feel gratitude, regretting his earlier failings and disobedience. Likewise, the imam, peace be upon him, guides humanity toward their own worldly and religious good, even as they resist it and object to how he deals with them. In time, when the benefit becomes clear to them, they regret their disobedience.

[37] We also say that because man is created from various opposing natures, he is never entirely free from natural sickness, illness, and disease. The influence of time, place, celestial spheres and stars, the effects of nourishment and other opposing elements in nature, over-indulgence, insufficient eating, exhaustion, sexual intercourse, and other such factors all give rise to illness and disease. Yet people were ignorant of medicine. Had they remained uninformed and ignorant in the face of so many illnesses, most people would have died and perished, bringing destruction upon the world. But God, the Exalted, created cures corresponding to each illness and inspired prophets and imams, peace be upon them, so that they came to understand the benefits of medicines, recognizing poisons and illnesses alike. They taught mankind, opening up for them the way of medicine, so that people began to build upon that foundation and improved it through experience and practice — just as they made progress in all trades, crafts, and sciences such as engineering,

<!-- page 72 -->
agriculture, navigation, and others — until there arose among mankind physicians to treat people, collecting medicines from far and distant lands. They laboured in that field, some for profit and some for the welfare of humanity, treating patients at times with wholesome and pleasant remedies and at other times with hot and bitter ones. A patient may detest the bitter, foul-smelling substance that will cure him, yet the physician makes him drink it — willingly or unwillingly — assuring him of its beneficial outcome, warning him that he faces death if he refuses, or gently coaxing him to endure the difficulty. At times the doctor forbids the patient from eating and drinking, even while the patient craves both. He may prohibit foods the patient enjoys and that suited him in good health. He may administer something harsh that strains a weakened constitution, all while the patient is in danger of death. If medication alone fails, the physician may order lancing, the opening of a wound, cauterization, or the amputation of a limb in the hope of saving the rest.

A person of sound mind will accept all this, being persuaded that the doctor knows more about his condition than he does and that the doctor is a trustworthy guarantor who seeks only his welfare and recovery. But a person lacking in reason will resent and despise the doctor, while the doctor responds with understanding when he sees a man of cold constitution afflicted with illness. Knowing that without treatment the patient will die, the doctor, out of kindness and compassion, restrains the patient and forces him to drink the medicine. The patient may curse him, grow agitated, and threaten to strike him, but the doctor bears it with patience, knowing that if the patient were aware and rational he would not resist treatment. The doctor is not diverted by the patient's bad behaviour; instead, he remains kind and continues treating him until his health is restored.

[38] In the same way, when spiritual diseases multiply — arising from disputes among people, from their arbitrary desires, their inclinations toward leisure, comfort, and self-disclosure, from innovation, worldly ambitions, conflicting doctrines, contradictory statements, diverse opinions, and the lower self that incites men to evil — while the night is dark and the path obscure, and people are ignorant of medicine for the soul: ignorant of its cure, how to preserve its health,

<!-- page 73 -->
and how to guard against harmful conditions and things — ignorant too of the religious ways and paths — then humanity needs a guide and a physician to treat their illnesses, show them right action, and save their spirits. This must be done with gentleness, compassion, counsel, affection, tolerance, and encouragement — requiring many times more patience and compassion than that shown by physicians of the body. There must be no greed in their treatment, no seeking of personal gain, no deflection of harm for selfish reasons. These guides must be the most reliable and trustworthy custodians of souls and of religion, surpassing even the most dependable physicians of the body in their expertise, experience, and knowledge.

[39] For the risk and danger involved in treating the soul are greater than those of treating the body, and errors in it can be far more severe. The necessity of such a guide is as intellectually inescapable as the necessity of a medical doctor is practically inescapable. This knowledgeable and compassionate physician of souls is the imam, peace be upon him, in every age and time. Were it not for the mercy, compassion, and kindness of the imams, peace be upon them, toward all of creation, people would perish in both their worldly and religious affairs — in this life and in the life to come. The imams, peace be upon them, treat created beings with subtle kindness, tolerance, and gentleness. They do not dwell on people's deficiency in obedience, their bad manners, or their evil actions; instead they look upon them with an eye of mercy, working to preserve their spiritual health and cure their illnesses — sometimes through a balanced and delicate diet, protecting their spiritual wellbeing through subtle sciences that strengthen the soul; at other times by withholding nourishment so that it is neither nauseating nor burdensome. At still other times, they compel people to drink a bitter, foul-smelling medicine so as to expel the corrupting humours (al-kaymūsāt). At yet other times, they prescribe a strict regimen until the person is brought to a state of utmost humility, all the while offering encouragement and warning — just as the Commander of the Faithful [al-Hakim] does with these people. Even as they continually desire corruption and he sees no good in them, he remains patient with the pain they cause. We pray that He allows us to be among those who benefit from the treatment of the imam of the time, peace be upon him, and that He does not place us among those who require burning

<!-- page 74 -->
or amputation of limbs through his turning away from us. Were it not for the mercy of the imams, not a single person would remain on the face of the earth — just as God has said: 'If God took humanity to task for what they deserve, He would not leave a [single] living creature on the surface of the earth ...'⁴⁶

[40] We also say that wise men and men of insight, in cultivating the world, consider their work analogous to God's actions in creating the world, arranging its environment, and preserving human welfare. When they find a good fertile land, they know it will receive seeds and bring forth growth. When they discover water in distant deep valleys or underground, they labour to bring it forth by digging canals and wells, directing that water to those fertile lands. They see no wisdom in leaving good fertile land unused and wasted, or in leaving water untapped — out of compassion for living creatures and concern for their welfare and the flourishing of the world.

Likewise, when the imams, peace be upon them, see people ready to receive the seed of wisdom and knowledge — even though they may be very far from actually being able to receive it — the imams invest great effort in digging canals through preparatory religious exercises on many occasions. People may be unaware of the purpose of such an exercise until they come to perceive its wisdom and become prepared and capable of receiving it. When the imams cannot bring people to that level through religious exercise alone, they embed wisdom in dense symbols — whether in words or in actions — so that people may receive those symbols and gradually arrive at their meaning, and are thereby elevated to that level of understanding.

[41] It is therefore necessary to believe in the supreme leadership, the imamate, of the imam of the time — to know that he is the head of the world and the vicegerent of God over His creation — and not to object to any of the imams' commands, prohibitions, actions, or teachings. One must know, in general, that all of this embodies wisdom and is for the welfare of creation. The correct meaning of a particular matter may at times be difficult for a believer to grasp, but in the end its benefit will become clear — just as many things that were once unknown have

⁴⁶. Qur'an, 35: 45.

<!-- page 75 -->
eventually become apparent and clear. So too, in much of the sharīʿa, the faithful do not initially understand the Legislator's purpose or the wisdom behind a ruling and may feel resistance to it; yet those whom God grants recognition of its meaning come to perceive the wisdom within it. Similarly, many people encounter things in the world whose wisdom they do not understand and mistakenly conclude that no benefit lies in them, only to realize later that wisdom was indeed present — wisdom they cannot then deny.

There are countless things whose creation people regard as harmful and destructive, seeing no wisdom or benefit in them, yet when they come to understand their wisdom they grow calm and their objections fall away. Whoever recognizes the Wise One (al-Hakim) and acknowledges that He is wise must inevitably acknowledge that everything the Wise One does is full of wisdom, that He would not do anything devoid of wisdom, and that He would not command, prohibit, or say anything except out of wisdom. A futile action is not permissible for the Wise One; were it permissible for the Wise One to act, speak, command, or forbid without wisdom, that would constitute a vain act — and vain acts are not permissible for the Wise One. Whoever does something where the doing and the not doing are entirely equivalent acts foolishly and steps outside the domain of wisdom.

[42] Furthermore, if it is not permissible for the Wise One to perform an action whose existence or non-existence amounts to the same thing, how then could the imam perform an action when wisdom lies in something other than what he has done — or in its very opposite — and when the welfare of creation lies elsewhere? The imam is absolutely wise, and his bestowal of wisdom upon any of his servants renders them wise. Whoever acts without wisdom and goodness acts corruptly and in vain — and may God protect us from those who believe such a thing of the imam of the time. To hold such a belief is to say that the individual has departed from wisdom, and whoever departs from wisdom has departed from the domain of his imamate — and that is a departure from the faith. May God protect us and all believers from any such doubt, notion, or belief, and may He bestow upon us contentment and unquestioning submission (taslīm).

[43] We also say that freely-grazing livestock, though possessed of their own benefits and worth, inevitably require a shepherd to tend them, look after and protect them, select their pasture and watering places, and safeguard them from thieves and beasts of prey. Without someone to undertake that role, they would perish — unable to manage their own affairs, defend themselves against predators, or secure their own good and well-being.

<!-- page 76 -->
People are likewise like sheep: without a shepherd to protect them, they would perish. The imam is their shepherd, safeguarding their worldly and religious affairs and protecting them from worldly and religious enemies alike. Were that role to be abandoned, not even two people would remain, nor would a single individual escape punishment. It is for this reason that people in all lands are called subjects (raʿīya) — because they are under the care of their shepherd — while the true shepherd is the imam, and all other claimants to that role are wolves devouring the flock.

[44] We maintain that the world is like the human body. If the body had no head, it would be a decaying corpse of no use to anyone. Similarly, if the world had no head, leader, or master (sāʾīs) to guide its worldly and religious affairs, people would become beasts devouring one another. The world would not endure even a single day, and there would be no wisdom in its creation. The imam is indeed the head of the world, its leader and its master.

[45] We say further that there are disparities (tafāwut) and differences in degrees of excellence (tafāḍul) among the organs of the body; the foremost and finest among them is the head. It is in the head that the face resides — the face by which each human being is recognized and distinguished from all others. Among the internal organs, the finest is the brain, which is their chief and the seat of the intellect. The imam holds a position in the world analogous to that of the head among the limbs, and analogous to the brain among the internal organs. All the affairs of creation revolve around him, just as all the actions of the body revolve around the brain. Among all the faculties and spirits in man, the finest is the intellect, and the imam is the Universal Intellect (ʿaql al-kullī) in the world — the one in whom and through whom all people of the world become united.

[46] As mentioned earlier, leadership in nature is present in everything. It is found among animals and birds: the stallion, for instance, protects and disciplines the mares. Likewise, the rooster protects


<!-- page 77 -->
Each species protects its young according to its capacity, defends its females, and guides the weak among them as each individual needs, benefiting from and learning from those above. The crane obeys its leader, and the bee obeys its queen.

[47] God, the Exalted, created the world and arranged its creatures — as we described to some extent earlier — according to this pattern of classification, disparity, and gradation in degrees of excellence, with varying levels of strength, nobility, and rank, so that it would be clear that within every species and genus there is a peak, a summit, and a chief in nobility and merit. By the same reasoning, all of mankind must likewise have a peak and summit in nobility and merit to serve as their leader and guide in the world. It has thus been established that headship and leadership are necessary features of nature and creation, as well as of natural disposition and intuition, and that even those who deny revelation and religion cannot deny — either rationally or by direct observation — that the world requires leadership, headship, and authority, for these are obligatory and necessary. It is therefore mandatory that this leader be the best among all people: the most noble, the most perfect, the most knowledgeable, and the most pure, in accordance with the arguments we have drawn from creation and nature. The foremost must be the imam in all things, the chief and the head. The title "supreme leader" (al-imām) cannot be applied to the one who is led (al-ma'mūm), nor the title "the excellent" (al-fādil) to "the one who is preferred over others" (al-mafdūl), nor the title "one who supersedes" (al-sābiq) to "one who is preceded" (al-masbūq). Indeed, people of every community and religion — including philosophers, atheists, and those who deny God all attributes — affirm that the leader of the world ought to be the best among them, the most perfect in merit, and the most knowledgeable of all people. In all the world, no one fits this description except the imams, peace be upon them.

[48] We also say that philosophers divided politics or governance (siyāsa) into three categories: governance of the individual (siyāsat al-khāṣṣa), governance of the family (siyāsat al-'āmma), and

<!-- page 78 -->
governance of the general public (siyāsat al-'āmma). Governance of the individual concerns a person's rule over himself; governance of the family concerns a man's management of his household and dependents; and governance of the general public concerns the administration of cities and provinces. To govern oneself properly, a person must restrain himself from all vices, bad character traits, bestial habits, and blameworthy desires, ruling his own soul with firm resolve — reproaching and repenting when it errs, and praising it and persisting in good conduct when it does well. To govern one's family is to manage one's household and dependents by protecting their welfare, encouraging them to acquire virtues and praiseworthy character, correcting those who err through reprimand and similar means, and rewarding those who do well with praise and the like.

[49] Governance of the general public means that a person governs cities, safeguards the welfare of people's livelihoods, and prevents them from reprehensible morals and bad conduct. He protects everyone in their proper stations, corrects those who err through reprimand, rewards those who do well through praise, and oversees the affairs of their religion and their hereafter. This is what philosophers call governance — yet it is found nowhere in the world among those who falsely claim the imamate, or among rulers, or among their scholars. This quality exists only in the imams from the family of Muhammad, may God bless them, and no one else in the world is capable of bearing this responsibility.

The philosophers agree that whoever cannot govern himself has no business governing his family, and whoever cannot manage his family well has no claim whatsoever to governance of the general public. Yet the literalists (ahl al-ẓāhir), their rulers,

<!-- page 79 -->
and their scholars do not possess this qualification. Those who have occupied the place of the imams and assumed their titles cannot govern themselves, their families, or the people; they are the ones we see today engrossed in amusement, moral depravity, and corruption. The qualities that philosophers use to define the political leader are found in none of these rulers. It seems evident that the author of this classification intended to encourage obedience to the imam, for he described the imam using the attributes of the ideal ruler, so that people would seek a ruler who met precisely this description. Whoever does not meet it would not be accepted — until the people find the true imam.

[50] We also say that since human beings are born ignorant, possessing no knowledge, and since knowledge does not arise in them from nature — nor does speech — they are in need of instruction. The proof that knowledge does not come from nature is this: if it did, people would naturally be scholars and would speak naturally, just as donkeys bray and dogs bark without any instruction, for it is obvious that these animals require no teaching for those sounds. The braying of donkeys and the barking of dogs is identical whether in Hind, Sind, Rūm, or the land of the Arabs.

Furthermore, if speech and knowledge were natural, as just noted, people would need no instruction for them, and all people would be equal in these respects with no differences among them — just as the braying of donkeys does not differ from place to place. Since people do need instruction, each learns from another, in a chain that leads back to the one who received no instruction from any human being but received knowledge through divine inspiration rather than teaching. This deficiency in human beings and their need for instruction necessitates a teacher, which in turn necessitates an ultimate end-point in the chain of teachers — namely, the prophet and the imam, peace be upon them. This is among the most compelling

<!-- page 80 -->
and convincing proofs for confirming the imamate and the prophethood.

[51] We also say that God, the Exalted, created human beings deficient and ignorant so that they would be receptive to knowledge and wisdom. Had He left them to themselves, there would have been no obligation upon them, no deeds to perform, no reward, and no punishment, and there would have been no wisdom in creating them at all. So when God inspired prophets and imams with wisdom and knowledge, they in turn conveyed it to mankind, explaining their guidance and their path to the Truth, and imposing upon them obligations, commands, and prohibitions.

Moreover, were it not for the imams, humanity would bear no responsibility, and there would be no guarantor or proof over them. The imams are therefore God's proofs to His creation at all times — from the very beginning of their moral responsibility and whenever they fall into disagreement. If people fell into sectarian conflict, each group citing a verse of the Qur'an, a hadith, or analogical reasoning, no proof would be established for any of them against the others. If there were no imam in the world to rescue them from disagreement, confusion, and perplexity and to guide them to the Truth, then God would have no proof against His creatures — rather, the creatures would have a proof against God, saying: "We found no one to guide us from falsehood to the truth." It is through the imam, therefore, that God's proof is established over the people, just as God, the Exalted, said: "Say, to God belongs the most apparent proof." (Qur'an, 6:149)

[52] We also say that the rules and principles attributed to the sages are, in reality, the teachings of the imams, peace be upon them. From them, people derived the foundations of every branch of knowledge and wisdom, including arithmetic and geometry. Each discipline has a purpose and an ultimate point that points back to the imam. Among geometric shapes — squares, triangles, arcs, and all others — the highest is the circle. The circle is the best

<!-- page 81 -->
and most perfect of all shapes, and it is the foundation from which all other shapes can be derived: the square, the triangle, and the arc alike. When a circle is divided, a square appears and arcs remain at the edges; if we extend horizontal and vertical lines, four triangles emerge. All other shapes are derived from the circle, and all shapes are contained within it. The relationship of shapes to one another mirrors the world and creation. Among shapes, the circle serves as a proof and symbol of the imam, who is the circle of religion (dā'irat al-dīn). All the ḥudūd — the ranks of the hierarchy — fall within his command and prohibition and derive their benefit from him. The circle has no need of any other shape, while every other shape needs it; similarly, the imam has no need of any other rank in the hierarchy in his age and time, while all ranks need him, and he encompasses them all.

[53] We also say that the origin and source of numbers is the number one. It is the most perfect of numbers, independent of two and everything above it up to a thousand, while the thousand and everything below it depends on the number one. The imam is uniquely one in his age and time: all the ranks of the hierarchy need him while he needs none of them. Some people may suppose that the number one points to the Creator of all creatures, but this is mistaken, because the number one is the cause of mathematics and the foundation of numbers — two is one plus one, and three is one plus one plus one, and so on. Rather, the one truly signifies the imam in his rank and station, unique and unequalled. No other creature in his age and time shares his position.

[54] We also say that the dot — which mathematicians describe as having no parts and as the origin of the line, the surface, and the body — is a symbol of the imam, whose rank no one can attain and who cannot be truly known. No one can adequately describe his attributes; people can only attest to their own inability to do so.

[55] Further, we say that among all the days Saturday holds the greatest distinction, and among all the months Ramadan is the finest. Within each day, there is one hour in which supplication (du'ā') is answered. Every genus and species in the world — whether created or posited — has a peak and summit in nobility and merit that none other can reach, and this points to the imam, as we have already explained. Were we to mention everything that deserves mention in this chapter, this treatise would far exceed its proper scope. What we have already presented should be sufficient for those who reflect carefully, enabling them to draw analogical conclusions from the principles and branches we have outlined. We will, however, mention a few further points in brief (nuktatan 'alā al-ikhtiṣār) by drawing on the obligatory religious duties (farā'id) and their structure.

[56] We also say that the ritual prayer — its arrangement, its prescribed standing, its direction toward the qibla, and the standing of the prayer leader (imam) at the head of the rows — constitutes a powerful indication of the imamate. The Prophet, may God bless him, first commanded the Muslims to face the qibla. The word qibla is derived from qabala, meaning "face to face": whoever faces the qibla turns his face directly toward it, and that direction is the imam. It is said that to be before (amāma) something means to stand in front of it and thus to precede it — the opposite of being behind or after it. The word imām shares this root, and the leader of the prayer is called imam precisely because he precedes the worshippers. The directive toward the qibla is thus a directive toward the imam, and the Prophet commanded people to follow and obey him in all forms of worship and to accept his direction. When a person faces forward during prayer, he is facing the qibla — and this mirrors, in the context of all his worship and obedience, his orientation toward the imam. When an individual prays with his imam — as the Prophet stated, a congregational prayer is equivalent to

<!-- page 82 -->
twenty-seven prayers performed alone — its merit stems precisely from the fact that it is performed with the imam, facing him, following him, and being led by him in both intention and action. This indicates that all of obedience consists in being led by the supreme imam (al-imām al-a'ẓam) — guided by him, following him, and directed by him — so that obedience itself attains excellence. The Prophet, may God bless him, also commanded that worshippers align themselves in rows, so that there would be no disparity among them in their standing; this indicates that the hearts of those guided and led by the imam must not differ from one another, and neither should their actions or their speech.

[57] The Prophet, may God bless him, commanded worshippers to follow the prayer imam even when the recitation, the rising, or the prostration becomes prolonged, and never to act contrary to him in gesture or intention. Indeed, even if a worshipper is in agreement with the imam's physical action but not his intention, that prayer is not accepted. And if one who is led is negligent, the imam compensates for that negligence. All of this makes clear that if obedience to a partial (juz'ī) imam — one who is not an imam in the absolute sense — is made obligatory, and the reward of that worship is multiplied, then what must be the case regarding obedience to the supreme (a'ẓam) imam, who leads in all of revealed law (sharī'a) and esoteric interpretation (ta'wīl)? Moreover, if it is impermissible to oppose the prayer imam in any act of worship, how can it be permissible to oppose the supreme imam in his commands and prohibitions governing a believer's entire life? The believer remains perpetually under the care of the da'wa in his prayer as long as he is included within the allegiance (walāya) of the imam. If he opposes the imam in action, intention, or decision, he will have annulled his prayers and departed from the norm of the worshipper — just as when a worshipper resolves to oppose the prayer imam, his bowing, prostration, standing, and sitting are rendered worthless, his action is futile, and he must restart the prayer. How much more vital, then, is obedience to the supreme imam, which God has made absolutely obligatory? The Prophet said: "The imam is the guarantor for those who follow him" —

<!-- page 83 -->
and this certainly does not refer only to the imam of the prayer.


<!-- page 84 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
67
prayer, for he requires someone who can vouch for him. The reference is, in fact, to the supreme imam, peace be upon him, who guarantees the purification of those he leads — freeing them from doubt, suspicion, confusion, and forgetfulness — and guarantees their salvation as long as they are guided by him and follow him. The Prophet indicated the position of the imam as preceding everyone and commanded them not to compete with the imam in the recitation of the Qur'an, saying: 'When he prostrates you should prostrate, when he bows you bow, when he recites you listen, and when he says the salutations (taslīm) then you should say it too.'

[58] All of this points to obedience to the imams, peace be upon them, and to their exaltation in all matters — to the permanence of their authority in their own time, the necessity of obeying them, and the submission (taslīm) of command (al-amr) at the close of each imam's affair to whoever he designates. Even if there were no proof for the imam and for obedience to him in the shari'a other than what is found in the prayer, that alone would be sufficient. The Prophet explained that the imam absolves worshippers of their negligence in prayer — meaning that the imam covers their lapses and seeks forgiveness on their behalf — just as God, the Exalted, said: 'If only when they wronged themselves, they had come to you seeking forgiveness of God and the Prophet, they would have surely found God Most Forgiving and Merciful.'54

If the imam were to withhold forgiveness and the acceptance of repentance from this community (umma), they would surely perish, for they stand in the greatest need of forgiveness. What endures, in every age and time, is this: whoever comes to the imam, the imam seeks forgiveness for him, and God forgives him, just as He did for those earlier people.

[59] We also say that the Friday prayer is not valid without the sermon (khutba) delivered from the pulpits (manābir). People pray

although there the reference is to the leader of prayer, which our author seems to
refute.
54. Qur'an, 4: 64. The Shi'a relate this verse to the concept of shafa'a in
the doctrine of imama. See A.J. Wensinck [D. Gimaret] 'Shafa'a', in EI2, vol. 9,
pp. 177-179 and Meir M. Bar-Asher, Scripture and Exegesis in Early Imami Shiism
(Leiden, 1999), pp. 180-189.

<!-- page 85 -->
68
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
behind the imam so that they understand that obedience to the imam is not lifted from any created being anywhere. Furthermore, the pilgrimage in its entirety is a proof for the affirmation of the imamate. The pilgrimage begins with an intention, and an intention must be directed toward something known, so that when the intention is made with full knowledge (ma'rifa) it becomes a clear and purposeful act requiring no further explanation — which is itself an indication of the intention directed toward the imam.

The Ka'ba, in some esoteric interpretations, is a proof for the imam. Its placement in a desert devoid of civilization, accessible only with great difficulty, in a location without planting or trees and stripped of worldly benefit, is arranged so that worshippers may understand that what it symbolizes (mamthul) can likewise be reached only through great effort, hardship, and injury from enemies. They should also understand that turning toward the imam and seeking him must not be for worldly purposes — out of greed, pleasure, or worldly prestige — but that visiting the imam must arise from pure faith alone. If a person faces hardship, weariness, attacks from enemies, or fatigue, his heart must not falter; he must endure with patience. He will then see and recognize all of this in its symbol (mathal), which is the pilgrimage. Its circumambulation, the touching and receiving of it (istilām), the pointing toward it, the running, the casting of stones, the wearing of the white garments (ihrām), and the giving up of perfume and sexual relations — all of this points to the affirmation of the imamate and obedience to him under every condition, to the seeking of guidance and the protection of one's soul, and to the discarding of reprehensible doctrines and corrupt beliefs.

[60] We also say that among the most emphatic proofs for the necessity of the imamate is the obligation of alms (zakāt) — in its religious duties, its safekeeping, and its distribution. God, the Exalted, commanded the Prophet to take almsgiving (sadaqa) from the believers when He said: 'Take alms (sadaqa) from their wealth, purify them and cleanse them through it and pray for them. Your prayers will indeed bring them tranquillity.'55

55. Qur'an, 9: 103. Both zakāt and sadaqa are used for alms, although it is
zakāt that is paired with salāt (prayer) in the Qur'an. Zakāt is obligatory alms,
while sadaqa is broader and usually refers to voluntary almsgiving. They are often
used synonymously.

<!-- page 86 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
69
The Prophet then explained the obligations of zakāt, its amounts, and how it should be collected. He himself collected it until the community understood both how to give it and how to receive it. Whoever subsequently sat in place of the imams also collected zakāt, claiming that the collection of alms belonged to him and citing this verse and the practice of God's Messenger, may God bless him, as his justification. He waged war against the Banu Hanīfa for that reason and killed them.56 Those who sat in place of the imams after him likewise collected alms, until this matter eventually reached their own leaders, who took offence at it, kept the alms money for themselves, and prevented it from being collected on behalf of the imam.

Is it any wonder, then, to see the condition of this community? They affirm that the verse in question abrogates (nāsikh) in both ruling and recitation, yet it is not abrogated (mansūkh), and that the Prophetic sunna continues to require the collection of alms. How, then, did they permit themselves to alter the ruling of the verse and of God's command, and to change the sunna of the Prophet and the sunna of those whose imamate they claim? The only motivation for making that change was their denial of the imams' rightful status and their rejection of the imamate within the Prophet's family (ahl al-bayt), may God bless him and his family. God, the Exalted, had made clear through His rulings on alms (zakāt) and the manner of collecting them the position of the imamate and the imams — forbidding sadaqa to the Prophet so that no one could accuse him of collecting it for personal gain, and likewise forbidding it to the Prophet's family so that they would recognize that the imam among them is the one who occupies the Prophet's position, may God bless him and his family, in collecting alms, and that they — the imams — are fully trustworthy in this regard.57

[61] Had people been entrusted to discharge the duty of almsgiving and its distribution themselves, God would have commanded them to do so, just as He commanded them with regard to prayer, fasting, and other obligatory duties. But He did not order them to distribute the sadaqa; He commanded the Prophet,

56. The author is referring to Abu Bakr and the ridda wars undertaken
during his time. Banu Hanīfa was the tribe of Khawla, another wife of 'Alī and
mother of Muhammad al-Hanafiyya.
57. See note below in paragraph [63].

<!-- page 87 -->
70
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
may God bless him, to do so. If it had been permissible for people to distribute it themselves, that would have been permissible during the Prophet's own time, when people were far more steadfast in their religion, more certain in their faith, and of greater integrity. How is it, then, that later generations have made themselves trustees of almsgiving? And why is it permissible for them that prayer — which is more meritorious than almsgiving — be performed behind their imams and other duties likewise, yet it is not acceptable to give their imams the alms? Why, further, are they considered trustworthy in matters of bloodshed and sexual offences and in all other aspects of property, yet not reliable or trustworthy when it comes to alms?

If people were truthful in what they say about their imams being untrustworthy with regard to alms, then those imams are not imams at all but embezzlers. And if they are embezzlers, it is not permissible to pray behind them, nor to conduct jihād or pilgrimage under them, nor to accept their rulings on sexual offences, bloodshed, or property. If, on the other hand, these people are lying in what they say and claim, then the scholars among them who issue religious opinions (fatwa) in that regard are liars as well, since they are the ones who bear witness to the leadership of those imams — and the testimony of liars is not acceptable.

God, the Exalted, also commanded the Prophet, may God bless him and his family, to purify the believers and pray for them when He said: '... purify them and pray for them; your prayers will indeed bring them tranquillity.'58 He thus made the imam's purification of them and his prayer upon them a source of their tranquillity, and that prayer was the recompense for the alms He took from them.

If God had granted this recompense to those present in the time of the Prophet, may God bless him, and withheld it from those who came after — even though the later generations are more in need of it than those who lived in the Prophet's time — that would be an injustice against those who came after that age. God is exalted far above injustice toward His worshippers, and He, may His name be exalted, does not impose an obligation and assign a recompense ('iwadan) for it in this world or the hereafter without making that recompense universal. Rather,

[58. Qur'an, 9: 103.

<!-- page 88 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
71
the purification, the prayer, and the tranquillity perpetually belong to whoever comes to the imam and gives alms (zakāt) from his property, whether in public or in private. The imam then purifies him and prays for him. Were the imam necessary for this alone, that would be sufficient. His distribution of alms to those entitled to it is grounded in his knowledge of who may rightfully receive it and who may not, and his awareness of when a category of eligible recipients has no one in it and how the portion intended for them should then be handled. The imam is thus the one who safeguards the treasury of the Muslims, distributing it according to the needs of religion and the demands of the time.

[62] We also say that jihad is one of the pillars of religion and that it is permissible to wage war only under the banner of an imam. No command for waging war can be established except from the imam, for the imam must first protect the treasury (bayt al-māl) of the Muslims — which is under his guarantee — and protect the border towns and the Muslim army, and ransom Muslim prisoners of war from the hands of the infidels. Moreover, an army may only be sent out under a commander appointed by the imam or by someone the imam has himself appointed. The imam is more knowledgeable about the right times for peace, for agreements, and for treaties, and it is he who concludes them. When prisoners of war are taken, he knows whom to pardon, whom to ransom, and whom to put to death. He is better acquainted with the ways of collecting the land tax (kharāj) and revenues so that they serve as a means of benefit and support for the Muslims. The imam also identifies the rebel, and it is by association with him that a party (fi'a) becomes the party of truth. The rebel is the one who departs from obedience to the imam, after which it falls to the imam to decide whether to wage war, make peace, or conclude an armistice. Al-Shafi'i said: 'If it were not for the Commander of the Faithful, 'Alī b. Abī Tālib, peace be upon him, we would not have known how to wage war against rebels, nor would we have been able to distinguish the rebel from the rightful believers.'59

59. al-Shāfi'ī, Kitab al-umm, ed. M. Zuhrī al-Najjār (Beirut, 1393/1974), vol.
4, pp. 223-226.

<!-- page 89 -->
72
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
[63] There is also the fifth (khums) that God assigned specifically to the Prophet's family and to the imams in place of the alms (sadaqa) that He had forbidden them.60 All the benefits of alms and zakāt, along with all the obligations connected to them, are tied to the imam. Beyond this, there are the 'īd feasts and the month of Ramadan, in which the imam preserves order and accepts the testimony of witnesses — regardless of whether opponents hold that fasting and breaking the fast are determined by sighting the moon or by the one appointed by the imam. The two feast prayers and the Friday prayer are not valid in congregation without a sermon (khutba) from the imam or his presence, or from someone he has appointed, even if the congregation is reduced from four to two on account of the imam's sermon.

[64] As for the execution of legal punishments (hudūd): only the imam, or someone the imam designates, may carry them out. The cutting off of hands, flogging, stoning, imprisonment, indemnity for injuries ('arsh al-jirāhāt), and judgement concerning them — all can be carried out only on the orders of the imam. Were these rules the only proof for the imamate in the Messenger's family, that alone would be sufficient. For the opponents themselves agree that whoever has no authority (walāya) cannot be permitted to rule on legal punishments. If a man witnesses another killing someone unjustly, it is not for him to kill that person. If he sees someone stealing, it is not for him to cut off his hand, stone him, or take any other such measure. This principle extends so far that most people agree that even a slave's owner may not carry out legal punishments or chastise him — only the imam may do so.

Legal punishments cannot simply be abolished, for if they were, people would perish and countries would fall to ruin. Yet if everyone were permitted to carry them out, every person with violent or harmful intentions would claim to be administering a legal punishment — asserting that the one he struck or killed had committed some act warranting it. But how can someone who himself incurs twenty legal punishments every day rule on legal punishments and be permitted to carry them out? If that were allowed for such a person, it would be permissible for every oppressor and rebel, and the result would be

60. The Prophet and his family are forbidden to benefit from either zakāt
or sadaqa as these are collected as a means of purification for the believers. As a
compensation, God reserves for the family of the Prophet a fifth (khums) of ghanima
(booty) and fay', property received by Muslims without waging war (Qur'an 8:
41).

<!-- page 90 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
73
the destruction of the people. Rather, when a rebellious party carries out legal punishments and destroys lives, and the imam has power over them, he fines them for whatever harm they caused to the righteous party, thereby preventing the wrongdoers from destroying the people and damaging their property.

Such people then claim that all they did was in accordance with a legal rule enjoined upon them. But God, the Exalted, has addressed this directly: 'Do you command people to do good and forget it yourselves?'61 God, the Exalted, made clear that whoever is himself in need of being commanded to do good and restrained from evil is not in a position to command others to do good or forbid evil. God further explained in another verse that enjoining good and forbidding evil (al-amr bi'l-ma'rūf wa nahy 'an al-munkar) is a duty of the imams, peace be upon them, when He said: 'You are the best community (umma) that has been raised for the people. You enjoin good conduct and forbid evil.'62 They are the equitable leaders (a'imma al-wasat) who stand as witnesses over the people.

How can someone command good if he does not practice it himself? How can he forbid evil if he loves evil and commits it a hundred times a day? How is it permissible for their imams — when they commit daily the very acts that necessitate legal punishment, driven by lust and desire, and when they draw close to themselves those who love such acts and join them in them — to forbid those same acts, while regarding those who perform them as elegant, victorious, agreeable, and refined? Whoever does not join them in drinking and debauchery, they will neither draw near nor employ, for such a person would be disagreeable to them. Is that not itself a lesson to those they lead, and an incitement toward corruption and debauchery?

61. Qur'an, 2: 44.
62. Qur'an, 3: 110.


<!-- page 91 -->
74
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
[65] We also say: it is universally agreed that legal judgement among people in matters of bloodshed, sexual offences, and property may only be exercised by one who knows the relevant cases and legal opinions, who understands legal norms, the Qur'anic revelation and its meaning — including the abrogating and abrogated verses (al-nāsikh wa'l-mansūkh) — and who applies analogical deduction (qiyās) according to its proper conditions, such that he can be trusted in rulings on bloodshed, property, and sexual offences. Does any of the usurpers who claim the imamate actually fulfil these conditions? On the contrary, every one of them depends on those so-called jurists who are said to possess knowledge yet lack its substance. The usurpers shower these men with the petty goods of this world, appoint them governors of provinces, and allow them to preside over public sessions. When an incident arises, they turn to the jurists for answers — and what do they produce? One says, "According to Abū Hanīfa, this man should be executed," while another says, "According to al-Shafi'i, he should not be executed." A third says, "According to Mālik, this woman should be divorced," and a fourth says, "According to Ahmad b. Hanbal, the woman is not forbidden to him."63 The usurpers handed the leadership of religious affairs to these jurists so that the jurists would judge according to their own desires and opinions — and in return, the jurists would leave worldly authority to the usurpers and bear false witness on their behalf. In doing so, the jurists disgraced themselves through their compliance. I do not know which of the two is more astonishing: the one who shamelessly claims the imamate while possessing no knowledge of religion or its legal norms whatsoever — and therefore stands in need of such scholars — or the one who adheres to his leadership and rewards their ignorance and shame. If the jurists were true scholars and authority genuinely rested on their pronouncements, then they would be the imams, not the man who falsely claims the title while being nothing more than an idol set up with no real authority, capable neither of benefit nor harm. Or should the greater wonder be reserved for those who follow such a leader and reward his ignorance? In the end, their imam is the mirror image of his followers, and his followers the mirror image of their imam.
