63. Abū Hanīfa (d. 150/767), Mālik b. Anas (d. 179/796), al-Shafi'ī (d. 204/820), and Ahmad b. Hanbal (d. 241/855) are the founders of the four major Sunni schools of jurisprudence (madhāhib).

<!-- page 92 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
75
[66] If their imams have no right to exercise legal judgement and authority (walāya) — because they lack knowledge, fail to meet its conditions, and possess neither modesty nor integrity — then it must be noted that most adherents of the doctrine of choice (ikhtiyār)64 themselves hold that an imam who is unjust and immoral forfeits the imamate, and according to some, must be removed from it. Likewise, a judge among them who betrays the people's trust should be removed from office, and a legal guardian should similarly lose his guardianship. Some go further, holding that the community is obliged to remove an imam who is unjust and immoral. Al-Shafi'ī said: "A sinner has no legal guardianship; if he had a daughter, he would have no right to give her in marriage — that right would pass to the judge, not to him."65 Furthermore, according to all these jurist-imams, the testimony of a sinner is inadmissible. Thus, by agreement (ittifāq) and consensus (ijmā'), their imams are not among those qualified to exercise legal reasoning (ijtihād) in religion, nor to give their daughters in marriage, nor to have their testimony accepted, nor to carry out legal punishments.

These men have been notorious and infamous offenders, and those who came after them followed the same path. How could anyone possessing such attributes be the Commander of the Faithful, the vicegerent (khalīfa) of the Lord of the worlds, and an imam whose obedience is obligatory? If they do not hold the right of guardianship, legal judgement, or testimony, how can those they appoint exercise guardianship, and how can testimony be accepted from those they endorse? How can their recommendation of someone as fit for judgement, witnessing, and guardianship carry any weight at all? Is there anything in this world more remarkable than these people — the contradictions in their rulings, the principles of their legal school (madhhab), and their

64. The doctrine of ikhtiyār, or choice, is the Sunni position on how the imam is determined, as opposed to the Shi'a position that the imam is appointed by divine designation (naşş), which is hereditary. See chapter 4 of Lalani, Early Shi'i Thought, for al-Bāqir's views on this.

65. al-Shafi'ī, Kitāb al-umm, vol. 4, p. 225.

<!-- page 93 -->
76
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
doctrine? And is there anyone more infamous than their imam and the people he leads?

[67] Were we to enumerate every contradiction and disgrace embedded in their principles, this treatise would need to be extended far beyond its intended scope. What we have already said constitutes a sufficient refutation of their claim to the imamate and a clear demonstration that everything they have done and continue to do amounts to falsehood, injustice, and transgression. That alone is shame and ignominy enough. If their imamate is false, as we have shown, and if it is true that the imamate is one of the foundations of religion (uşūl al-dīn) — an inevitable necessity to which all revealed laws and norms are bound — then the imamate truly belongs to the pure imams from among the progeny of Tāhā and Yāsīn,66 who are protected from corruption and oppression, lying and transgression, and purified of all filth and blame. What we have set out through rational deduction and the religious law is sufficient.

We shall now turn to establishing the necessity of the imamate on the basis of past prophetic cycles, and to demonstrating that there has never been an age or period bereft of a true imam whose obedience is obligatory.

Section Seven

[68] We say that if it were acceptable for there to be creatures on earth without an imam or caliph, this should have been acceptable in the time of Adam, peace be upon him, when only angels existed. Yet at that time God, the Exalted, said: "Indeed, I am placing a vicegerent on earth. They said: 'Are You going to create one that will corrupt and shed blood on it while we glorify Your praises and regard You holy?' He said: 'Surely, I know what you know not.'"67 God, may He be glorified, made plain in that passage that there would never be a time when the earth is without an imam — even if all its inhabitants were impeccable and saintly — so that they would recognise how indispensable the imam is even for them, given their ignorance, while most people are in fact corrupt. God, the Exalted, also made clear in that passage that if it were acceptable for the imam to be chosen (ikhtiyār), the angels would certainly have been granted that choice. God would not have overruled them, for they were immune from base inclinations, false desires, and cravings for worldly power and domination. Since choice was not acceptable for the angels, it is not acceptable for those beneath them. This meaning is implicit in the verse of God, the Exalted, concerning the rights of parents: "Do not even say 'fie' to them or tell them off."68 God thereby prohibited even harsher treatment — striking or abusing them — by implication from the lesser.

66. Two sūras of the Qur'an are named after them: sūra 20, Tā Hā, and sūra 36, Yā Sīn.

67. Qur'an, 2: 30.

68. Qur'an, 17: 23.

God, the Exalted, has indeed recounted the stories of past communities and prophets so that people would recognise that the same pattern would unfold in the cycle of the Prophet Muhammad as it did in earlier cycles. The Prophet said: "My community will experience exactly what happened among the Banū Isrā'īl."69 He also said: "You will follow the footsteps of those before you, step by step in an identical manner (qudhdha bi-qudhdha), to the point that if they entered the hole of a lizard, you would enter it too."70

[69] In the story of Adam there is an indication that the imamate and the caliphate cannot be established by choice or consultation. Had they been permissible by such means, the choice and consultation of the angels would have been the most fitting, since they were immune from jealousy and rebellion. In that story there is also an indication that it is not for people to declare whether a given person is or is not suitable for the imamate, or to prefer one candidate over another. God, the Exalted, informed those who objected to Adam of the error of their position, reproached them, and rebuked them, exposing the falsehood of their words. He preserved this account in the Qur'an so that people would take it as a lesson and

69. See A.J. Wensinck, Concordance et indices de la tradition musulmane (Leiden, 1936–1969), vol. 1, p. 95, citing al-Tirmidhī, tradition 18 in the section on 'īmān'.

70. See al-Muslim b. al-Hajjāj, Şahīh Muslim, Kitāb al-'ilm, tr. A.H. Siddiqi (Lahore, 1976), vol. 4, p. 1403. I would like to thank Dr Moojan Momen for this reference.

<!-- page 95 -->
78
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
would not dispute the imamate or contest the one whom God and His Messenger chose for it, as they had done. In the appointment of Adam's son to the imamate there is a further indication that the world can never be without an imam. All people accept the imams of Adam's period yet deny them in the period of Muhammad, may God bless him, even though God, the Almighty and Glorious, has declared: "Such was Our way (sunna) with the messengers We sent before you, and you will find no change in Our way."71 He also said: "You will find no change whatsoever in Our way (sunna)."72 What is intended here is the sunna of the imamate, not the sunna of ritual practices such as prayer, fasting, and the like — for the Prophet, may God bless him, did indeed alter the sunna of all previous prophets regarding acts of worship ('ibādāt), but he did not alter the sunna of the imamate.

[70] We also say that when Noah called people to the ark out of compassion, he knew full well that those who stayed behind would perish and drown — by their own choice and by their conviction that their welfare lay elsewhere. The pattern is clear: every people summoned by their prophet and imam to a matter, who then refused and rejected it, were destroyed on account of that refusal. The ark was none other than the imam himself, and Noah's call to the people was a call to the imam, just as the Prophet said: "The likeness of my family (ahl al-bayt) among you is like Noah's ark: whoever boards it will be saved, and whoever stays behind will be drowned."73

Noah, peace be upon him, appointed his son Shem (Sām) to the legateeship and the imamate by designation (naşş) and appointment (tawqīf), and thereafter the line continued, one after another, until the time of Abraham, peace be upon him. Those who oppose us accept the imams of Noah's cycle yet deny them in the family of

71. Qur'an, 17: 77.

72. Qur'an, 33: 62.

73. The story of Noah's ark appears in the Qur'an in several places, including 29: 15. References to this tradition in Sunni sources can be found in Wensinck, Concordance, under th-q-l. See also al-Qādī al-Nu'mān, Da'ā'im al-Islām, ed. A.A.A. Fyzee (Cairo, 1951–1961), revised and annotated by Ismail K. Poonawala as The Pillars of Islam (New Delhi, 2002–2004), vol. 1, pp. 37 and 100.

<!-- page 96 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
79
Muhammad, even though the sunna has not been replaced or altered and people cannot do without an imam.

[71] When Abraham, peace be upon him, appeared, he commanded the glorification of fire. If what has been said about this is correct, then it was a symbolic indication of the glorification of the light of the imamate (nūr al-imāma), as we have already mentioned.74 Then Abraham and Ismā'īl, peace be upon them, built the House — the Ka'ba — and commanded the people to venerate it, which was itself an indication that the imamate was to reside within Abraham's household and lineage. He urged the people to honour the imamate and to venerate and obey the imams of his family.

[72] When Moses, peace be upon him, arose, he directed the people to face toward Jerusalem in prayer. Since Moses had no children or family in whom the imamate could continue, he pointed toward Jerusalem (bayt al-muqaddas), meaning: whoever among the Banū Isrā'īl possesses the sanctified spirit is your imam and your direction of prayer (qiblatakum). Then Jesus, peace be upon him, appeared. He had neither family nor children, and it is reported that he directed the people to face toward the east. If this is true, his command to face east meant turning toward the place from which the light of the imamate rises — that is, whoever radiates that light is your imam and your direction of prayer.

When the Prophet Muhammad, the seal of the prophets, may God bless him and his family, appeared, he directed people to turn from Jerusalem toward the Ka'ba as the qibla of the Muslims. The Ka'ba had been built by Abraham and Ismā'īl, peace be upon them. In giving this command, the Prophet indicated that the direction had returned to the house of Abraham — that is, the family of Ismā'īl — and that the imamate had therefore returned to the children of Ismā'īl, who are the children of the Prophet Muhammad, may God bless him. He commanded the people to venerate the House and perform the pilgrimage to it — something no previous prophet had commanded — as a way of impressing upon the people the supreme excellence of the imams from the family of Muhammad above all other imams in past cycles. Is there anyone in this world more iniquitous than this community, which accepted the

74. See the note on this in paragraph [17] above.

<!-- page 97 -->
80
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
imams of every prophetic period, affirmed that the world cannot be without an imam, and affirmed that there was never an imam in previous cycles who came to power by choice (ikhtiyār) — yet now denies the imamate of Muhammad's family, rejects designation (naşş), and calls for choice instead? God, the Exalted, has said: "Your Lord creates what He wills and chooses what is good for them."75 If the imamate were determined by choice, every people would simply choose whomever they wished. The elders have treated the debate between designation and choice at length. We have stated our intention to present what the elders have not addressed in the books on affirming the imamate. What we have said here is sufficient. We shall now establish that the imam must be the most excellent, the most knowledgeable, and the noblest of mankind.

[73] We say that the virtues God, the Exalted, has mentioned and by which He has distinguished some people above others are of three kinds (ashyā'):76 knowledge, jihād, and piety. God, the Exalted, said: "God has preferred those who strive and struggle over those who sit back by a great reward in degrees of rank."77 He also said: "'Are those who know and those who do not, equal?'"78 And: "Those who have been given knowledge have degrees of rank."79 And: "The most noble among you in the presence of God is the most pious among you."80 As for jihād, both the elite (khāşş) and ordinary Muslims ('āmm) know well that these men never personally fought in a single battle. The military exploits of the Commander of the Faithful, 'Alī, are well known and celebrated. Their only legacy in holy war was their flight at the battle of Hunayn, when 'Umar disobeyed the Prophet's explicit order and recommendation to hold the mountain pass, earning the censure of the Muslims, while the Prophet and 'Alī continued to fight and cut down the enemy until they were routed.81

75. Qur'an, 28: 68.

76. lit. "things."

77. Qur'an, 4: 95–96.

78. Qur'an, 39: 9.

79. Qur'an, 58: 11.

80. Qur'an, 49: 13.

81. See the battle of Hunayn described in Kitāb al-jihād in al-Nu'mān,


<!-- page 98 -->
As for piety (taqwā): where there is no knowledge, there can be no piety, for piety comes through knowledge — through knowing what belongs to oneself and what belongs to others. It has been reported from Muhammad b. al-Hasan, Abū Hanīfa's companion, that a God-fearing man (muhtasabin) once asked him to compile a book on asceticism. He compiled instead a book on trade, and when asked to explain, he replied: 'Whoever has not mastered jurisprudence — buying and selling — how can he consume only what is lawful? And whoever does not consume only what is lawful has no asceticism.' It follows, therefore, from the Qur'anic verses we have cited, that the imamate rightly belonged to the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, for he had precedence (sābiq) in every virtue: he was the most knowledgeable, the most pious, and the one who fought most in the cause of Islam.

[74] We further maintain that those who ruled did not claim excellence, knowledge, or piety for themselves — it was their companions who made those claims on their behalf. Indeed, Abu Bakr was the first to ascend the pulpit and declare: 'You have appointed me, although I am not the best among you.'82 If he was speaking the truth, then those who claim superior excellence for him are liars. If he was lying, then a liar cannot be an imam. If it is said that he spoke out of humility, we answer that humility has its proper time and place. The Prophet himself, may God bless him, would openly speak of the rank and status with which God had singled him out, and that was not arrogance. He said: 'I am the master of the Arabs, without boasting'; 'I am the master of humanity, without boasting'; and 'I am the most eloquent of the Arabs, without boasting.'83

<!-- page 99 -->
The Commander of the Faithful, 'Alī b. Abī Tālib, peace be upon him, said: 'Ask me before you lose me.'84 He also said: 'If a cushion were folded for me to sit upon, I would pass judgement on the followers of the Torah according to the Torah, on the followers of the Bible according to the Bible, and on the followers of the Furqan (Qur'an) according to the Furqan.'85 Many such examples could be cited, yet none of them constitutes arrogance or negates humility. Abu Bakr's words — 'I have been put in charge of you although I am not the best among you' — indicate that neither God nor His Messenger had granted him preference or designated him, whether by ordering him to lead the prayer or in any other way. He confirmed about himself

<!-- page 100 -->
all that was to be expected of him, and so he added: 'Put me right.' Correction of that kind is only necessary in the case of grave and serious sins, since lesser ones can be cancelled by seeking forgiveness.

[75] When 'Umar ascended the pulpit after Abu Bakr, he declared: 'The oath (bay'a) to Abu Bakr was a falta — a hasty, unconsidered decision — but God has averted its evil from us. Whoever repeats the like of it, kill him.'86 In saying this, he himself demolished the claim of the literalists that the Prophet's instruction to have Abu Bakr lead the prayer made his imamate obligatory and served as a designation for it.87 Furthermore, his statement refutes what they cite in the traditions, such as: 'Follow the two who come after me, Abu Bakr and 'Umar.'

[79] We shall now mention some of the merits of the lord of the age and time (walī al-'asr wa zaman), the Commander of the Faithful (al-Hākim) — though someone of my standing could never attain the degree that would permit a true account of his merits, just as God the Exalted has said: 'They have not valued God according to His true value.'88 Yet I will speak according to my limited knowledge and capacity, and the Commander of the Faithful is most worthy to accept the excuse of his servant. God the Exalted, in the fullness of His bounty toward His servants, called upon them to give thanks for His blessings; and when they proved unable to render thanks commensurate with those blessings,

<!-- page 101 -->
He forgave them and was content with their saying 'al-hamdulillah' (praise be to God).89 This was a lesson for His creation: whenever gratitude to a benefactor is obligatory yet one cannot express it in full proportion to the bounty received, one should give thanks to the best of one's ability and not abandon gratitude altogether merely because one cannot give him his due.

We say further that God the Exalted has made the earth radiant with His light, as He promised and declared: 'And the earth shines by the light of her Lord ...'90 — meaning that the earth of outward reality shines through the justice of the Commander of the Faithful, and the earth of inner meaning shines through knowledge and interpretation.

[80] In every species, genus, root, branch, and law, there is a peak and summit of nobility, merit, rank, degree, and station that nothing else can reach. It is therefore necessary that the imams, peace be upon them, likewise possess a peak, a summit, and an utmost degree that no one before them has attained. That peak and utmost degree is the Commander of the Faithful ['Alī]. He is the summit of all summits, the furthest limit beyond which there is no other limit. Just as the wisdom of the world — and of the animals and plants within it — came to full expression in the emergence of human beings, and the well-being of all creation was made complete through humanity, and the benefit of all created things returns ultimately to mankind; and just as it is through the imams, peace be upon them, that the merits of humanity have been perfected and wisdom has become manifest in creation, so that the benefit of all human beings flows back to the imams: in the same way, through the Commander of the Faithful, all the affairs (umūr) of the prophets, legatees, and imams reached their completion, their order (amr) was organized, and perfect wisdom became manifest in their dispensation, their laws, and their esoteric interpretations. He, peace be upon him, is like the head, and they are like the body. They are the body, while the Commander of the Faithful is the spirit — and no body is complete without the spirit. The Commander of the Faithful is like the heart, while all those who

<!-- page 102 -->
have passed away are like the limbs. It is through the union of spirit and life in the heart that all the limbs receive life and sustenance. Every nail, every hair, every limb receives its share of nourishment and vitality by means of the heart. This is the sensory knowledge — the recompense of action — that comes to the embryo in its womb (dār 'amalihi)91 through the six stages of development from sperm to fully formed creation.

Likewise, it is through the Commander of the Faithful that people attain recompense and the fullness of life in the seventh stage. He, peace be upon him, is the one who awakens in every soul the desire to draw near to him, and he is the one through whom God has promised creation the fulfilment of its reward on His day. As God said of the Judgement: '... And you will be paid only that which you have earned fairly on the Day of Resurrection. Whoever is removed from the Fire and allowed to enter Paradise will indeed be triumphant.'92 God commands creation to believe in Him and in His Day of Judgement when He says: '... They believe in God and the Last Day ...'93

[81] Just as all of humanity exists as the final realm of nature (mawalid) in the world — having absorbed everything that existed in the previous realms and surpassing it in ways those realms could not share — and just as the imams gathered within themselves all the merits that existed in humanity, being their peak, their summit, and their quintessence, capable of receiving what lay beyond ordinary human capacity, so that the benefit of all who preceded them flows to the imams: in the same way, the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, gathers within himself all the merits of those who came before him, and all their benefit and knowledge converges in him, surpassing what came before many times over. Just as the Prophet's power and merit derived from gathering within himself the merits of those in previous cycles — all their knowledge and more — so too the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, gathers within himself the merits of all the prophets, imams, and sages who preceded him in previous cycles, and exceeds them

<!-- page 103 -->
in matters beyond their capacity. Had he not arisen in this station, the perfection of wisdom in their arising, peace be upon them, would not have been achieved. The Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, is therefore the totality of everything in the worlds today — spiritual, physical, and conventional (wad'i). It is for his sake that these worlds were created, and all of them are his agents and propagators until His Day of Judgement. He is the one who arose in the world to perfect the Creator's vicegerency among humanity in the universe. We have indeed said that he is the perfector (mutimm) of all those who have passed away.

Some may object that this description applies to the final qā'im, and that the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, is not the final one. We reply that the Commander of the Faithful [al-Hākim], peace be upon him, is the qā'im of his own time and age, and the qā'im of all those who preceded him — the perfector of them all. He is the sixth through whom the command of the seventh will be perfected.94 He will complete what the seventh requires, just as the Prophet, may God bless him, established all that the qā'im would need in order to become the perfector of what the Prophet had established and arranged. The Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, is the ninth in a different reckoning.95 The command of the seventh [al-Mu'izz],96 who has passed away, is perfected through the ninth [al-Hākim], just as the seven members of the human body reach their perfection, order, and function through the nine — that is, the four natures and the five senses — and just as the perfection of the human creature is achieved in nine months. This reckoning is founded on the number nine.97 Life in the hereafter is grounded in four spiritual


<!-- page 104 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
87
natures and five spiritual senses, as God said: 'Indeed, the abode of
the hereafter — that is life, if only they knew.'98

[82] The Commander of the Faithful [al-Hakim], peace be upon
him, is the second by a different calculation. The order at the
beginning of each cycle is perfected by the second. He is also the
sixth, through whose sword God will conquer the whole world, just
as He conquered through the Prophet — may God bless him — the
sixth one from Adam. The Commander of the Faithful, peace be
upon him, arose in the world to perfect the vicegerency (khilafa) of
the Creator of the universe among His creatures. He deals with
creation in all circumstances in the same way that God, the Exalted,
deals with His creatures — with regard to their constitution, their
sustenance, and the abundance of His mercy toward them. Each of
those who arose before him fulfilled only a small portion of this, but
the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, fulfilled the
whole of it. A vicegerent is one who, over those entrusted to him,
carries out everything that the one who appointed him would do.

Indeed, Adam — like all other vicegerents — was called 'vicegerent'
(khalifa) because he was the first to undertake the vicegerency.
Those who came after him fulfilled some of its conditions and so
were called by that name, for whoever discharges part of a duty is
known by its name. The Commander of the Faithful, however,
performs both the worldly and the religious vicegerency in full
reality. When the Prophet, may God bless him, said, 'The ruler is
the shadow of God on earth,'99 he meant none other than the
Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him — for a shadow
perfectly imitates what casts it in every detail. No one deals with
creation as God, the Exalted, does, except the Commander of the
Faithful, peace be upon him. The Prophet, may God bless him, also
said: 'Indeed God, the Exalted,

<!-- page 105 -->
88
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
establishes (naza'a) proof through the ruler beyond what He
establishes through the Qur'an,' and the Commander of the
Faithful, peace be upon him, is the one through whom God
establishes what He does not establish through the Qur'an.

[83] The philosophers have said that philosophy is the pursuit of
resemblance to God (al-tashabbuh bi'l-bāri'), glory be to Him,
according to human capacity. To seek resemblance to the Creator,
glory be to Him, means dealing with mankind as God, may His
mention be exalted, deals with them — interacting with them, and
with oneself, in the manner that God acts with both humanity and
the individual, and in accordance with their nature. The
philosophers have borrowed this idea from the 'people of the truth'
(ahl al-haqīqa),100 yet none of them have been able to realise its
true meaning — except the Commander of the Faithful. Indeed,
everything he does with humankind mirrors God's own dealings
with them in the order of the world, in sustenance, and in all
related matters. We shall now mention a small portion of what is
appropriate to the time in which we live.

[84] Let us begin by saying that the compassion, mercy, bounty,
and forgiveness of the Commander of the Faithful [al-Hakim] —
together with his forbearance toward people's failings and
disobedience — are beyond my power to describe. All of it follows
God's own conduct toward humanity, in obedience to God's
command and in emulation of His character.

God in His bounty bestows blessings upon people whether they
are pious or impious, believers or unbelievers. Had He granted
worldly benefit101 to believers alone and withheld it from
unbelievers, people's faith would have been motivated by worldly
gain rather than by God or His religion. Conversely, had He denied
it to all believers and given it only to unbelievers, most people's
belief (īmān) would surely have reverted to unbelief (kufr). He
therefore gave it to some unbelievers and withheld it from others,
just as He gave it to some believers and withheld it from others —
giving it to each at certain times and withholding it at others.
Likewise, the Commander of the Faithful

<!-- page 106 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
89
bestows worldly benefit on Muslims and unbelievers alike, and
withholds it from them too; he bestows it on believers and
hypocrites alike, and withholds it from them too. He gave to all of
them at some times and withheld at others. Had he given everything
to the believers, their faith would largely have been for the sake of
this world; but had he withheld from all believers at all times, they
would surely have grown despondent and their good intentions
would have weakened. Therefore, the Commander of the Faithful
bestowed blessings on the believers for a time such as no one else
had ever bestowed, and he also withheld from them for a time, for
their own benefit, lest they become like those of whom God said:
'Nay, indeed man certainly becomes rebellious, considering himself
self-sufficient.'102

God gave worldly goods to the pious and the impious alike, so
that the pious would not perform good deeds merely for the sake of
this world, nor refrain from immorality merely for worldly reasons,
nor hold back from disobedience only for worldly benefit — which
would deprive them of their reward in the hereafter. Rather, their
conduct should be purely for the sake of religion and God. Likewise,
the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, gives worldly
benefits to the righteous and the immoral alike.

[85] God, the Exalted, has given this temporal world to those who
obey and those who disobey, out of His wisdom, so that obedience
should not be for the sake of the world, nor restraint from
disobedience because of it; rather, both should be purely for the
sake of God. Similarly, the Commander of the Faithful gives to the
obedient and the disobedient alike, following the actions of God,
the Exalted, so that humanity's obedience to him is not for worldly
advantage and the reward of the hereafter is not forfeited.

God gives worldly goods to the worshipper and the non-worshipper
alike, lest people's worship be motivated by worldly gain. Similarly,
the Commander of the Faithful looks upon his people not focusing
on their faults and disobedience, but regarding them with a merciful
eye. God, the Exalted, has given worldly benefit to the simpleton
and the ignorant, and has given it to the learned and the intelligent,
while also withholding it from

<!-- page 107 -->
90
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
both. Likewise, the Commander of the Faithful gives to the
simpleton and the ignorant, the learned and the intelligent alike,
out of his wisdom and virtue. Were he to give only to the learned
and the intelligent and withhold from all others, people would
pursue knowledge solely for the sake of this world, and their use of
reason and acquisition of virtues would equally be for this world
alone, leaving the ignorant and the simpleton in despair. But God
has equally given the world to the simpleton and the ignorant.

He has also given worldly benefit to the wrongdoer and the just,
to the generous and the miser, to the noble and the depraved and
the lowly, to those possessing every virtue and those lacking it —
giving to all of them equally out of His wisdom, mercy, and grace.
Likewise, the Commander of the Faithful treats all of them equally,
following the actions of God, the Exalted, and His mercy upon His
creatures, so that everyone may receive their fair share of His bounty
and kindness.

[86] Furthermore, God, the Exalted, has concealed the workings
of the world and the means by which His creatures may benefit
from it, hiding from mankind both the methods and the actions
required to acquire worldly benefits. Thus one person becomes
wealthy while another has nothing to eat;103 one grows rich through
trade and commerce while another goes bankrupt; one prospers
from agriculture while another loses even his seeds; and one
becomes self-sufficient through hunting while another is
impoverished. All of this reflects God's surpassing wisdom, lest a
single path to worldly gain become universally known while all
other paths become futile, bringing disorder upon creation and
corrupting the world. The Commander of the Faithful's dealings
with mankind in this world follow the same pattern. He conceals
the means to worldly goods, so that one person seeks them by a
particular means and receives them, while another who seeks them
by the very same means is denied. Were people to discover a
definite method for obtaining worldly goods from the Commander
of the Faithful, all other means would certainly become futile. All
those who lacked that one means would despair of his mercy, be
left deprived, and there would be no merit in any of it.

<!-- page 108 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
91

[87] The Commander of the Faithful's conduct in drawing people
close to him or keeping his distance from them follows the same
principle. Were he to push the hypocrites away and draw only the
believers near, people would become believers in order to gain his
favour, not out of genuine religion. And were he to keep his distance
from all believers at all times, they would withdraw from him,
anxiety creeping into their hearts — either from a sense of the
Commander of the Faithful's displeasure or from a fear that some
slip on their part had warranted it. He therefore deals with people
according to what the prevailing times and conditions require —
arousing desire and alarm, drawing near and pushing away, granting
and withholding, punishing and forgiving — all of it following the
actions and character of God, the Exalted, so that people remain
perpetually in a state of both fear and hope: neither feeling so
secure that they grow bold, nor so despairing that they become
despondent. In all of this he emulates God's manner with His
creation. Just as God, may He be glorified, gives abundantly to all,
including those who have no fear of poverty or the exhaustion of
wealth, so too the Commander of the Faithful gives to those who
have no such fear, surpassing the generosity of all other human
beings.

[88] Whoever looks with clear sight at the actions of the
Commander of the Faithful in matters of religion and the world will
find that everything he does with creation — commanding good,
forbidding evil, granting and withholding — runs parallel to the
actions of God, the Exalted. Yet people are baffled by this because
they judge his actions by the standard of ordinary human beings
and of what they have previously heard. They compare the actions
of the Commander of the Faithful with those of other people and
other rulers, against what they have heard, seen, and understood.
Finding it at odds with what they are accustomed to, they fall into
perplexity, confusion, and speculation. If they were instead to view
his actions in relation to God's dealings with creation, everything
would become far clearer. The difficulty arises only because, from
the time of Adam to our own day, no one has ever acted in this
manner. If anyone did act this way at any time, it was only in a small
portion, as is reported in the story of the sage [Khidr]104 with
Moses, peace be upon him.

<!-- page 109 -->
92
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
Moses was not patient with Khidr's actions because he measured
them against his own deeds, knowledge, and understanding. Had
he viewed them in relation to God's actions, the outcome would
certainly have been different. Another example is the reported
judgement of David and God's command in the perplexing and
astonishing story of the owner of the vineyard105 and the owner of
the bull. Similarly, God, the Exalted, has given warning in His
words: 'On the day when the summoner calls them to a painful
end … their eyes will be lowered in humiliation,'106 and He said:
'They were called to prostrate while safe and secure.'107 It is
remarkable that if people cannot endure these worldly actions, how
can they then endure the far greater demands of religious matters
and things more difficult still? Everything found at the two
extremes does not exist at the centre, and what exists at the centre
does not exist at the extremes — yet people judge these matters by
what they observe at the centre while themselves standing at the
extremities. That is precisely why they become confused and
confounded, unable to bear or endure it.

[89] We also observe that the gifts of other monarchs are perceived
by people as being given in return for a service, a relationship, an
obligation, love, need, the pursuit of some benefit, or the averting
of some harm. Such gifts may also arise from other motives that
make them necessary: fear of calamity, hope of good fortune, fear
of someone's censure, the desire for praise or exaltation, the wish
for gratitude or goodwill, payment to an official, or provision of
sustenance. All of this is conducted on the basis of calculation and
assessment. The gifts of the Commander of the Faithful, however,
transcend all of that — for gifts given for any of the reasons cited
above cannot truly be called generosity, virtue, or beneficence.
They amount to either the discharge of a past obligation or an
advance payment toward a future one. Delaying what one owes and
fulfilling rights belatedly is blameworthy and miserly; and advancing
money in expectation of future benefit is not generosity or virtue.
Nor is whatever is given in connection with indebtedness considered
generosity, nobility, or virtue. The gifts of God, glory be to Him,
transcend all of that, for God is under no obligation to give anyone
anything, and He seeks no reward from any of His creatures. In the
same way, the Commander of the Faithful does not give to anyone
for any of the reasons cited, nor does he seek recompense for it.
God, the Exalted, said regarding the Commander of the Faithful,
'Alī b. Abī Ṭālib: 'We do not desire either reward or gratitude from
them.'108 Rather, he bestows upon them spontaneously, out of
generosity, nobility, and virtue, following the actions of God, the
Exalted, with His creation.

Were people to view the actions of the Commander of the Faithful
in this light, they would not fall into confusion. Yet some look at
him with short-sightedness, judging his actions by the standard of
other human beings, while others look at him with exaggeration,
claiming divinity for him.109 The actions of the Commander of the
Faithful surpass both of these positions. He is, rather, the Universal
Vicegerent of God among His worshippers (al-khalīfat al-kullī
lillāh ta'āla 'ala 'ibādihi).

[90] Just as God, the Exalted, has concealed from mankind the
reasons for creating the world while explaining the reasons of
religion and

<!-- page 110 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
93


<!-- page 111 -->
94
DEGREES OF EXCELLENCE
the means of attaining it, He also explained ways of attaining His
pleasure, clarifying it through the words of His Prophet. He gave
knowledge and wisdom to those who deserved it and denied it to
those who did not, and 'He made before them a barrier and behind
them a barrier',110 establishing signposts of guidance and the summons
of the proof and the guide to the bounty of the hereafter. He
disclosed ways of attaining to it and opened its gates, so that whoever
deserves it attains it and he who does not misses it in utmost wisdom.
Likewise, the Commander of the Faithful concealed the means of
attaining worldly fortune through him, giving it to both those who
deserve and those who do not. He explained the means of attaining
religion, knowledge and wisdom, and bestowed them upon those who
deserved it, neither withholding them nor being miserly. He
bountifully bestowed favours upon people, giving more than anyone
could ever give in any time or age. He also withheld favours from
the undeserving, differentiating between the people of truth
(ahl al-haqq) and the people of falsehood (ahl al-bāṭil), following the
ways of God, the Exalted, in governing the world and religion alike,
and in building both this present world and the world to come.

[91] We ask God to bestow upon us gratitude for what He has
blessed us with in words, deeds and intention, and to grant
us success in obedience to the lord of the age and time (wali al-'asr
wa'l-zaman), peace be upon him. May He provide us with the means
of striving on his behalf with our hands, tongues and hearts. May
God open the entire world111 to him and, through his sword, purify
the earth of polytheism (shirk), unbelief (kufr) and hypocrisy (nifāq)
through his bounty and generosity.

This worshipper begs and desires the blessings of the Commander
of the Faithful, peace be upon him, and seeks 'the supreme
command' (al-amr al-ālī) to examine what the servant has gathered
and compiled, to grant him permission for what is sound and
correct, and to draw his attention to any mistakes, so that the servant
may be thankful to him for deepening his insight and certainty, and so

110. Qur'an, 36: 9.
111. lit., 'the east and the west of the world'.

<!-- page 112 -->
AFFIRMING THE IMAMATE
95
that this would become a means of guidance and evidence for those
who seek to be guided, and a proof against those who deny.112

Praise be to God, the praise of the thankful, the mindful and
the pious, and prayers upon His Messenger, our master Muhammad,
and all his progeny.113

112. This suggests that al-Naysābūrī had submitted his work to the Imam
al-Hakim for his blessings as well as for rectifying his mistakes, although it is
difficult to determine from the text itself whether or not any such corrections were
received and incorporated before the work became public.
113. The following is added in manuscript alif: 'God is sufficient for us,
blessed is His wakil, and blessed is the mawlā and blessed is the helper. There is
no power except in God, the Supreme, the Great. This book has been completed
by the help and favour of God and the support of His wali, peace be upon him,
on Friday afternoon, the 27th of Rabī' II, 1134/14 February 1722, May God protect
it. This book on Ithbāt al-imām [imāma] has been completed by the Grace of
God, the Bountiful Ruler (malik al-manān), by the help of His friends, owners of
knowledge and explanation ('ilm wa'l-bayān).'

<!-- page 136 -->
In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful — and by Him we seek help in all matters.

[1] Praise be to God, the Sender of Messengers and Prophets, the Appointer of Imams and Caliphs — a blessing upon the saints and a curse upon the enemies, a source of good for religion and this world, and a foundation for the Hereafter. Ahmad Hamad, who recognized the greatness of these graces and gave thanks for them, turned toward God and hastened to Him. I bear witness that there is no god but God alone — knowing the full meaning of that testimony and fulfilling its obligations diligently. I further testify that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, the most beloved of the Messengers, protected from all sins and errors. May God's blessings upon him be beyond numbering and without end, and upon his Guardian, al-Wafi al-Sadiq, the master of word and truth, and upon the Imams who are the descendants of the martyr — foremost in praise and glorification, in infallibility and recompense — and upon the lord of the age, the supporter of conquest and victory. May God's peace and blessings be upon him in the purest and most abundant measure, and upon the Crown Prince of the Muslims and the Caliph of the Commander of the Faithful, the finest peace that tongues have ever spoken, as long as light and darkness remain distinct.

<!-- page 137 -->

[2] Now, since the Imamate is the axis of religion and its very foundation — around which all matters of religion and this world revolve, and through which the good of the Hereafter and the present life is organized — the affairs of God's servants, the administration of lands, and the acceptance of reward are all ordered within its covenant. Through the Imamate one arrives at knowledge of divine unity and prophethood by way of argument, proof, and signification, and at knowledge of the Shariah, its structure, its interpretation, and its clarification.

[3] We have said that the Imamate is the axis of religion and its foundation, and we have not placed the Message above the Imamate, because in establishing the Imamate the Message is itself established — every person who acknowledged the Prophet acknowledged the truth of the Imam. Moreover, the proof of the Message is needed for those who stand outside the house of Islam, while the proof of the Imamate is needed for those within it — and engaging Muslims, who are closer to the believers, and striving with them through reasoned debate is more urgent than debating those who deny the Messenger and the Message altogether. As for those who remain among the polytheists, the call has already reached them, and the proof has already been set before them; the primary engagement with them today is by the sword. Furthermore, the world is never at any time or in any era without an Imam, whereas a Messenger comes only in certain times and not others. And because the proof of the Imamate is itself the proof of the Message —

<!-- page 138 -->

God Almighty named the Imam a Messenger in His saying: *"And mention in the Book Ishmael — he was true to his promise and he was a messenger"* [Surah 19, Maryam: 54]. And God said to Ibrahim: *"I will make you an Imam for the people"* — and this came after the completion of his prophethood, as God the Almighty said: *"And when Abraham was tried by his Lord with certain words, and he fulfilled them, He said: I will make you an Imam for the people"* [Surah 2, al-Baqarah: 124].

[4] It is narrated from Ja'far al-Sadiq, peace be upon him, that he said: "God took Ibrahim as a prophet, then took him as a close companion, then took him as a messenger, and then said: I have made you an Imam for the people." God also named Adam a caliph, and the title of caliph is one of the titles of the Imams. God named Dawud, peace be upon him, a caliph as well, and he was one of the Imams among the earlier Prophets. God called Muhammad, peace be upon him, and the other Messengers prophets, and addressed them as prophets just as He addressed them as messengers. Moreover, the prophet is the first to ascend

<!-- page 139 -->

to the degree of the Imamate; then, when the time requires him to establish a Shariah, he becomes the seventh Imam — the one in that role who establishes the law. The Imam takes the place of the Prophet in his own time. And before the Prophet established the Shariah, he was himself among the Imams who received the matter from the Imam; and after completing the Shariah, he handed the matter over to the Imam. The Imam exists in the world at all times, and the world is never without him. He preserves the Shariah and its inner truths as we have described. It has thus been established that the orbit of religion turns upon the Imam — even though, when the Imam acts within the law of the Prophet in his role, he does not attain the Prophet's rank and station. The true Shariah neither changes nor is altered except through the Imam, and the deep truths of the Shariah, its interpretation, and its meanings are accessible only from his side.

[5] Many works proving the Imamate have been composed by the elders of the da'wah, drawing on inference and citation from the well-known Quranic verses revealed concerning the Imamate and the Imams, peace be upon them, and from the reports narrated from the Prophet, peace be upon him. The earlier scholars left little for those who came after them, and the later scholars did not merely repeat their meanings in different words — all of this has been of great benefit. We therefore wished to compose a treatise on the proof of the Imamate following the path of testimony and inference from perspectives, souls, and principles,

<!-- page 140 -->

from rational demonstration, necessity, natural disposition and instinct, and from agreement among people of every sect — philosophers, arithmeticians — and then to cite from the obligatory prayers and the Sunnah from an angle that the elders of the da'wah have not previously cited. If it meets the approval of the guardian of the age and time and proves beneficial to his servants, then the credit belongs to him. If there is error in it, that is due to my own incapacity and shortcoming — and that is the first thing I acknowledge.

**Chapter One**

[6] We say that the obligation of the Imamate and the Imams, peace be upon them, is affirmed by natural disposition, innate constitution, instinct, reason, sound governance, circumstances, and rational demonstration. It is obligatory in every law, religion, and creed, and it is likewise obligatory in every sect and religion — though people have differed regarding the Maker after acknowledging this obligation. They ruled according to their own whims, opinions, and the imitation of their forebears and teachers, holding that the Maker to whom instinct and natural constitution point is the essence, or the elements, or the celestial spheres, or the sun, or something else — or they described the Creator as

<!-- page 141 -->

an analogy or as a partner in their rulings, speaking without knowledge. Similarly, regarding the obligation of the Imamate, they fell into dispute even after they had committed themselves to an Imam. Some of them said: the Imam whom reason and necessity require is simply the wise man of each age — as philosophers do, calling their teachers imams, sages, heads of scholars, world statesmen, and master teachers.

[7] The people of the various religions call the Imams of the past prophets and wise men, each in their own era. The people of Islam give the title of imam to those who claim authority in the shadows of ignorance, and they call their scholars and jurists imams. Yet if you examine any of these claimants in light of what the Imam truly requires, none of them can deny that the office is obligatory. Whoever sincerely desires salvation and guidance must set aside inclination and whim, and recognize the incapacity and deficiency of every claimant to this title — how each one falls short in knowledge, in meeting all the virtues and conditions of the Imamate. If one reflects carefully on any claimant's lack of knowledge, lack of piety, lack of integrity, and inability to bear the burdens of the Imamate, the Imamate will be denied to him, and one will ultimately arrive at knowledge of the true Imam.

<!-- page 142 -->

[8] We will review a portion of each claimant's arguments, expose their incapacity and deficiency, and respond to their claims at their foundations. We will deny the Imamate to all who are unworthy of it, and establish it for the Imams of Truth — may God's peace and blessings be upon them — with evidence and proof. We will demonstrate that obedience to them is obligatory, along with compliance with their command and the reception of Shariah, interpretation, and monotheism from them, as we have stated in the Book of Tawhid: whoever comes to know a creature and stands upon its incapacity and deficiency denies divinity to it, until he has denied divinity from every creature — and monotheism then remains, free of all association, neither metaphorical nor obstructed.

[9] We say: God Almighty, through His grace of wisdom and the vastness of His power, created the body of man with origins, causes, and roots, and made all branches and secondary things visibly present within them — appearing from them by a precise and measured decree at a known time — until from them there appears a sensory body aligned with and proportionate to those origins. He made those origins and causes the source of its strength and nourishment, and through them its survival is sustained, and from them come its physical and sensory reward and punishment. He likewise made for souls, spirits, and religious forms

<!-- page 143 -->

intellectual origins, causes, and reasons from whose effects souls, spirits, and religious forms emerge — in which lies their strength, nourishment, survival, and reward. When a soul's substance is cut off and it violates its origin, it is punished.

[10] The Almighty made the visible origins of the body and its causes a witness to the origins of the soul and its branches and nourishment, so that religious creation mirrors physical creation and runs parallel to it. Just as the body did not appear and could not have come into being without the heavens and the celestial spheres — and could not have come into being through the heavens alone without the spheres, even though the spheres revolve within the heavens and the flow of all things operates through the spheres — so too the manifestation of religious and intellectual forms, and the survival of souls, appear and are realized under the influence of the Imams, peace be upon them. From their sciences comes the nourishment of the religious stars. As the Prophet, peace be upon him, said: "My companions are like the stars — whichever of them you follow, you will be guided." Yet their role stands in the place of the prophets, who are like the heavens.

<!-- page 144 -->

The streams of the affairs of creation and their return flow back to the Imams, peace be upon them. We have described the parallel between the heavens of religion and the heavens of nature so that the discussion might be extended and developed further. However, we have confined ourselves here to a brief reference at the opening of this letter — for "whoever wishes" may build upon what we have mentioned, God willing.

[11] We say: inequality and differentiation in all things are foundational realities. The secondary things and branches provide the most conclusive evidence and argument for establishing the Imamate and the Imams. This is because God Almighty created things in differing races and species, and within each genus and type He placed a summit, a peak, a pinnacle, and a supreme virtue that nothing else of its kind attains. He made those races and species either primary and indispensable — things the human world cannot do without, without which neither its body nor its existence can subsist — or food for it, or medicine, or beauty, or adornment, or a tool it cannot forgo.

<!-- page 145 -->

He placed in each of those things a benefit that He placed in no other, and through that quality made the thing distinctive — so that creation requires it of necessity and compulsion, not through any choice of their own. They have no mastery over it; the affairs of their bodies cannot be organized without it. Nor does hardship ever befall them in gathering what serves their purpose and in seeking and acquiring it — if one does not find it in its place, one fulfills the need elsewhere, or hastens to wherever it is found. As long as a person is in this world, his body cannot dispense for the blink of an eye with breathing and drawing benefit from the origins of his world, its branches, its secondary products, its plants, and its food. It is the wisdom of God Almighty and His justice that these serve as witnesses and analogies drawn from the perceptible world as religious parables for the lasting, chosen things. The benefit derived from each of these parables — whether origins, secondary products, food, or religious medicines — is more excellent and more precious than the benefit of the perceptible analogies, and the need for it is greater than the need for the perceptible world, because the benefit of the perceptible accrues to the body, while the benefit of its spiritual counterparts accrues to


<!-- page 146 -->
Because the soul is superior to the body, the ideals and rational analogies are preferable to natural ones — and by the same measure that what endures is preferable to what perishes, the religious parable excels the natural one, and by the same measure that each one's advantage differs, the defect does not encompass it entirely. That a person lacks something of the physical things he needs is not his ruin, even if there is some harm in that lack, and even if his body perishes — for it will inevitably decay and be destroyed. But when the religious parable is absent, the soul is destroyed and falls into eternal torment. How, then, could their diligence in seeking what the soul needs possibly equal their diligence and eagerness in seeking what the body needs? To think so is to fall into doubt and error, and there is no analogy between the two.

<!-- page 147 -->
[12] We say that everything Allah has created in this order — in creation and in what people need, as we have mentioned — serves as a warning to them about the religious parable, as a testimony for those who cite it, a proof for those who draw inference from it, and an insight for those who observe it. It stands as an argument against those who deny the religious parable of the imams and the hudud, their sciences, and the benefit derived from them, just as they accept the benefit they receive from natural origins, the planets, and the heavens. Allah Almighty created these principles, causes, and natural forces in a compelled manner, so that no one can deny them, falsify them, or alter them — whereas people did alter and distort the Sharia, and they changed what the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) had testified to regarding the proof of the Imamate, its obligation, and loyalty to the imams, as well as the obligation to obey them, the censure of those who disobey them, and the promised reward for those who obey them. They changed the Quran and distorted it. As Allah Almighty related concerning them:

<!-- page 148 -->
He said: "They distort the words from their places and forget what they have been reminded of." And the Almighty said: "But the woman is an increase in disbelief" — and this they did not forget; rather, they forgot that testimony as though they had never heard it or seen it.

[13] Just as the testimony of horizons and souls is compulsory and not optional — no one being able to change it — so too is the station of the Prophet (peace be upon him and his family) established without choice, and no witness is able to alter it, replace it, or conceal it. Whoever conceals it has been promised a painful punishment. Allah Almighty commanded that judgments be attested by two witnesses, and He mentioned in His saying: "If one of them goes astray, then the other will remind her," and that the witnesses must not refuse when they are called. This means that the true judgment in religion — the proof of the Imamate and the hudud — rests on two witnesses: one is the natural situation and the other is the legal one.

<!-- page 149 -->
If they deny the positive witness, then the natural one — which comprises the horizons and the souls — is recalled and reinforced, and the testimony of the positive witness is confirmed, as God said: "We will show them Our signs in the horizons and in their own selves until it becomes clear to them that it is the Truth." And the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) was asked: "What will testify to your prophethood?" He said: "Every stone and every clod of earth bears witness to me," and he pointed thereby to the testimony of the horizons and souls. When both the legal and natural witnesses testify, the intellect — which accepts witnesses without inclination or caprice — swiftly demonstrates the truth.

[14] We will now mention the disparity and differentiation within each origin and birth,

<!-- page 150 -->
and cite from every race and type its purpose and its pinnacle, showing how it points to the Imam in every age and time. Although these races and types indicate the Imamate and the imams through another arrangement as well — for instance, the seven planets bearing witness to the seven imams and other such correspondences — it is nonetheless valid to draw guidance from the aspect of the pinnacle and perfection of every kind and genus pointing to the Imam, as we have mentioned.

[15] We say that the wise philosophers spoke of the ten categories and said that nothing can be mentioned or discussed except through these ten. They are: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and passion. The foremost of the ten categories is substance, and it is

<!-- page 151 -->
the totality in which all the rest inhere, and upon which all nine depend. They described it as proof of the true eternal substance — the mental light from which all souls are illuminated — by means of which all nine religious attributes are fulfilled. It is the one that is independent of all creation, benefiting from no one in the world while everyone benefits from it and is in need of it: this is the Imam (peace be upon him). The rest of the hudud beneath him are like the nine attributes.

[16] We say that the four elements from which all compositions are made consist of two that are active and two that are passive. The two active ones are heat and cold, and the two passive ones are moisture and dryness. The most noble and purposeful of them is heat, which encompasses light, illumination, movement, and elevation. It possesses the three powers and has precedence in origin and in the universe; no origin or birth comes into being except through it. Likewise, the Imam is the luminosity of the soul — from him comes the mental light, movement, and elevation — and nothing among the origins comes into being except through him, nor does anything

<!-- page 152 -->
come to be in religion except by him. Then consider the heavens: the highest and uppermost is the last heaven, and everything revolves within it. So too in religion, the Imam is the religious heaven of religion, and all religious planets and constellations move under his command. If one says: "This is a parable for the standing one," then likewise the highest pillars of the world — the proof of the intellect from the four principles — lead us to say: the Imam is the one who speaks in his time, standing in his place, and the Imam occupies his position as the universal intellect does in his world.

[17] We say: The four elements are such that the highest and noblest among them is fire, which reaches the pinnacle that the other elements and natures cannot attain. Likewise, the Imam in

<!-- page 153 -->
his time is the highest pillar of religion, and in him is the light of souls. It is from this perspective that Ibrahim (peace be upon him) was commanded to venerate fire, if that is what was truly meant, and was forbidden from extinguishing or putting it out — though the real reference was to venerating the Imamate and not allowing it to be unjustly extinguished by those who claim its station. The worshippers of fire fell into confusion and did not understand its likeness. And Allah Almighty has said concerning those who oppose and usurp the station of the Imamate: "They want to extinguish the light of Allah with their mouths, but Allah refuses except to perfect His light, even if the disbelievers hate it."

[18] We say that among the many planets there are differences and distinctions, and their purpose and pinnacle in honor and virtue is the sun. It illuminates physical sight, and through it every animal, plant, every child,

<!-- page 154 -->
and every living thing grows — and anything that does not reach the influence of the sun does not grow. Likewise, the Imam is the sun of religion: through him insights are illuminated, souls are lit with the light of guidance and wisdom, and the hearts of the guardians of God are brightened. The worshippers of the sun fell into confusion, for they knew the parable but did not know what it stood for. It is from this perspective that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) referred to the rising of the sun from the west as a reference to the departure of the Imam (peace be upon him) from the west. For the physical sun — if it were to disappear from its orderly cycle of rising and setting even for the twinkling of an eye — would corrupt the entire world, and this is self-evident to both the mind and to nature. The words of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) are not understood correctly except through their interpretation, and that interpretation is sound, by the praise of God and from Him.

[19] We say: Among the twelve zodiac signs, four of them are better and more excellent, and of those four, one is the end and the pinnacle of honor. Likewise,

<!-- page 155 -->
the one with whom the Imamate of the twelve is united is the best and most honorable of them all, surpassing all the rest.

[20] The fourth region, which faces the sky — the fourth facing the sun — is the fairest, best, and most honorable of the regions. So too is the Imam the best of the seven hudud, their term, their goal, and their pinnacle.

[21] We say that the finest of the dissolved jewels and metals is gold, upon which people's transactions are conducted and which is the measure of value for all things. Neither fire nor water affects it or spoils it. So the Imam (peace be upon him) is the finest of the mental religious essences in his time, through whom the transactions of creation and their well-being in religion and in the world are conducted. The other dissolved essences each have high distinctions of their own. Among the stone gems, the purpose of gems is ruby, and the purpose of precious stones is white

<!-- page 156 -->
green, yellow, and red — the finest being the red ruby, which no other jewel rivals in this. Its permanence is like his permanence — it endures while the world around it changes, unaltered by fire or water. It has purity, brilliance, and clarity, and it has been accepted from its original nature that no one else can match it in this, whether comparing it with other jewels, emeralds, Badakhshi rubies, lapis lazuli, garnets, diamonds, or turquoise — for the ruby is the ultimate jewel. So the Imam is the ultimate of all creation in his time, unique above all the other hudud — the proofs, the suffixes, the da'i, the wing, the ma'dhun, and the rest. The Imam is the one whom, in his time, no one can equal in station or rank. In another respect, the seven jewels serve as proof of the seven pronunciations and the seven imams. And this mode of inference we employ is stronger


<!-- page 157 -->
In its position, the natural analogy holds at all times: just as its likeness must be present in every age, so too the stars, natures, births, and jewels are ever-present — and therefore the limits and their effects on souls must likewise be present at all times.

[22] We say that the grains sustaining and nourishing man include seven categories, the foremost of which is wheat — the noblest and most honored, and by which the body is sustained. In the same way, the Imam is the culmination and summit; through him come the benefits that nourish souls, and in him lies their strength, their life, and the sciences that reach them.

[23] As for fruit-bearing trees, their purpose and peak is the palm tree. Like human beings, palm trees have male and female, and require pollination — the male being like a stallion among animals. And yet

<!-- page 158 -->
nothing of the palm tree goes to waste; every part yields benefit, its fruits eaten fresh or cooked in every season. When a person sees in a dream that he is receiving fresh dates from a palm tree, the interpretation is that he benefits from a believing man — and the true believer is in fact the Imam, who is the head of the believers.

[24] We say that among plants there are gentle medicines that serve as antidotes, saving those on the brink of destruction; some cure grave diseases, and some fall below that degree. The highest and culminating example is the proof of the Imam, whose words and teachings are an antidote to souls, healing them and saving them from destruction, disease, doubt, and disagreement. As for medicines that are deadly poisons and things that cause harm — these are analogous to those who usurp the station of the Imams, peace be upon them. They are fatal poisons,

<!-- page 159 -->
deadly to those who hear from them, accompany them, or incline toward them, unless the Imam connects those people to the greatest antidote that saves souls from destruction.

The same applies to stones that are useless and serve no purpose — they are analogous to those opposites. For this reason, Allah the Almighty said: *"You and what you worship shall be fuel for Hell, and to it you will return."* And He said: *"Guard yourselves against the Fire whose fuel is people and stones, prepared for the disbelievers."* (Surah 2, Al-Baqarah 24)

[25] We say: among animals, the noblest is the horse; among birds, the eagle; and among the seven types, the lion. The varieties of animals differ and are distinguished in strength, usefulness, and suitability for service, each type rising to its own summit. The proof and peak is that whoever stands as the culmination and goal of the animal kingdom and of the human species is an end

<!-- page 160 -->
for all, and he is the Imam, peace be upon him.

[26] We say that some plants are sweet, good, pleasant, and delightful — beneficial to people — while others are bitter, yielding neither benefit nor pleasure, and others still bear no fruit at all and are merely thorns that harm those who approach them. The good and sweet are like the Imams and a parable for their words and sciences, which souls desire and eyes delight in. The bitter, the foul-smelling, and the thorny are like the imams of the people of the outward. Therefore, Allah the Almighty said: *"Have you not seen how Allah has set forth the parable of a good word? It is like a good tree whose root is firmly fixed and whose branches reach into the sky, yielding its fruit in every season by the permission of its Lord. And Allah sets forth parables for people so that they may reflect. And a corrupt word is like a corrupt tree, uprooted from above the earth — it has no stability."* (Surah 14, Ibrahim 24–26)

[27] We say that among plants there are those that smell good, and people find rest in their fragrance and scent, and are healed by them from every disease and ailment — the purpose is in the pleasing

<!-- page 161 -->
fragrance. Such a plant is like the Imam and his words, which bring rest to souls. The stench and harmful odor, on the other hand, are evidence of the misguided imams and their words, which harm souls and may even lead to their destruction.

[28] We say: the gazelle produces musk, and bees produce honey — yet their hives are also evidence of the Imam. For this reason, the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said to the Commander of the Believers Ali, peace be upon him: "You are the dragonfly of the believers." And the Almighty said: *"Your Lord inspired the bees to make their homes in mountains, trees, and what people build, then to feed on all kinds of fruits and follow the ways your Lord has made easy for them. From their bellies comes a drink of varying colors in which there is healing for people."* (Surah 16, An-Nahl 68–69)

[29] We say: human beings are the end of animals and all else, and the benefit of everything mentioned above — origins and branches alike — flows back to human beings; all that is born into

<!-- page 162 -->
the world, all animals, plants, and the like, and their usefulness, their worth, and their quality, ultimately serve human beings. Were it not for human beings, the virtue of things would not have appeared, the order of the world would not have been established, and nothing would have been derived from it — neither benefit nor preservation — whether from plant or animal. It is human beings who take water and direct it across the land, bringing it under governance and skill, drawing it from valleys, rivers, and wells. Human beings plant the land, cultivate it, build dwellings in it, draw water for plants and agriculture, and bring the earth under organized governance.

Then it was human beings who made use of the wind and the air, and who drew fire out by striking flint — that fire which is latent in nature and appears in animals only through the mating of one with another. Human beings grafted trees together until they produced fruits that had never existed in the world.

<!-- page 163 -->
They extracted jewels from ores, and brought trees, grains, and fruits under their governance and management, preserving the goodness of animals, plants, and animal produce, and placing the animal under their rule and governance. Just as the purpose of animals, plants, minerals, and all that is born was human beings — and through human beings the animal was reformed and ordered — so too the Imam is the goal, the summit, and the highest of human beings, their perfection, and through the Imam the affairs of human beings are ordered. And just as all the above are of benefit to human beings and are referred back to human beings, so too all the benefits of human beings and their attributes are attributed to the Imams, peace be upon them. Through them, human beings are taught virtues and sciences; through them, intellects and sound judgment are cultivated; and through them, their well-being in religion and worldly affairs is secured — as is guidance in dealing with their enemies. Through the Imams, human beings are guided to know the Creator and to understand that gratitude to the Blessed One is obligatory, and how to render that gratitude. Through them, they come to know the Messenger and how to obey him.

[30] Just as the benefit of all animals and what they offer is attributed to human beings — and by virtue of this, human beings became their stewards — so too all the benefits of human beings are attributed to

<!-- page 164 -->
the Imams, who thus became the commanders and the authorities over human beings. Were it not for the Imams and their guidance to human beings, their direction, the preservation of their goodness in their livelihood and against their enemies, and their guidance toward acquiring virtues and avoiding vices and animal habits — there would be no distinction between animals and humans. Indeed, the animal would be better, because if a person does not accept refinement, has not acquired virtues for himself, has not learned from the Imams, and has not drawn on their intellect, then he is worse than the beasts — for the beasts are incapable of the corruption that man can perpetrate. There is much benefit in animals, but in human beings, if they lack reason and religion, there is nothing but evil and corruption. For this reason, Allah the Almighty said: *"Surely the worst of creatures in the sight of Allah are the deaf and dumb who do not understand."* (Surah 8, Al-Anfal 22) And He said:

<!-- page 165 -->
*"They are like cattle — nay, they are more astray."* (Surah 7, Al-A'raf 179)

And just as, were it not for human beings, there would be no wisdom in the creation of animals and plants, and no benefit would have been derived from them — so too, were it not for the Imams, peace be upon them, there would be no wisdom in the creation of human beings, and their virtue would not have been revealed. Human beings were created to receive virtues and knowledge, and to learn knowledge and religion from the Imams, peace be upon them. Through the Imams, religion is established and fulfilled. If the virtue of human beings had not appeared through religion, then the virtue of animals and plants would not have appeared either, and there would have been no wisdom in the creation of humans, plants, and animals. And if the wisdom in their creation were negated, then the wisdom in the creation of all the worlds would be negated.

[31] If it is established that all the worlds were created for the sake of mankind, and that human beings were created

<!-- page 166 -->
for the Imams, peace be upon them, and to receive religion from them — and through the Imams religion is fulfilled and its virtues, order, and strength are made manifest — then the worlds and all of creation are created for the Imams, the peace of God be upon them. All the benefits of the worlds are attributed to them, and the rest of creation are their dependents. Everything the Imams do for creatures, they do by their grace, generosity, and mercy — no creature holds any obligation or claim over them. Whatever is taken from this world without their permission and consent is therefore forbidden and a usurpation. For this reason, Allah the Almighty said: *"Whatever Allah restored to His Messenger from the people of the towns belongs to Allah and to the Messenger."* (Surah 59, Al-Hashr 7) And for this reason, the shadow is called the shadow of the faith, because from that place it goes out and returns to it. The whole world came forth from the wealth of the Imams and from their kingdom, and whatever reverts to their kingdom is a fief.

[32] We say: the plant does not increase upon the earth with a single spirit, which is


<!-- page 167 -->
The spirit of growth animates the earth beneath a plant, and the plant grows upon it and withers by the power drawn from it. When the animal exceeded the plant by possessing one additional spirit — the spirit of sensation — it thus had two souls: the spirit of growth and the spirit of sensation. With these it subdued the earth, moved across it, and made use of the plant, eating from it. When human beings exceeded the animal through the additional spirit of speech, they subdued the earth, built upon it, cultivated it, and brought it under their dominion. They tied knots, ate plants and fruits, used them as they wished, and burned what they wished. They subdued animals, riding what they needed, slaughtering, milking, and shearing them — employing them at will, compelling them despite the animals' aversion. When the Imam exceeded others by additional spirits, powers, and virtues unlike anything before — for the ultimate purpose of the elite of the animal kingdom is humanity — that humanity became united with the Imam in its refined nature. The culmination of the animal kingdom's

<!-- page 168 -->
highest capacities, its sensual and purified aspects, the purpose underlying the happiness inscribed in the stars, the moderation of nature, and the subtle influence of the heavens and all the planets — all of this converged until the Imam became the brain of the entire world, and then the soul of the soul. The faculty of speech, pure thought, and the rational soul were united through him, as was the holy faculty of Jerusalem, which has never received the slightest good fortune except from the side of the Imam — and from the Imam to the one he has chosen, whom he designates as his successor, and with whom the Holy Spirit is united: the seventh perfect spirit that God has prepared for humankind in the world of recompense, which is the spirit of unity.

[33] These are some of the virtues that God Almighty bestowed upon him, followed by the acquired virtues that no one else possessed: purity, noble character, goodness, generosity, forbearance, courage, and countless other qualities beyond what ordinary people can enumerate — along with complete distance from every impurity, defect, and deficiency. When God Almighty gathered all these powers, virtues, and souls in the Imam, beyond what is found in other human beings, He placed all of humanity and their dominion — along with animals, plants, the earth, and minerals —

<!-- page 169 -->
entirely under his authority, just as human beings harnessed animals and took possession of them through the increase of one weak additional soul, and just as animals harnessed plants through the increase of one weak additional soul, and just as plants harnessed the earth through the increase of one weak spirit.
