Through the increase of souls in the Imams, peace be upon them, their ownership of human beings was established: the authority to govern them and the legitimacy to judge them in accordance with what the powers and souls require, as described above. Human beings are thereby bound — no human being can remove himself from the Imam's rule and kingdom, just as no animal has the power to stand outside the rule of human beings. Any animal that departs from human governance is like a wild beast in which no benefit or virtue remains, having lost the governance of reason and religious law. Such an animal is to be killed and destroyed, because it has deviated from obedience to human beings and from their rule over it.

Likewise, whoever disobeys the Imam and turns away from him is like a ferocious wild beast: killing him becomes permissible, and his destruction is justified by his departure from the Imam's kingdom in religious law,

<!-- page 170 -->
reason, nature, and governance. But whoever obeys the Imams, peace be upon them, and submits to their rule in this world will be spared the sword and punishment and will live in security, righteousness, and justice. The Imam, peace be upon him, is in turn obligated by the rule of kingship, nature, governance, and religious law to preserve the wellbeing of those under him, just as human beings are obligated to preserve animals — providing them with fodder and care. Whoever submits to the Imam's judgment in matters of both soul and body will have knowledge and wisdom flow abundantly upon him, bringing him to life. He will thrive among creation, living in the ease and goodness that knowledge bestows, and his inner dwelling will be refined and made ready to receive its recompense and remain in it. But whoever is shielded from the Imam's judgment — in matters of both flesh and soul — will be afflicted with ignorance, confusion, doubt, disagreement, and regret. His inner dwelling will be disordered and unready to receive recompense, and he will remain in punishment.

[34] Human beings at all times preserve the wellbeing of animals, forbid them from what harms them, drive them toward what benefits them, and protect their water and pasture,

<!-- page 171 -->
along with the time of their breeding and their offspring. They prevent animals from what would destroy them and from places where their food and drink would be harmful. Indeed, animals often resist this guidance and protection, even as human beings, compelled by their aversion, preserve every aspect of the animals' interests. So too the Imam, peace be upon him, preserves the consequences of human affairs, their interests, all their conditions and their underlying causes, treating what is hidden as well as what is manifest — caring for them with compassion. Were it not for the Imam, human beings would have no order in their world, no security, and no salvation from corruption — a corruption more powerful than any they could escape from — nor any escape from disagreement and punishment in the life to come. And just as human beings bear with the harmful conduct of animals and endure their bad manners when seeking their wellbeing, it is obligatory in wisdom to rule over knowledge — so that one who knows of a soul's destruction and corruption is not permitted to let it be destroyed without justification, but must save it from ruin. Similarly, governance over properties is obligatory in wisdom in order to preserve them, prevent their damage, and keep them from being removed

<!-- page 172 -->
from the benefits intended for creation. So the Imam, peace be upon him, bears with the poor conduct of creation and endures the harm and hardship they cause him, because the rule of kingship and the rule of knowledge demand guiding the ignorant, preserving their wellbeing, and preventing them from perdition and destruction. In the end this leads them back to righteousness and salvation. He does not regard their poor conduct or their ignorance with reproach, nor does he withhold from those who are disobedient — rather, he looks upon them with the eye of mercy.

[35] Just as human beings endure harm and hardship from their young children and show them mercy in the hope that they will reach the level of full humanity and become rightly guided — so the Imam, peace be upon him, endures harm and fatigue from creation out of mercy and compassion for them, as a father spends himself for his children, hoping that from among them will emerge survivors who are rightly guided.

<!-- page 173 -->
[36] Consider how a teacher endures hardship from boys and compels them to learn knowledge and proper conduct, even as the boy resists it, dislikes the teacher, and prefers play and idleness — yet the wise educator guides him toward what is good for him despite that resistance. When the boy reaches the level of knowledge, he recognizes the benefit of what he was taught, he ceases to struggle against it, and he regrets his shortcomings and his failure to obey the educator. So too the Imam, peace be upon him, takes creation in hand for the sake of their wellbeing in religion and worldly affairs, even as they resist this and are averse to what he teaches them. Yet in the end, when their goodness through it is made plain to them, they will regret having abandoned obedience.

[37] Human beings were not created immune by nature from temperamental disorders and natural diseases. They have never been free from the influence of space and time, the influence of the heavens and planets, and from food and other

<!-- page 174 -->
things contrary to their constitution — excessive eating, fatigue, sexual intercourse, and other causes of illness and disease. People were once ignorant of medicine, and had they remained so, overwhelmed by the multitude of these diseases, most would have perished and died, which would have led to the destruction of the world. But God Almighty created remedies corresponding to every disease, and He revealed these to prophets and imams, peace be upon them, so that they knew the benefits of medicines, understood the heavens, recognized diseases, and taught creation — opening the way of medicine to them — until others built upon that foundation and expanded it through experience and practice, just as they expanded in other crafts, industries, and sciences such as engineering, agriculture, navigation, and the like. From this, physicians arose among creation to heal people and gather medicines from distant lands, laboring in this — some for the benefit of all, some for the good of creation.

<!-- page 175 -->
They heal people sometimes with pleasant and sweet things, and sometimes with bitter and sharp things. The sick person may despise the bitter, foul-smelling remedy that heals him, and spit it out in revulsion or by choice. The physician gives him glad tidings of the good outcome and warns him, cautioning him about destruction if he does not drink it. The physician may apply a treatment that causes discomfort, and may prevent the patient from eating and drinking the things he craves, commanding him to abstain from the food he loves — food that agrees with him in times of health — while administering to him what is strong and affects him when he is in danger of perdition. If the patient is unaware of how to restore himself with medicine, the physician orders cauterization, lancing, and the cutting of certain organs in the hope of saving what remains. A sane person accepts this because he knows it is right for him, and he knows that the physician knows his interests better than he does, that the physician is trustworthy and honest, and wants his wellbeing and his recovery through all of this. If the patient is not wise,

<!-- page 176 -->
he will despise the physician, and yet the physician has mercy — if he sees that the patient has the nature of a sick man who will not heal himself. It is out of his mercy and compassion that he persists with the patient against his will. The patient may curse the physician and wish to strike and drive him away, but the physician holds firm, knowing that this is mercy toward him, and that if the patient truly understood and were in his right mind, he would not hate what is good for him. The physician does not respond by abandoning him, but continues to treat him until he returns to health.

[38] This is the case in religious matters as well, for religious diseases are many because of people's differences, their passions, their inclination toward comfort and permissiveness, innovations, desires, contradictory doctrines, scattered opinions, and corrupt tendencies. The night is dark, the road uncertain, and people were ignorant of the treatment of their souls and of how to preserve their spiritual health — ignorant of the harmful things they were obligated to avoid, and ignorant of the path and the ways of religion. Creation needed a guide and a physician who could cure their illnesses, show them the sound path, and save their souls.


<!-- page 177 -->
With kindness, compassion, counsel, tenderness, and forbearance — far more so than physicians of the body, however compassionate the latter may be, however free from greed in their treatment, however beneficial their care. The Imams are more trustworthy guardians of souls and religion, more honest, more knowledgeable, more experienced, and more discerning than physicians of the body.

[39] The risk and danger in treating the soul is greater than the risk in healing the body; errors in it are more serious. Just as a physician of the body must possess sound judgment, so too is sound judgment a necessity for this physician of souls. This compassionate, learned physician is the Imam, peace be upon him, at every time and in every age. Were it not for the mercy, compassion, and tenderness the Imams, peace be upon them, show toward the people of this world and the next, all would be lost — for they, peace be upon them,

<!-- page 178 -->
treat creation with forbearance, tolerance, and kindness, and they do not fix their gaze on people's failure to obey or on their ill conduct and bad deeds. Rather, they look upon them with the eye of mercy and strive earnestly to preserve their health and cure their diseases — sometimes through measured sustenance that keeps the soul in good order, sometimes by withholding sustenance so that no burden or excess accumulates, sometimes by giving them bitter medicines that are taken reluctantly in order to purge corrupt and regressive elements from within, and sometimes by commanding them to take shelter until they are weakened, and by admonishing and warning them, just as the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, deals with these people. In all circumstances the Imams wish well for those who are corrupt, and they see potential for good in them and remain patient. We ask God to make us among those who benefit from the healing of the Imams of the age, peace be upon them, and not to make us among those who require the iron and the knife because they have turned away.

<!-- page 179 -->
As God Almighty said: *"And if God were to take mankind to task for what they have earned, He would not leave upon it a single living creature."* (Surah 35, Fatir: 45)

[40] We say: The wise and the people of insight are those who reflect on the workings of this world and on God Almighty's actions in His creation — in how He arranged the world and preserved the welfare of creation. When they see fertile land that they know will receive seed and bring forth growth, and they find that the water lies far off in deep valleys or beneath the ground, they labor to bring that water up from those valleys and from underground by digging channels and wells and conveying the water to that land. They see no wisdom in leaving good land idle and wasted, in letting water go unused — for that would be a squandering of creation and a failure to preserve its welfare and build up the world. In the same way, the Imams, peace be upon them, when they see people ready

<!-- page 180 -->
to receive the planting of wisdom and knowledge, yet far from being able to accept it, strive earnestly — digging channels, so to speak, through vigorous and repeated effort. Perhaps those people do not yet know the reason for this effort; but if they are wise, they will be made ready to receive it. And if they are blind to having it conveyed to them at that degree, the Imams cast wisdom before them in dense parables, whether in words or in action, through examples — and then they descend to the meanings of those parables and gradually elevate the people toward them.

[41] In light of this, the believer must hold and know that the Imam of the Age is the head of the world and the deputy of God over His creation, and that no command, prohibition, action, or saying of the Imam, peace be upon him, is to be objected to. He must know in principle that all of this is wisdom and goodness for creation, and that if its purpose is difficult for him to grasp at the time, then in

<!-- page 181 -->
time its benefits will become clear — just as they have become clear in many things that people did not understand at first. How often it is that in the Sharia people did not know the purpose of its Author, nor the wisdom in it, and condemned it — until God granted someone the knowledge of its meanings and he came to recognize the wisdom it contained. And how often people see things in the world whose wisdom in creation they cannot perceive, and they suppose that there is no purpose in them, yet if they knew the wisdom, they would not condemn them. And many things they see as harmful and corrupt, with no wisdom or good in them, yet if they knew the face of wisdom in them, they would be filled with wonder. Whoever knows a wise person and acknowledges his wisdom should know and acknowledge that everything he does is wisdom, that he does nothing except for wisdom, commands nothing except for wisdom, forbids nothing except for wisdom, and says nothing except for wisdom — and that it is not permissible for a wise person to tamper with his decisions, even though

<!-- page 182 -->
it is inconceivable that a wise person would do, say, command, or prohibit something in which there is no wisdom. That would be vanity, and it is not permissible to tamper with the wise. Whoever does something — and one act contradicts another — has gone beyond wisdom.

[42] If it is impermissible for a wise person to do something whose existence and non-existence are one and the same, how then is it conceivable that the Imam, peace be upon him, would do something whose wisdom lies elsewhere, or whose righteousness lies in its opposite — and yet he is the Absolutely Wise? For if wisdom could reside in something other than what he does, then what he did would be corruption and futility — God forbid. Whoever believes in the Imam of his time in such a fashion has taken him out of wisdom. And if he takes him out of wisdom, he has deviated from his Imamate — and this is a departure from religion. God and all believers protect us from such suspicions and dangers to belief. Our sustenance is satisfaction and submission.

<!-- page 183 -->
[43] We say: Wild animals that have value and virtue require a shepherd to tend them, protect them, choose their fodder and water, and guard them from predators and thieves. Those that have no one to do this for them will perish and be unable to manage their own affairs; they will neither ward off harm nor attend to what is right for them. People are like sheep: if they have no shepherd to protect them, they will perish. The Imam is their shepherd, who guards them in the affairs of their religion and their worldly life and wards off from them the enemies of religion and the world. Were they left without him, none would remain, and none would escape torment. It is for this reason that people are called the flocks of their kingdoms — they are under the care of their shepherd, and the shepherd is in reality the Imam. In the same way,

<!-- page 184 -->
the adversaries are wolves that prey upon the sheep.

[44] We say: The world is like a body, and a body without a head is a useless, rotting corpse. Likewise, a world without a head and a ruler — one who sets right the affairs of religion and worldly life — becomes a slaughterhouse in which its members prey upon one another. The world cannot last a single day in this condition, and there is no wisdom in creating the world without an Imam. The Imam is the head of the world, its statesman, and its guiding intellect.

[45] We say: The organs of the body vary and differ, and the highest and finest of them is the head — and in it is the face by which every human being is known and distinguished from others. Among the internal organs, the finest is the brain, which is the head of them all and the seat of the intellect. Similarly, the Imam in the world is the head of all organs and

<!-- page 185 -->
the brain — and just as the brain governs the workings of the body, so do the affairs of creation revolve around the Imam. Among all the faculties within the human being, the finest of souls is the intellect, and the Imam is the whole intellect of the world; through him and with him, all the people of the world are united.

[46] We say: Leadership exists by nature in all things, as mentioned above. It is found among animals and birds — the stallion tends to his mares and disciplines them, the rooster tends to and disciplines the hens, and every male guards the young of its kind to the extent of its capacity and defends its females and the weaker members of its kind. Females and young, as those in need of care and nurturing, are above

<!-- page 186 -->
their leader in that they obey him, just as cranes obey their leader and bees obey their queen.

[47] Indeed, God created the world and fashioned creation — as we have described in part — according to this order of disparity and differentiation, with gradations of strength and weakness, of honor and humility, so that people might know that just as every species and gender has a goal, a summit, and a head in honor and distinction, so too must all of creation have a goal and a summit in honor and distinction — one who is the head of the world and its statesman. Once it is established that leadership and governance are obligatory by nature, creation, and instinct, religion cannot deny the statesman and the leader, the guiding intellect of the world — for reason and observation confirm it as a necessary duty. And it is a duty that this leader be the most excellent of all, the most honored, the most complete, the most learned, and the most refined — as evidenced by what we have mentioned of creation and nature. This leader is the Imam in all things, the head and the summit, and the designation of Imam does not apply to any lesser rank.


<!-- page 187 -->
On the led in prayer, nor the name of the virtuous over the less virtuous, nor the name of the one who precedes over the one who follows. Yet all people — across sects, religions, philosophical schools, and the broader world — acknowledge that those who govern the world's affairs ought to be the best and most complete among its people. And there is no such governor in the world with this description except the Imams, peace be upon them.

[48] We say: Philosophers have divided governance into three categories — private governance, household governance, and public governance. Private governance concerns a man's rule over himself; household governance concerns his management of his family and dependants; and public governance concerns cities and their core affairs. A man's governance of himself requires that he restrain himself from all vices, corrupt morals, and bestial habits,

<!-- page 188 -->
and blameworthy desires. The firm man governs himself by reproaching and correcting himself when he falls short, and by praising and rewarding himself when he does well, always striving toward his ideals. Household governance requires that a man guide his family and dependants in preserving their goodness and acquiring virtues and noble character; he rebukes those who fall into fault and praises those who excel.

[49] Public governance concerns a man's governance of cities — preserving the well-being of their people, preventing them from blameworthy conduct and evil deeds, ensuring every person occupies his proper station, punishing those who transgress with censure, and rewarding those who do well with praise. It also means protecting the affairs of their religion and guarding against their enemies. This quality that the philosophers

<!-- page 189 -->
attributed to the true statesman is found nowhere among those who lay claim to the Imamate, among sultans, or among their scholars. It is found only in the Imams of the family of Muhammad, peace be upon them, and no one in the world acts as they do.

The philosophers agreed that whoever fails to govern himself cannot govern his household, and whoever fails to govern his household cannot be said to govern the public in any meaningful sense. The Imams of the outward tradition, their sultans, and their scholars fall entirely short of this standard. Those who seated themselves in the councils of the Imams called themselves by those honorable names, yet they defiled themselves, their families, and the people. We see them today immersed in amusement, debauchery, and corruption. This quality that

<!-- page 190 -->
the philosophers required of the true statesman was not fulfilled by any of those sultans. Perhaps the intent of this argument is to urge people to obey the Imam, presenting him as the true statesman so that they will seek such a statesman — and when they do, they will find that no one of lesser capacity can be accepted, and they will be compelled to refer to the true Imam.

[50] We say: Since people are born in ignorance, with no knowledge, and since knowledge does not arise from nature or innate utterance, they stand in need of education. The proof that knowledge does not arise from nature is this: if knowledge were innate, people would be knowers by necessity and speakers by necessity — just as the braying of a donkey and the barking of dogs, being innate to them, requires no education

<!-- page 191 -->
and does not differ across India, Sindh, Byzantium, or Arabia. If speech and knowledge were innately printed in human beings, as we have described, people would need no education, and no one would differ from another — just as the donkey's bray does not vary by region. But since people do need to teach and learn from one another, this process must ultimately trace back to one who learned from no one else but received knowledge through divine support rather than human instruction. This need for education makes a teacher necessary, and the chain of teachers must have an end — and that end is the Prophet and the Imam, peace be upon them. This is among the most compelling proofs of the Imamate and prophethood.

[51] We say: God Almighty created humanity with the capacity to receive knowledge and

<!-- page 192 -->
wisdom, but He created them deficient and ignorant. Had He left them purposelessly, there would have been no basis for assigning them duties — no deeds, no reward, no punishment — and their creation would have served no wisdom. When God strengthened the Prophets and Imams by granting them wisdom and knowledge, they conveyed it to creation, showed the blind the way, and guided them to the truth. Creation was thus obligated to follow their commands and prohibitions — and without the Imams, no duty could have been assigned, and no argument established against anyone. The Imams are God's proofs against His creation at all times: at the outset of their assignment and in every dispute that arises. When people fall into disagreement and each party argues against the other — citing a Quranic verse, a report, or an analogy — the argument of one side does not settle the other. Were there no Imam in the world to rescue them from disagreement and doubt and guide them to the truth, then God would have no standing argument against creation, and creation could say: We found no one to guide us to the truth, and truth was indistinguishable from falsehood to us. It is through the Imam that God's argument against creation is established, as the Almighty said:

<!-- page 193 -->
*"So that God might have the argument."* (Surah al-An'am 6:149)

[52] We say: The branches of knowledge attributed to the wise — which in truth are the Imams, peace be upon them — trace back in origin to all knowledge and wisdom, including arithmetic and geometry. Everything in those disciplines has an end and a summit that leads back to the Imam. Consider geometric shapes: squares, triangles, and arches. The most complete and excellent of all forms is the circle, which is the origin of all the others, for from it one can derive the square, the triangle, and the arch. When an arm is drawn from a circle, the square remains within it; the arches appear at its edges; and if a circle takes its diameter as a plane, it yields four triangles. All other shapes

<!-- page 194 -->
are derived from the circle, and every shape is contained within it — making it the whole of all forms. The circle of shapes is thus a guide and a witness to the Imam, who is the circle of religion: all boundaries fall under his authority, and he is the one from whom all benefit. The circle needs none of the other shapes, while all other shapes need it. Likewise, the Imam needs none of the religious boundaries in his time and age, while all boundaries need him, and he encompasses them all.

[53] We say: The origin and source of numbers is the One, which is the most complete of numbers. The one thousand dispenses with everything below it, and everything below a thousand is in need of the One. The Imam is one in his age and time, and all the religious boundaries need him, while he is dispensed with them. If some think that the One falls among ordinary things, that is a mistaken assumption — the One is the cause of counting, the origin of numbers, and two is

<!-- page 195 -->
one and one, as is three. But the One is the Imam in his boundaries and station: he is singular, and no one in creation shares that with him in his time and age.

[54] We say: The point, as defined by mathematicians, has no part — yet it is the origin of the line, the surface, and the body. This is proof of the Imam: no one can reach the limits of his knowledge, and whoever attempts to describe him finds himself incapable of doing so.

[55] We say: Among the days, the most excellent is the Sabbath; among the months, it is Ramadan; and among the hours, there is one hour in which supplication is answered. Every creature and subject remaining in the world belongs to some genus and type, and each has a summit

<!-- page 196 -->
of honor and distinction that no other has attained, as we have mentioned. Were we to enumerate everything that falls under this chapter, it would exceed the scope of this treatise. What we have mentioned is sufficient for one who reflects on it, and from these principles he may measure and derive what he wishes concerning the origins and branches. We now turn to citing the obligatory duties and their arrangement as further evidence, by way of summary.

[56] We say: In prayer — in its arrangement, its standing, the direction faced, and the imam's position at the head of the rows — lies the strongest proof of the Imamate. The Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, first commanded the facing of the Qibla. The word *qibla* is derived from the root meaning alignment and directing one's face forward — it is said: the front of a thing is what lies before it and faces it, as opposed to what is behind and distant. The imam of prayer is called *imam* by virtue of this same meaning, because he leads the people.

<!-- page 197 -->
Facing the Qibla thus signifies facing the Imam, and the command to the people to face it signifies their obligation to turn toward him and obey him in all acts of worship, taking guidance from his direction. The one who turns toward the Qibla in prayer is, in effect, turning toward the Imam in all his worship and obedience. Then, if his prayer is performed with his imam — as the Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, stated, that the congregational prayer is twenty-seven times the prayer of the individual — then one must stand with the Imam facing him, following him as a follower in both intention and deed. This is proof that all obedience must be performed behind the Grand Imam, following him and entrusting oneself to him, so that it may hold virtue. The Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, further commanded that the rows be straightened and that people's standing not be uneven — which is proof that it is not permissible for the hearts of the believers who follow the Imam to differ, nor their deeds, nor their words.


<!-- page 198 -->
The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) commanded the congregation to follow the prayer imam even when the recitation, standing, or prostration is prolonged, and not to contradict him in deed or intention. If the follower agrees with the imam's action but not with his intention, his prayer does not split into two separate prayers, even if that were theoretically possible.

All of this demonstrates that the imam of a single prayer — who is not an absolute Imam and whose obedience, though obligatory, yields a reward many times greater — demands that level of compliance in worship. How much more, then, must obedience be given to the Great Imam, who leads in all of the Shariah and its interpretation? If it is impermissible for the follower to deviate from the imam in any act of prayer, how can it be permissible to disobey the Imam in his commands and prohibitions throughout one's life?

<!-- page 199 -->
The believer remains under the authority of the imam throughout the prayer for as long as that state continues. If he breaks with the imam through an act, intention, or resolution, he invalidates his prayer and departs from the ruling that governs the worshipper. Likewise, if the worshipper resolves to disobey the imam, he invalidates his prayer, his bowing, his prostration, his standing, his sitting, and all the acts he performs, and he must begin the prayer again. How, then, can one refuse to obey the Great Imam whom Allah has made obligatory to obey?

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: "The imam is a guarantor." A prayer lacks anyone to guarantee it without an imam. Through the Imam (peace be upon him), the follower is assured freedom from doubt, suspicion, confusion, and omission, and his soundness is guaranteed so long as he follows the Imam's example. The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) pointed to the imam's station above all others, and commanded his community accordingly, saying: "If he prostrates, then prostrate;

<!-- page 200 -->
if he bows, then bow; if he recites, then listen; and if he submits, then submit."

[58] All of these are indications of the obligation to obey the Imams (peace be upon them) and to hold them in the highest regard in all their affairs; of the steadfastness of their station in their era; and of the requirement to obey them until authority is delivered to the one who rightfully receives it. Even if the Shariah contained no other indication of the Imam and the obligation of obedience to him than the prayer, that alone would be sufficient. The Prophet then made clear that the imam lifts omissions from the people in prayer — that is, the imam removes such shortcomings from them, just as Allah Most High said: "Even if they have wronged themselves, they came to you, and Allah forgave them and the Prophet forgave them; they found Allah most forgiving and merciful" (Quran 4:64). If the Imam offered forgiveness and accepted repentance on behalf of this community, they would have perished otherwise, and they stand in even greater need of forgiveness. All of this applies at every time and in every age to those who

<!-- page 201 -->
come to the Imam seeking forgiveness, and Allah forgives them just as He forgave those people.

[59] We say: The Friday prayer is valid only with a sermon delivered from pulpits, and the people pray behind the imam so that it is made known that obedience to the Imam does not arise from creation spontaneously. Moreover, the whole of Hajj is proof of the Imamate. Hajj requires an intention, and intention is directed toward a known and existing thing. Even when intention is expressed through knowledge, it is a recognized intention that needs no further explanation — and it is a reference, in the end, to the Imam. The Ka'bah itself, in some interpretations, represents the Imam. Allah placed it in a desert where there is no nearby settlement, reachable only with hardship, in a place free from crops and trees and from worldly benefit, so that people might understand that reaching its counterpart also comes only through hardship, fatigue, and the harm of

<!-- page 202 -->
enemies — and that one's intention must not be directed toward the Imam out of worldly desire, greed, pleasure, or worldly prestige, but only for pure religion. When hardship, fatigue, hostility from enemies, or weariness overtakes the pilgrim, his chest does not constrict; he bears it with patience, for he knows the Imam and sees in the pilgrimage his likeness. He then circumambulates the Ka'bah, receives it, gestures toward it, performs the sa'i, the casting of stones, and the ihram — refraining from good things and from sexual intercourse — all of which serves as evidence for the Imamate and for obedience to the Imam in the varying conditions of the guides, as well as for guarding one's soul and casting off reprehensible and corrupt beliefs.

[60] We say: The matter of paying zakat, fulfilling its obligations, safeguarding it, and distributing it is among the most compelling proofs of the obligation of the Imamate. Allah Most High commanded the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) to take charity from the people, as He said: "Take from their wealth a charity that purifies them and cleanses them, and pray for them, for your prayer is a comfort to them"

<!-- page 203 -->
(Quran 9:103). The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) explained the obligatory duties of zakat, its nisab, and the manner of collecting it. He collected it himself, so that people would know both how to give it and how to receive it. The one who later assumed leadership from the council of Imams claimed that collecting it was his right and used this verse and the practice of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) as proof against the community. He fought the Bani Hanifah for that reason, killed them, and collected zakat from those who refused, until the matter was settled. Later, however, some fell into immorality, preferring the wealth of zakat for themselves and refusing to deliver it to the Imam. What a remarkable state this community has reached — acknowledging that the verse is not abrogated in its wisdom or its text, and that the Sunnah of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) was acted upon, yet permitting themselves to change the ruling of the verse and the command of Allah Most High, and to alter the Sunnah of those who claimed leadership over them. Their only motive in doing so was to deny the ranks and

<!-- page 204 -->
the obligation of the Imamate for the Ahl al-Bayt of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him). Allah Most High made zakat a right of the Imamate and assigned its collection to the Imams, just as He forbade charity upon the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) so that no one could accuse him of collecting it for personal gain. He likewise forbade it for the Prophet's family so that it would be clear that the Imam among them stands in the place of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) — that he and his family are entrusted with its collection and bear responsibility for it.

[61] Even if people were themselves entrusted with paying zakat and distributing it — since Allah Most High commanded them to do so just as He commanded them to pray, fast, and fulfill other obligatory duties — He did not command them to separate charity from the Prophet's command. If it had been permissible for people to manage zakat on their own, it would have been permissible in the time of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), when they were stronger in religion, more certain, and more faithful. Why, then, did the people after them become

<!-- page 205 -->
trustees of zakat? Why is it permissible for them to pray — which is a greater act than zakat — behind their imams and to fulfill other obligatory duties under their leadership, yet it is said to be impermissible to deliver zakat to those same imams? Why were they deemed trustees of blood and lives, and empowered to judge in all other matters, but not trusted with wealth in zakat?

If the people are telling the truth about their imams — that those imams are not to be trusted with zakat — then those imams are not truly imams, and indeed they are traitors. And if they are traitors, it is impermissible to pray behind them, to conduct jihad under them, to perform Hajj with them, or to accept their rulings on matters of life, blood, and wealth. But if the people are lying in what they say and claim, and their deeds and the fatwas they issue on this basis are false, then those who bear witness to the Imamate of such individuals are also liars — and the testimony of liars is inadmissible.

Allah Most High commanded the Prophet to collect zakat from them and to pray for them, saying: "Take from their wealth a charity that purifies them and cleanses them, and pray for them, for your prayer is

<!-- page 206 -->
a comfort and a dwelling place for them" — making it a dwelling place and a prayer for them in return for what was taken from them in zakat. If this was established for those people who lived in the era of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) as a substitute for zakat, and it was not extended to those who came after them — even though those later people are in greater need of it than those who lived in the Prophet's time — then those who came after that era and Allah Most High are both exonerated from injustice toward the servants. Allah Most High would never command an obligation and assign a substitute for it in this world and the Hereafter without that substitute being universal. Rather, the call to prayer and its comfort remain for the one who comes to the Imam (peace be upon him) and delivers the zakat of his wealth to him, outwardly and inwardly. The Imam should then take the zakat from him and pray for him. Even if the Imam had answered for nothing else, this alone would have been

<!-- page 207 -->
sufficient. He then distributes the charity to those entitled to it, for he knows who falls into each category of recipients. Whoever's share is spent, the Imam is the one who safeguards the wealth of the Muslims and disburses it, while holding both time and religion in trust as his obligation.

[62] We say: Jihad is one of the foundations of religion, and it is not permissible except under the banner of the Imam. The command of jihad can only be carried out by the Imam, for the Imam is responsible first of all for maintaining a secure abode for the Muslims, preserving the frontier posts, guarding weapons, and freeing Muslim captives from the hands of the disbelievers. He reminds the people that it is impermissible for any group to set out without a commander appointed by the Imam or designated by someone the Imam has established. The Imam also determines the times of reconciliation and truce, and it is he who arranges these matters. When captives are taken, he knows who may be released through pardon, ransomed, or put to death, and he understands what is best in levying tribute and managing wealth.


<!-- page 208 -->
So that the Muslims might enjoy comfort and prosperity. The Imam will recognize the rebel, and through him the category becomes clear — the right is with him. The aggressor is the one who breaks obedience to the Imam, and the Imam may fight them, make war, or conclude peace and truce. Al-Shafi'i said: "Were it not for the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, we would never have known the rulings governing the fighting of rebels, and that fighting them is lawful."

[63] As for the khums that Allah Almighty has reserved for the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, and for the Imams — in place of what Allah has forbidden them of charity — all acts of charity, zakat, and their related obligations are referred to the Imam. The observance of the month of Ramadan likewise falls to the Imam, who preserves it and accepts the testimony of witnesses regarding it, as has been said: fasting begins by sighting and ends by sighting, or by the Imam, peace be upon him. The two Eid prayers and the Friday prayer are not permissible in congregation except with a sermon, and they are contingent upon the Imam

<!-- page 209 -->
and his presence, or upon whoever stands in his place. The four prayer-leaders in the congregation responded by delivering the sermon on behalf of the Imam.

[64] As for the establishment of the hudud, these prescribed penalties may only be carried out by the Imam or by one whom the Imam has appointed — penalties such as amputation, flogging, stoning, imprisonment, and the punishment of surgeries. No ruling on these matters may be issued except through the Imam. Even if this were not itself a proof of the Imamate, there is still the principle that all scholars agree no one without proper guardianship may issue rulings on the hudud. If a man witnesses another killing someone unjustly, he has no right to kill the killer himself; if he witnesses a theft, he has no right to amputate the thief's hand, to stone him, or to carry out any other penalty — even if many people hold the same view. Some scholars say that an owner does not have the right to impose a prescribed penalty on his own slave, but may only discipline him. It is the Imam who disciplines and imposes the hudud. It is not possible to simply suspend the prescribed penalties, for suspending them leads to the destruction

<!-- page 210 -->
of people and the ruin of the land. If people were permitted to carry out the hudud on their own authority, everyone would be killing and flogging one another, each claiming that the person he killed had killed someone else, or that the person he robbed had stolen money and kept it. The prescribed penalty would then become obligatory against that person twenty times over each day — so how could those penalties ever be applied? How could it be permissible to impose them? If that were allowed, then every conqueror or rebel could do the same, and this would lead to the utter destruction of society. As for a rebellious group that carries out the hudud and thereby takes lives, if the Imam later issues a ruling against them they are held liable for the souls they destroyed — from the loyal community of truth — so that it may serve as a deterrent against oppressors reaching out to destroy people

<!-- page 211 -->
and their wealth. They then claim that what they did was justified and was directed against those who opposed them. Allah Almighty addressed this when He said: "Do you command people to righteousness and forget yourselves?" — thus warning that whoever needs to be enjoined to do good and forbid what is evil is not himself qualified to enjoin righteousness and forbid evil. In another verse He made clear that the command to virtue and the forbidding of evil belongs to the Imams, saying: "You were the best nation brought forth for mankind, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong." These are the middle Imams who stand as witnesses over the people. How can one enjoin good while not doing it himself? How can one forbid evil to someone who loves it, when he himself commits it a hundred times each day? How can it be permissible for their imams to do this, when they commit what they are supposed to forbid every single day, following their own lusts and desires,

<!-- page 212 -->
drawing close to themselves those who love and practice the same vices, and joining with them in those acts? How can they denounce what they themselves indulge in with ease, when anyone who refuses to join them in drinking and immorality is kept at a distance from them? Is this conduct nothing more than a directive to the believers, urging them toward corruption and depravity in order to win their approval?

[65] We say: Judicial rulings over people concerning blood, marriage, and property are — by consensus — permissible only for one who is thoroughly versed in legal precedents and fatwas, in rulings and revelations, in the meanings of texts, in abrogating and abrogated verses, and in analogical reasoning, and who is safe from error in matters of blood, property, and marriage. Is any of those who have claimed the Imamate — among the usurpers — such a person? Not one of them was. They all depended on men called jurists and attributed with knowledge, though without genuine merit, rewarding them with the scraps of this world, granting them appointments, and seating them in their councils.

<!-- page 213 -->
Whenever a case arose they would ask: what is your ruling? One would answer according to Abu Hanifa: the man must be killed. Another would say, according to al-Shafi'i: it is not obligatory. A third would say, according to Malik: his wife is thereby divorced. And yet another would say, according to Ahmad ibn Hanbal: such-and-such is forbidden to his wives. They handed the religious leadership to these scholars, allowing them to rule according to their own preferences and personal opinions, and then left the affairs of governance to those same scholars and bore false witness on their behalf — until the contradictions were exposed and laid bare. I do not know whom to wonder at more: those who claim the Imamate while knowing nothing of religion or its rulings, to the point that they must rely on such scholars? If these scholars are the real authorities and governance follows whatever they say, then the Imams are not the ones who make these claims; the claimant is merely a figurehead assigned a title, one who neither benefits nor harms. What is truly astonishing is that those who follow such a person's imamate find

<!-- page 214 -->
their own ignorance and shame to be sufficient — for the Imam resembles the Imam, and the Imam is just like the Imam.

[66] If their imams are not among the people qualified for governance and guardianship — because they do not know the religious sciences, do not meet its conditions, and possess neither uprightness nor capacity — then at most, those who hold the position of election say: if an Imam becomes immoral and corrupt, he is to be removed from the Imamate and dismissed. According to some of them, the same applies to a judge who commits a breach of trust, so that a guardian loses his guardianship. Some of them say the community must dismiss the Imam if he becomes immoral and corrupt. Al-Shafi'i said: an immoral person has no guardianship — even an immoral man has no guardianship over his own daughter and no right to give her in marriage; rather, the judge marries her. He has no guardianship over his daughter, and the testimony of an immoral person is not accepted — and this is the position of all. By their own agreement and consensus, their imams were not people of ijtihad in religion, nor

<!-- page 215 -->
among those permitted to give their daughters in marriage, nor among those whose testimony is accepted, nor among those who may establish the hudud — for they were known and notorious as adulterers, fornicators, and drinkers, and those who remained followed their same way of life. How, then, can men of this description be the Commander of the Faithful, the Caliph of the Lord of the Worlds, and the Imam whom obedience is owed? If they themselves lack guardianship, jurisdiction, and the right to testify, how can those who invest them with governance have any authority to do so? How can those who vouch for them offer valid testimony? How is their testimony permissible when they themselves affirm: "So-and-so is fit for the judiciary, for testimony, and for guardianship?" There is nothing more astonishing in the world than these contradictions in their rulings, their doctrinal foundations,

<!-- page 216 -->
their pronouncements, and the ways in which they expose their own Imam and his imamate.

[67] Were we to enumerate all the contradictions in their foundations and all their scandals, we would far exceed the intended scope of this work. What we have mentioned is sufficient to make clear that the imamate they claim is void — and it is evident that all they have done and continue to do is false, unjust, and hostile. That alone is sufficient as their shame and disgrace. Now that their imamate has been invalidated, as we have shown, and it is established that the Imamate is one of the foundations of religion — an obligation and a necessity — and that all matters of Sharia and its rulings are entrusted to the Imam to

<!-- page 217 -->
carry out, it follows that the true Imamate belongs to the pure Imams from the family of Taha, who are infallible from immorality and tyranny, free from aggression, and purified from all impurity and defect. What we have mentioned from the Sharia's evidence is sufficient to establish this. We now recall from preceding discussions the obligation of the Imamate and the fact that no age or era has ever been, or ever can be, without a true Imam to whom obedience is owed.

A Chapter

[68] We say: if it were permissible for creatures to exist on earth without an Imam or caliph, it would have been permissible at the time of Adam, peace be upon him, when the angels were present. Yet Allah the Exalted said: "I will place a caliph in the earth." The angels said: will You place in it one who will spread corruption and shed blood, while we glorify Your praise and sanctify You? He said: I know what you do not know. Allah Almighty thus made clear that the earth is never devoid of

<!-- page 218 -->
an Imam — even when the people are infallible and holy — so that it may be known how much more they stand in need of one when they are ignorant and most of them are corrupt. How then can they do without an Imam? Allah Almighty also showed that had the Imamate been a matter of election, the choice would have fallen to the angels and not been refused, for they were infallible from inclination toward whims, injustice, and worldly desire. Since it was not permissible for them to go without an Imam, it is not permissible for anyone besides them either. This meaning is implicit in the verse, for when Allah Almighty said concerning parents: "Do not say to them even 'uff'," He thereby forbade beating and cursing them, even though He mentioned only the lesser thing. In the same way, Allah Almighty cited the stories of earlier nations and prophets so that it might be known that what held true in the era of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, likewise held true in all other eras — just as the Prophet said: "There is nothing in my community that was not also in the Children of Israel," and: "You will follow the path of those before you, as precisely as one sandal matches the other."


<!-- page 218 -->
In the story of Adam, there is evidence that the Imamate and the Caliphate cannot be established by popular choice or by consultation. Had such a method been valid, the angels would have had the stronger claim, since their counsel carries greater weight — they were free from envy and corruption. There is also evidence in this story that it is not the people's prerogative to declare that such-and-such a person is fit for the Imamate or not, based on whom they favor and whom they do not. The angels did not favor Adam, and so God Almighty addressed their error, rebuked them, refuted their position, and recorded the entire episode in the Quran — so that people would reflect on it and not presume to speak about the Imamate and about whom God and His Messenger have chosen, as the angels presumed to do. Furthermore, the establishment of the Imamate through Adam's progeny is proof that the world cannot be without an Imam. Yet all people accept the Imams of the age of Adam while rejecting the Imams of the age of the Prophet Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him.

<!-- page 219 -->
"We sent before you from Our Messengers, and you will find no alteration in Our way." He said: You will find no change in the way of God — that is, the Sunnah of the Imamate, not the Sunnah of acts of worship such as prayer, fasting, and the like. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not follow the same Sunnah as all the prophets in matters of worship, yet the Sunnah of the Imamate was never changed. The people were never without an Imam.

[70] We say: When Noah, peace be upon him, called the people to board the ark, he showed compassion for them. He recognized that those who stayed behind would be destroyed — that their own choice and judgment led them to believe their salvation lay elsewhere, when in fact it led to their drowning. This illustrates that when a people's prophet and Imam calls them to a command and they refuse, their destruction follows from that refusal. The ark was nothing other than a symbol of the Imam and of his call to the people to follow the Imam — just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: "My family among you is like

<!-- page 220 -->
Noah's Ark — whoever boards it is saved, and whoever stays behind is drowned." Noah then appointed his son Shem to succeed him in the guardianship and the Imamate, and after him the line continued, each designated by the one before, until it reached Ibrahim, peace be upon him. Yet people accept the Imams of Noah's age while rejecting the Imams of the family of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. The Sunnah has not changed, and people have never been able to do without an Imam.

[71] Then, when Ibrahim, peace be upon him, rose up and the fire was ordered to be cooled for him, this — if the interpretation is sound — is a reference to the elevation of the light of the Imamate, as we have discussed above. When Ibrahim and Ishmael, peace be upon them, built the House and commanded the people to venerate it, this pointed to the fact that the Imamate belongs to his household and his descendants, and was an exhortation for people to honor the Imamate and to venerate the Ahl al-Bayt and obey them.

[72] When Moses, peace be upon him, arose, he commanded the people to go toward Bayt al-Maqdis. Moses had no children and no house in which the Imamate would reside, so he directed them toward a house

<!-- page 221 -->
and said: Whoever among the children of Israel is upheld by the Holy Spirit — he is your Imam and your Qibla. And when Jesus, peace be upon him, arose — having neither a house nor a son — he commanded the people, according to what is narrated of him, to go toward the east. If this is accurately reported, then his command to face east was a command to face the place from which the light of the Imamate would rise — that is, whoever radiates that light is your Imam and your Qibla.

When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, arose as the Seal of the Prophets, he commanded the people to turn their faces from the Holy House toward the Kaaba, making the Kaaba the Qibla of the Muslims. The Kaaba is the house that Ibrahim, peace be upon him, and Ishmael built — a sign that the Qibla had returned to the house of Ibrahim, which is the house of Ishmael, meaning that the Imamate had returned to the children of Ishmael, who are the children of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his family. He commanded the people to venerate the House and to make pilgrimage to it, as no prophet before him had commanded.

<!-- page 222 -->
The prophets thus gave warning of the preeminence of the Imams of the family of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, over all other Imams in the ages that came before. Is there anything more unjust than this situation? The Ummah acknowledges the Imams of each prophetic age and affirms that the world has never been without an Imam — that in every past age there was an Imam appointed by designation rather than by popular choice — yet they deny the Imamate of the family of Muhammad, peace be upon him, reject the explicit designation, and claim that the matter is decided by election?

God Almighty said: "Your Lord creates what He wills and chooses — the choice is not theirs." If the Imamate were determined by popular choice, then every people would simply choose whomever they preferred. The scholars have mentioned the hadith on designation versus election, and we have undertaken to include here what the scholars do not mention in the standard books proving the Imamate. What we have set out is sufficient to establish that the Imam must be the most excellent, the most knowledgeable, and the most honorable.

[73] We say: The virtues that God Almighty has mentioned and by which He has distinguished some people above others fall into three categories: knowledge, jihad, and piety. As He said:

<!-- page 223 -->
God Almighty said: "God has conferred on those who strive a great reward over those who remain behind." He said: "Are those who know equal to those who do not know?" And He said: "God will raise those who have been given knowledge by degrees." And He said: "The most honored of you in the sight of God is the most pious of you." No one can claim that Abu Bakr was the most knowledgeable of the people — he knew only thirteen surahs of the Quran, and if he did not even know the Quran itself, how could he know its interpretation, its meanings, its rulings, its abrogating and abrogated verses, and all that is similar? Can anyone cite a single instance of wisdom, knowledge, or legal ruling narrated from him — or from Umar, for that matter? Whoever makes such a claim, let him bring his proof.

As for jihad — both in its personal and its broader dimensions — it is well known that they never fought with their own hands. The military record of the Commander of the Faithful is well known to all. Even setting aside all other examples in jihad, they fled on the day of Hunayn and abandoned the Prophet's command, peace and blessings be upon him,

<!-- page 224 -->
to hold the position and hold the high ground — an act that brought disgrace upon the Muslims — while the Prophet, peace be upon him, and the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, peace be upon him, continued fighting the enemy until the enemy was routed.

As for piety — what piety could they have, when they usurped the right of the Commander of the Faithful and the right of the Ahl al-Bayt? Moreover, there is no piety without knowledge, for piety consists in knowing what one owes and what belongs to others. It is reported about Muhammad ibn al-Hasan that Abu Hanifah said that some of those who called others to account asked him to write a book on asceticism. He was informed of this and replied: Whoever is not well-versed in the jurisprudence of buying and selling and the rules of transactions — how can he eat only what is lawful? And whoever does not eat only what is lawful has no true asceticism.

Given what we have cited from the Quranic verses, it is clear that the Imamate belonged to the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, for he was foremost in all virtues, the most knowledgeable of the people, and the most engaged in jihad.

[74] We say: They did not appeal to virtue, knowledge, or piety

<!-- page 225 -->
for themselves — as their own supporters acknowledge — since Abu Bakr was the first to ascend the pulpit and say: "I have been placed over you, though I am not the best among you." If he was truthful in what he said, then those who claimed he was the best are liars. And if he was lying in his statement, then a liar cannot be an Imam. If they say this was an expression of humility, we reply: humility has its proper place. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to remind people of the distinction and rank that God Almighty had singled out for him, without disparaging himself and without falling into arrogance. He said: "I am the master of the Arabs — I say this without pride. I am the master of the sons of Adam — I say this without pride. And I am the most eloquent of the Arabs." The Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, said: "Ask me before you lose me." And he said: "If I were given a cushion to sit upon in judgment, I would rule for the people of the Torah by the Torah, for the people of the Gospel by the Gospel, and for the people of the Criterion by the Criterion." This kind of declaration is not mere boasting — it was not said in the past nor has it been abandoned.

<!-- page 226 -->
As for humility — the words of Abu Bakr, "I am not the best among you," in fact constitute evidence against him, since he attributed nothing to God or His Messenger, nor did he refer to any guidance in the matter of prayer or anything else.

Abu Bakr also said: "I have a devil that comes over me — so if I do well, obey me, and if I do wrong, correct me." This man testified against himself in his opening statement that he was afflicted, because one who is possessed by a devil is not of sound mind. It has also been noted that such possession leads to grave sins, and so people would be obliged to restrain him and remove him from authority. A person who is mentally unsound or possessed by a devil cannot be an Imam — by universal agreement — because he commands the believers to do what the devil dictates. He himself admitted that he would commit offenses, and offense encompasses sin in all its forms, including apostasy, adultery, and murder. The devil commands all of this and makes it appear good in one's own eyes.


<!-- page 227 -->

As Allah Almighty said: "Satan promises you poverty and commands you to commit immorality." He declared that all of this was to be expected of such a person, and he said: "Rise against me, for the calendar requires it in great matters and small — the small ones fall away by seeking forgiveness." He declared that he had been raised above creation so that they should obey him, not disobey him. He testified that he was the companion of the devil, as Allah (exalted be He) said: "And whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Most Merciful — We appoint for him a devil, and he becomes his companion." The Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, represents the remembrance of the Most Merciful, and whoever turns away from him has a devil enveloping him as a companion. He testified against himself that he needed people to correct him, and that he rises up claiming to be the Imam while standing beneath one who surpasses him. He testifies against himself that he is crooked, and the crooked one does not

1 Surah 2, Al-Baqarah, 268.

<!-- page 228 -->

serve as an imam. When one is crooked and distorted, the devil commands the people through that distortion — commanding what amounts to obedience to the devil and disobedience to the Most Merciful, and perhaps even commanding outright disbelief and apostasy. Such a person commands people to do what he has chosen for himself, and the devil is the enemy of Allah. Whoever is the companion of the enemy of Allah is himself the enemy of Allah. Is there anything in the world more astonishing than this?

Consider: if a man stood up in the marketplace and announced that he himself was prone to wrongdoing, people would rise against him and forbid him if he tried to ruin their garments, burn them, or contaminate them — they would stand against him and stop him, because they had already been paid to guard those garments from the very harm he might do. And if he then claimed to be a just witness before a judge, yet was known to distort his testimony, the judge would dismiss him and refuse his testimony thereafter

1 Surah 43, Al-Zukhruf, 36.

<!-- page 229 -->

over a matter worth as little as ten dirhams. Yet people invoke his Imamate and accept it, even though the arguments against him are many and overwhelming.

[75] As for 'Umar, he was the first to mount the pulpit after Abu Bakr, and he declared: "The pledge of allegiance to Abu Bakr was a hasty affair — and whoever returns to anything like it, kill him." This statement indicates that what the Ahl al-Zahir claimed — that Abu Bakr's leadership was legitimately established and that the Imamate was made obligatory through it — is invalid. In fact, 'Umar's words contain a refutation of those who invoke the precedent of Abu Bakr and 'Umar. Everything narrated in support of the imamates of Abu Bakr, 'Umar, and 'Uthman is fabricated and forged after the fact, because 'Umar himself said: "The pledge of allegiance to Abu Bakr was a hasty, unplanned affair." The hasty act is precisely one that proceeds without deliberation, planning, or forethought. Everything they narrate

<!-- page 230 -->

in its support is therefore false by the very admission of 'Umar — and his statement is the most truthful thing they have to offer. He himself testified that it was evil, not good, and that an act which is evil has no place in the religion. The Imamate built upon it is therefore void. As for his claim that "Allah protected its evil" — Allah did not protect it. The evil of that affair is nothing but misguidance, dissension, heresy, injustice, cruelty, unlawful killing, usurpation, the seizure of wealth, the permitting of what was forbidden in bloodshed and violation, and the alteration of the religion from the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family, until the Day of Resurrection — all of this flows from the evil of that pledge of allegiance, and its burden rests upon those who initiated it. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: "Whoever establishes a good practice will have its reward and the reward of all who act upon it until the Day of Resurrection; and whoever establishes a bad practice will bear its burden and the burden of all who act upon it until the Day of Resurrection." Every innovation is misguidance, and every misguidance leads to the Fire. The pledge of allegiance that established that Imamate was an innovation — a bid'ah — and not a Sunnah.

<!-- page 231 -->

This testifies that Abu Bakr's ascent to power was not founded on any virtue, lineage, knowledge, jihad, or piety. His pledge of allegiance was a hasty and reckless act, an evil — and 'Umar affirms it as such. Those who carried it out were wrongdoers, and wrongdoers are the people of the Fire. 'Umar said that whoever returns to such a thing should be killed, and every action is judged by the same standard in the first instance as in the second. If killing is required in the second case, then the first case demands the same reckoning. And if a capital offense is established by the second act, it is established by the first no less. 'Umar said what he said only because he feared that, if they permitted such a vice and overturned the order of the Imamate, they would be seen to have repudiated the deeds of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, the acts of Allah Almighty, the Quran, the transmitted reports, and the established virtue of the Commander of the Faithful,

<!-- page 232 -->

'Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him — and to have openly rejected the Prophet's declaration at Ghadir Khumm and all the other narrations. Their predecessors and builders had no real foundation, no argument, and no proof — nothing but the pledge of allegiance of some by choice and of others under compulsion, leaving the door open for anyone else to claim a pledge of allegiance from other people, rise up, and claim the Imamate. They had no argument in hand to prevent such a claim, since they themselves had no prior and more deserving right to it. 'Umar sought to close off that avenue, but Allah Almighty caused the truth to emerge on his own tongue, exposing their errors, hypocrisy, and falsehoods.

[76] As for 'Umar's statement to women when he addressed them and said, "Do not exaggerate in giving dowries to women" — a woman stood up and refuted him, and said to those around her:

<!-- page 233 -->

"People, I know better than 'Umar. You hear this and do not rebuke him until a woman from among the last of the people corrects him?" The people recognized this as proof that he did not know the Quran, nor the rulings, nor the Sunnah, and that he had failed to retain even the most basic lessons from the days of the Prophet, peace be upon him and his family — let alone the rulings that came after. He had committed an error of this kind. Had a boy been asked this, he would not have formed such an opinion. That he had no reply to this rebuke is itself testimony against him before Allah and His Messenger — proof that all people knew better than 'Umar, and were more equitable than he was. Yet the greater scandal lies with those who witnessed and heard such disgraceful episodes and then obeyed him — just as Allah says: "So he incited his people to treat him as foolish, and they obeyed him."

1 Surah 43, Al-Zukhruf, 54.

<!-- page 234 -->

If 'Umar was truthful in what he said, then all people knew better than him. They cannot excuse this as humility — it was rather a confession of inadequacy and helplessness, and the circumstances and judgments testify to the truth of what he said, even as he remained blind to its implications. His statement that "there was a time when I did not need to give a reply" shows that on other occasions he simply had none. And his saying, "Were it not for 'Ali, 'Umar would have perished," is proof that he brought about the ruin of religion — even if his body did not perish — and amounts to an admission that he knew nothing of the rulings and issued no judgment without the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, rescuing him from confusion and public exposure.

[77] When he made the matter of succession a consultative council among six men, he said: "If you succeed, follow the example of one who is better than me" — meaning Abu Bakr — "and if you do not succeed, follow the example of one who is better than me" — meaning the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

<!-- page 235 -->

He thereby testified that the Prophet had not appointed Abu Bakr as his successor, had not entrusted the Imamate to anyone from among the creation, and that Abu Bakr's appointment of him to lead the prayers was not a designation of the Imamate. He likewise testified that Abu Bakr was not the true caliph of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. If that is indeed the case — as he himself declared — then why did Abu Bakr leave the matter as he found it, without appointing a caliph? If the obligation was for the people to gather and choose whom they wished and give their pledge, and if he had not been charged with any decision over them, then why did 'Umar not leave it the same way, instead of settling it through an innovation? And if the binding obligation was what Abu Bakr had done, then why did 'Umar depart from it by making the matter a consultation among six? His actions — the administrative registers, the stipends, and the other measures recorded at length — proceed from a position of presumption and invention.

[78] Then 'Uthman committed what he committed of betrayals against the property of the Muslims:

<!-- page 236 -->

he expelled Abu Dharr from Medina, beat Ibn Mas'ud, and empowered Marwan ibn al-Hakam, dispatching him as he pleased. He disregarded the counsel of Muhammad ibn Abi Bakr until the Muhajireen and Ansar rose against him, while the rest of the Muhajireen and Ansar looked on and considered him deserving of what befell him — yet they did not intervene to help him. When he was killed, they left him unburied for three days, angered by his deeds and conduct, and they refused to allow him to be buried in the Prophet's mosque or in the Muslim cemeteries. Then his supporters demanded blood and made war against the Commander of the Faithful. Were we to enumerate all their scandals, the arguments against them, and the refutations of their claims and conduct, the book would extend greatly — and what we have mentioned is sufficient for anyone who seeks justice and does not willfully deny the evident truth.

[79] We now turn to mention some of the virtues of the Guardian of the Age and Time, the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him — though a person of my standing is not capable of doing justice to his virtues in full, as Allah Almighty said: "Allah has not decreed the truth


<!-- page 237 -->
In proportion to my understanding of him. But I speak according to the extent of my own knowledge and capacity, and the Commander of the Faithful is the first of those who accept the excuse of their servants. Just as Allah Almighty, despite His abundant blessings, did not require His servants to give thanks proportionate to those blessings — for they were incapable of such gratitude — He forgave them and limited them to saying: "Praise be to Allah, the Lord of the two worlds," as a discipline for His creation. For even if the Blessed One desired them to give thanks, they would be unable to do so. They come according to their capacity and are not held accountable in the first place for their inability to perform what lies beyond their means.

We say: Allah has illumined the earth with His light, as He promised and declared: "And the earth shone with the light of its Lord." And the inner earth shone with knowledge and exposition.

<!-- page 238 -->
[80] We say: In every kind, genus, root, branch, and law, there is a goal and a summit in honor, virtue, rank, and station that no one else has reached. In addition, it is necessary for the Imams to have a goal and a summit — and the end of all who have proceeded to that degree. The one who has reached that end is the Commander of the Faithful; he is the end of all ends, beyond which no further end or limit exists. Just as with the rise of human beings the wisdom of the world was revealed, and animals and plants were reconciled through humans, so that the benefit of all returned to mankind — likewise, through the Imams, peace be upon them, the virtues of human beings were fulfilled, wisdom appeared in their creation, and the benefit of all human beings returned to the Imams. Similarly, through the Commander of the Faithful, the affairs of

<!-- page 239 -->
the prophets, the guardians, and the Imams were brought to completion, their affairs were set in order, and all wisdom appeared in their subjects, their laws, and their interpretations. Allah's peace be upon him is like the head, and they are like the body; and the Commander of the Faithful is like the spirit, for the body is only made complete by the spirit. The Commander of the Faithful is like the heart, and all who have gone before are like the limbs. In the union of soul and life, the heart sustains all the limbs with life and reward, and every nail, hair, and limb receives its share of reward through the heart — which is knowledge of the esoteric, the reward of the work — obtained from the soul in its place of development across the six states from generation to the creation of the next.

<!-- page 240 -->
Likewise, through the Commander of the Faithful they are informed of the reward and receive life all at once. The seventh — peace of God be upon him — is that which each one wished to be, and that which Allah has promised creation: to fulfil the reward for it. One longs for one's wages on the Day of Reckoning, and whoever escapes the Fire and enters Paradise has truly attained. He is the one whom all creation was commanded to believe in and in his day, as He said: "They believe in Allah and the Last Day."

[81] Just as the distinction of all human beings lay in the fact that the last one born into the world possessed, before all others, what all the other newborns possessed — and added to it things that none of the others had —

<!-- page 241 -->
each receiving their share, and just as the Imams encompassed all the virtues that human beings had, standing as humanity's goal, summit, and excellence, able to receive what was beyond the capacity of ordinary men, with the benefit of all who came before returning to them — so too the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, gathered the virtues of those who preceded him and drew their benefit and knowledge back to himself, and added to that many times over. The Prophet's strength and virtue lay in his gathering the virtues of those who had gone before, across their ranks and beneath all their banners, and surpassing them. Likewise, the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, gathered the virtues of all who had passed in the roles of the prophets, the faithful, and the wise, and added to that things they had been unable to achieve. And had he not risen in this station, he would not have stood in their station, peace be upon them.

<!-- page 242 -->
Perfect wisdom — the harvest of all worlds, both spiritual and physical — belongs to the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, and for him all things were created; everyone was his worker and herald, pointing toward him and toward his day. He is the one who fulfilled the caliphate in the world for the Creator of all things in His creation.

Rather, we say: he is the end of all who have passed. Should someone object that this is the attribute of the last Qa'im, and that the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, is not the last, we say: the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, is the Commander of the Faithful in his own time. He is the one who came before and the one who was designated, and he is the sixth who embodies the perfection of command. The seventh empties what the seventh needs, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, supplied all that the ruler needs, completing in what he established the rank of the Prophet, peace be upon him — which is the ninth by

<!-- page 243 -->
another reckoning. The order of the seventh, which has passed, is fulfilled in the ninth, just as the seven organs, their structure and their function, appear in the nine: four natures and five senses. The perfection of creation is accomplished in nine months, and reckoning is set upon nine. The creature of the Abode of Permanence has four spiritual natures and five spiritual senses, as Allah Almighty said: "And the abode of the Hereafter belongs to the living, if they only knew."

[82] The Commander of the Faithful is the second by another reckoning, and he is the wisest of the two at the beginning of each cycle. He is the sixth through whom Allah opened the entire world with his sword, just as He opened through the Prophet, peace be upon him, the sixth of Adam, peace be upon him. He established in the world the perfect caliphate for the Creator of all in His creation, dealing with creation in all its conditions as Allah Almighty deals with His creation — in their sustaining, their provision, and the abundance of His mercy upon them. Everyone who rose before him fulfilled only a part, while the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, rose to fulfil all things. The caliph is the one who raises up all that those before him accomplished.

<!-- page 244 -->
